Rare transitions between collective states in an active fluid via a weakly nonlinear reduction
Yves-Marie Ducimetière \aff 1, Michael John Shelley \aff 1,2

Abstract
We study a model for a dilute suspension of rod-like particles swimming at constant velocity in a Stokes flow. As the translational diffusivity of the particles decreases, a two-dimensional uniform concentration of randomly aligned particles undergoes either a codimension- 2 2 pitchfork bifurcation or a codimension- 4 4 Hopf bifurcation, depending on the particles’ swimming speed. We use a weakly nonlinear expansion to reduce the system to a low-dimensional one for the amplitudes of the bifurcating eigenmodes. The originality of our calculations lies in incorporating spatio-temporal white noise forcing. The stochastic forcing terms in the amplitude equations are derived analytically, from the noise acting on the original system, and via a generalized non-resonance condition in the variance. Past the onset of the bifurcations, the particles deterministically self-organize into steady or oscillating states of collective motion. For the Hopf bifurcation scenario, two stable periodic orbits are found to coexist, each corresponding to a distinct collective dynamics. The stochastic forcing induces rare transitions between them. Owing to the low dimensionality of amplitude equations, steady and dynamical statistics can be computed directly from the Fokker-Planck equation, or via the Adaptive Multilevel Splitting (AMS) rare-event algorithm. In particular, extremely long mean transition times and associated out-of-equilibrium paths between the metastable periodic orbits are obtained. These paths can be understood in light of the invariant manifolds of the low-dimensional system, which brings insights into the mechanism behind the transitions. We also performed fully nonlinear stochastic simulations and used the AMS algorithm directly on the full system. The statistics are in good quantitative agreement with those computed on the reduced systems, the latter being obtained at a considerably lower numerical cost.



\lefttitle

Y.-M. Ducimetière and M.J. Shelley

\righttitle
A weakly nonlinear analysis of a stochastically forced active fluid model

\corresau
Yves-Marie Ducimetière,

keywords

…

1
Introduction

Active matter systems consist of collections of discrete agents, for example, particles or micro-swimmers, each of which can convert a source of energy (usually chemical) into mechanical work. Examples include experimental works on fish schools
(
38
)
and bird flocks
(
3
)
, ensembles of biopolymers cross-linked by motor proteins
(
67
;
62
)
, and both experimental and theoretical works on colloidal suspensions of self-propelled spherical particles
(
10
;
13
;
30
)
and suspensions of motile bacteria
(
20
;
70
;
66
)
.
Agents that constitute an active matter system typically interact on their own individual scale. This can occur either directly (e.g., dipolar magnetic interactions, excluded volume, etc.) or indirectly through the medium in which they move. These interactions, occurring at the individual scale of the agents, can sometimes translate into collective dynamics at much larger scales, as reported in
68
;
47
;
14
;
79
;
78
, among many others. Active matter systems are thus known to exhibit an extremely rich phenomenology, spanning many length and time scales. In the context of swimming bacteria, this includes chaos
(
49
;
20
;
70
;
16
;
23
)
, turbulence
(
23
)
, and complex pattern formation
(
64
;
51
)
.

The system of present interest is a coarse-grained version of the Doi-Saintillan-Shelley (DSS) model for a dilute suspension of active, rod-like particles
(
59
;
60
)
. The DSS model consists of a high-dimensional conservation equation for particle number, coupled with the Stokes equations for the surrounding fluid velocity. In the dilute limit (considered here), the particles interact with each other only hydrodynamically through an active stress within the fluid. Here, we remove the orientational dependency of the model by evolving only the first three moments of the density function with respect to the orientational coordinates. This results in a “coarse-grained” model, depending only on space and time. However, such moment expansions are notorious for leading to a closure problem, since the equations for the lower moments require higher moments. Consequently, we adopt the generalized polar Bingham closure to express all the higher moments in terms of the first three, which was shown in
73
to preserve the thermodynamics structure of the system.

For some fixed set of parameters, the DSS model was reported in
25
and
51
to exhibit multiple, distinct stable states of collective particle motion (see figures 8(c) and 10(a) therein, respectively). As we shall demonstrate, this is also true for the coarse-grained version studied here. This coexistence of distinct stable states, for a fixed set of parameters, is referred to as “multistability”. It is the central motivation behind the present analysis. In the presence of noise, multistability implies that the system can exist in a specific state of collective motion for an extremely long time, but, from time to time, due to a rare fluctuation, it exits the basin of attraction of that state and transitions to another, e.g.
33
. Noise-induced transitions typically are increasingly rare as the intensity of the fluctuations vanishes.

Characterizing the transition to collective behavior in an active matter system is of both historical
(
40
)
, conceptual, and possibly practical value. In many situations, bacterial contamination manifests as biofilms, typically in domestic water systems
(
17
)
or coronary implants
(
26
)
. Biofilm growth is triggered when the local bacterial population density exceeds a certain threshold. Thus, it would be useful to elucidate how a uniform density evolves into a non-uniform one. More generally, a proper modeling of the transition to collective motion could help control active matter systems, with applications in liquid crystal displays.

In the context of equilibrium statistical mechanics, steady states of a system minimize a potential. Therefore, in simple situations, formulas for the transition rates can be derived analytically. Consider, for example, a bi-stable overdamped system driven by a stochastic noise
\epsilon\xi(t)
with intensity
\epsilon

\mathrm{d}_{t}x=-\mathrm{d}_{x}V(x)+\epsilon\xi(t),

with
V
a double-well potential with a potential barrier
\Delta V
. The Arrhenius law predicts the expected time
T
between two transitions to go like the exponential of minus the potential barrier separating two attractors, divided by the square of the intensity of the fluctuations, i.e.,
T\propto\exp(-2\Delta V/\epsilon^{2})
.

However, active matter systems are out of equilibrium, with energy constantly injected and dissipated at the individual scale. Due to their out-of-equilibrium nature, no potential exists, which complicates the computation of transition rates and trajectories. For instance, the absence of potential implies that the forward and backward routes from one basin of attraction to another are generically not the time-reversals of one another.

Another fundamental difficulty is that descriptions of active matter systems of interest usually involve a considerably large number of degrees of freedom. This applies to the coarse-grained model considered here. This means that direct numerical simulation is too costly a tool for the statistical study of extremely rare transition events.

Overall, the out-of-equilibrium nature of active matter systems and the large number of degrees of freedom involved make the statistical characterization of rare transitions a current scientific challenge. Some advances have been made based on the Freidlin-Wentzell’s large deviation theory, valid for out-of-equilibrium systems subject to infinitesimal noise
(
27
)
. Their theory says that the most probable trajectory selected by the system for a rare transition from one basin to another, called an “instanton path”, can be computed
a priori
, as it minimizes a certain action functional in the path integral representation of the system. The transition rates are then proportional to the exponential of the minimal action (i.e., the action along the instanton) divided by the squared noise intensity. This minimal action is called a “quasipotential”, as it is analogous to a potential barrier in the equilibrium case. Recent progress in numerical methods has made it possible to compute instanton paths in different contexts, ranging from fluid mechanics
(
6
;
34
;
71
;
63
)
to reaction-diffusion equations
(
35
;
77
)
, and canonical low-dimensional dynamical systems
(
39
)
.

Another, more indirect, way to determine the instanton path is to use rare events algorithms, which aim to oversample the number of transition events and related trajectories. One such algorithm is the Adaptive Multilevel Splitting (AMS) algorithm.
(
11
;
9
;
56
)
.
In short, the AMS algorithm performs killing and cloning iterations to generate a larger number of trajectories, initiated in the basin of one attractor, and ending in the basin of another. This large number of transition paths is expected to concentrate around the instanton and reveal it. Moreover, the algorithm still applies for non-infinitesimal forcing, where the mean transition paths are sometimes found not to be the instantons
(
2
;
58
)
.

The present article aims to characterize the statistics of rare noise-induced transition events in a multistable, out-of-equilibrium, space-time-dependent coarse-grained version of the DSS model. For that purpose, and in the continuation of
21
, we propose the following approach: the dimensionality of the model, subject to a stochastic forcing, will first be reduced analytically. As in
51
, we restrict the parameter space to be asymptotically close to a pitchfork or Hopf bifurcation point. This justifies using an asymptotic expansion to approximate the solution. The originality of the present calculations with respect to those in
51
lies in the inclusion of stochastic forcing and in the treatment of the closure map inherent to coarse-graining procedures. The original model, a system of PDEs with a continuously infinite number of degrees of freedom, can thus be reduced to a system of
4
or
8
ODEs for the scalar amplitudes of the bifurcating eigenmodes.

Critically, the reduced systems obtained here are substantially easier to study and physically interpret than the original equations. In particular, they are amenable to standard tools from statistical mechanics at low numerical cost, such as the Fokker-Planck equation or the AMS algorithm. We can therefore make predictions, for instance, about the average out-of-equilibrium transition rates and associated trajectories between the different attracting states of particle collective motion. These predictions align well with the results obtained by applying the AMS algorithm directly to the full DSS model, which requires considerably more numerical effort.

We begin with a brief presentation of the DSS model and its coarse-grained version in §
2
, followed by a review of the linear stability properties of the isotropic, uniform base flow in §
3
. The method for deriving a stochastically forced amplitude equation system past the onset of the Hopf and pitchfork bifurcations is outlined in §
4
. We present numerical and algorithmic techniques in §
5
. Sections
6
and
7
are dedicated to our weakly nonlinear results, which we systematically compare to those obtained from the full DSS model. We summarize our main findings and propose further work in §
8
.

2
Problem definition

In this section, we first briefly recall the DSS model for a dilute suspension of rod-like particles swimming in a Stokes fluid
(
59
;
25
;
66
)
. We then present a coarse-grained version of this model, which is studied in this article
(
73
)
.

2.1
The DSS model

Consider a suspension of
N
swimming rod-like particles, each of length
l
and much smaller diameter
b\ll l
, so that the aspect ratio
b/l\ll 1
of each particle is small. The particles are immersed in a fluid, and the whole system has a characteristic length scale
L\gg l
(e.g., domain size) much larger than the particle length.

Let
\psi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p},t)
denote the particle density function, with
\boldsymbol{x}
the spatial coordinate,
\boldsymbol{p}
with
||\boldsymbol{p}||=1
the orientational vector of the particles, and
t
the time. The function
\psi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p},t)
should be interpreted in the sense of continuum mechanics. Specifically,
\psi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p},t)
gives the number density of particles found within an elementary element centered at
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p})
, at a time
t
. Thereby,
\psi
has the dimension of number of particles per (spatial and orientational) volume. We first assume that there are no particle sources/sinks. The ensuing conservation of the number of particles inside a material volume leads, via the transport theorem, to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\right)-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{p}\cdot\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}\psi\right).

(1)

In (
1
),
\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}
and
\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}
denote the material derivatives of
\boldsymbol{x}
and
\boldsymbol{p}
, respectively. We have also introduced the symbols
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
for the standard spatial gradient operator and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{p}\coloneq\left(\mathsfbi{I}-\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\right)(\partial_{p_{1}},...,\partial_{p_{d}})^{T}
with
d
the spatial dimension of the problem (typically
d=2
or
d=3
). The latter operator is a standard gradient along the orientational coordinates
\boldsymbol{p}=(p_{1},...,p_{d})
, in which only the component orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{p}
, i.e., tangent to the unit sphere
||\boldsymbol{p}||=1
, is kept by application of the projection matrix
\left(\mathsfbi{I}-\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\right)
. Indeed, variations normal to the unit sphere are not permitted by imposition of
||\boldsymbol{p}||=1
.

We consider a periodic spatial domain
\Omega
, where the coordinate along each of the
d
dimensions ranges from
0
and
L
. The total number of particles within
\Omega
is conserved and equal to
N
, i.e.,
\int_{\Omega}\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p},t)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=N
, for all times.

In the DSS framework, all the particles have the same swimming (i.e., intrinsic) velocity
V\boldsymbol{p}
; this is why
\psi
does not depend on velocity coordinates: because the swimming velocity of a particle can be deduced from its
\boldsymbol{p}
. The background fluid velocity is named
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
. Based on the slender body approximation, the DSS model is given by

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{u}-D_{T}\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\log\psi),\quad\text{and}

(2)

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathsfbi{I}-\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\right)(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{p}-D_{R}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{p}(\log\psi),

(3)

with
(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{p}=(\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla})\boldsymbol{u}
the matrix-vector product between
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u}
and
\boldsymbol{p}
. Equation (
2
) says the velocity of a particle is the sum of its intrinsic swimming speed
V\boldsymbol{p}
plus the advection velocity
\boldsymbol{u}
from the fluid.
In (
3
), the term

(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{p}=\lim_{l\rightarrow 0}\left(\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}+l\boldsymbol{p},t)-\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\right)/l

is the spatial derivative of the fluid velocity at
\boldsymbol{x}
, and in the direction
\boldsymbol{p}
of the particle. Thereby, for an infinitely slender and infinitesimally small rod-like particle, the component of
(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{p}
normal to
\boldsymbol{p}
, i.e.,
\left(\mathsfbi{I}-\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\right)(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{p}
, naturally gives the rotation rate
\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}
of the particle under the action of the fluid
(
36
)
. By contrast, the component of
(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{p}
along
\boldsymbol{p}
would give the stretching rate of a particle, which is prohibited by its assumed rigidity. Such resistance to stretching should translate into a stress exerted on the fluid, but it is formally second-order in concentration and thus is neglected in the model.

Eventually, in (
2
)-(
3
), the terms in
D_{T}
and
D_{R}
model random (thermal) and supposedly isotropic collisions of the fluid molecules with a particle. At a continuum level, this must translate into translational and rotational isotropic diffusion of the particle density function, respectively.

To close the system, we need an equation for the fluid velocity field
\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
. Because we consider small particles as compared to the characteristic macroscopic length scale (
L\gg l
), as well as a small velocity scale, we model the fluid by the incompressible Stokes equations

\displaystyle-\mu\Delta\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}\varpi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma},\quad\text{with}\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=a_{0}\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p},t)\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},

(4)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(5)

Above,
\mu
denotes the fluid viscosity,
\varpi(\boldsymbol{x},t)
the pressure field, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
the stress tensor exerted by the particles upon the fluid. The latter consists of the force dipole
a_{0}\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}
that a single particle oriented along
\boldsymbol{p}
exerts upon the fluid, weighted by the density function and integrated over all possible orientations. For self-propelled particles, the dipole strength
a_{0}
typically scales as
a_{0}\propto\mu Vl^{2}
, with a negative sign for pusher particles and a positive sign for puller particles. However,
a_{0}
can be nonzero even when particles do not swim: “shaker” particles, for instance, still exert the same stress on the fluid even though
V=0

(
25
;
65
)
. Note that, in (
4
), one can equivalently replace
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
by its traceless version
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{x},t)-\mathsfbi{I}/d
and absorb the removed isotropic part in the tensor in the pressure.

Overall, (
1
)-(
5
) form a closed system of equations. The particles are advected by the fluid velocity and rotated by the associated shear, and feed back onto the fluid by exerting a stress on it due to their intrinsic movement (swimming and/or shaking). These phenomena are local in space, but (
4
) is non-local in orientation.

While no modeling assumptions are made when deriving (
1
) (other than each particle having intrinsic velocity
V\boldsymbol{p}
), there are several in (
2
)-(
5
). In particular, we have neglected direct (“contact”, or “steric”) interactions between the particles. Such interactions could result, for instance, from the minimization of excluded volume between the particles. In the context of the Maier-Saupe theory
(
46
)
, or in the work of Doi and Edward
(
19
)
, contact interactions are modeled as an additional torque in (
3
), deriving from an interaction potential that aligns neighboring particles. Furthermore, this tendency must create a flow, which would translate into an extra stress tensor in the fluid equations.

We neglect contact interactions in this article, which is justified in the “dilute suspension” limit where the mean number density is small, i.e.,
N/L^{d}\ll 1
. We refer to
4
;
25
for a proper inclusion of contact interaction terms in (
3
).

The same gauges as in
51
are chosen for nondimensionalizing the equations. That is, we choose (i) the mean density
\Psi_{c}=N/L^{d}
for the density scale, (ii) the rescaled periodic box length
L_{c}=L/(2\upi)
for the length scale, (iii) the inverse shear rate of the fluid under an active stress
\sim\Psi_{c}|a_{0}|
for the time scale,
T_{c}=\mu/(\Psi_{c}|a_{0}|)
, and eventually (iv) the associated velocity difference across a distance
L_{c}
for the velocity scale
U_{c}=L_{c}/T_{c}
. This gives the set of nondimensional equations

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\right)-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{p}\cdot\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}\psi\right),

(6)

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\boldsymbol{p}+\boldsymbol{u}-D_{T}\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\log\psi),

(7)

\displaystyle\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathsfbi{I}-\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\right)(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{p}-D_{R}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{p}(\log\psi),

(8)

\displaystyle-\Delta\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}\varpi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\boldsymbol{\nabla}\psi\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},

(9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(10)

In (
7
) and (
8
), the diffusion coefficients have been nondimensionalized according to
D_{T}\rightarrow T_{c}L_{c}^{d}D_{T}
and
D_{R}\rightarrow T_{c}D_{R}
. Moreover, the nondimensional swimming speed
\beta=V/U_{c}
was introduced in (
7
).

2.2
A coarse-grained version with Bingham closure

System (
6
)-(
10
) possesses
d
dimensions in space and
d-1
dimensions in orientation (
\boldsymbol{p}
is a
d
-component vector but recall the constraint
||\boldsymbol{p}||=1
). The numerical cost associated with this high dimensionality is prohibitive, as we anticipate that producing steady statistics requires long (and/or many) simulations. The numerical cost is particularly problematic in the statistical characterization of rare events, which is the main focus of this article.

We will study a version of (
6
)-(
10
) that is coarse-grained over the orientational degrees of freedom. Specifically, we will not solve for
\psi(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p},t)
directly but for its first orientational moments, which by definition depend only on space and thus reduce the domain to
d
dimensions.

Let us define the zeroth to fourth moments of
\psi
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&c(\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi\boldsymbol{p}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\\
&\mathsfbi{R}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi\boldsymbol{p}^{(3)}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsfbi{S}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi\boldsymbol{p}^{(4)}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\end{split}

with
\boldsymbol{p}^{(n)}
the
n
th-order dyadic product of
\boldsymbol{p}
(e.g.
\boldsymbol{p}^{(3)}=\boldsymbol{p}\otimes\boldsymbol{p}\otimes\boldsymbol{p}
, etc…). The zeroth-moment,
c
, is the (spatial) concentration field of the particle. The first moment,
\boldsymbol{n}
, is the polarization vector of the particles, not to be confused with their preferred direction of nematic alignment. The polarization vector is meaningful only when particles exhibit a head-tail asymmetry, arising, for example, from propulsive mechanisms as here
(
52
)
, polarity sorting
(
28
)
, or asymmetric geometric effects
(
76
)
. The vector
\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
thus gives the local polar (i.e., signed) orientation of the particles.

The second moment,
\mathsfbi{D}
, is such that
c^{-1}\mathsfbi{D}
corresponds to the nematic tensor. The second moment obeys the conditions
\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\right)=c(\boldsymbol{x},t)
and
\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{x},t)^{T}=\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
, where the trace condition follows from
||\boldsymbol{p}||=1
. Due to its symmetry,
\mathsfbi{D}
has orthogonal eigenvectors with real eigenvalues. The eigenvector associated with the largest eigenvalue is called the “director”, and represents the preferred nematic (unsigned) alignment direction of the particles. The isotropic case, in which particles within the elementary volume are oriented randomly (and independently), corresponds to
\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\mathsfbi{I}/d
.

Note that particles can exhibit a clear preferred nematic alignment direction for a given
\boldsymbol{x}
, say
\boldsymbol{m}
, such that
\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\approx\boldsymbol{m}\boldsymbol{m}^{T}
, while having a zero polarization vector
\boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=0
there. It suffices that, around
\boldsymbol{x}
, there are as many particles along
-\boldsymbol{m}
as along
\boldsymbol{m}
.

By injecting (
7
) and (
8
) in (
6
), then integrating the resulting equation for
\psi
against
1
,
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}
over the unit sphere
\left\{\boldsymbol{p}:||\boldsymbol{p}||=1\right\}
, we are left with evolution equations for the moments
c
,
\boldsymbol{n}
, and
\mathsfbi{D}
, given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{D}c}{\mathrm{D}t}&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+D_{T}\Delta c,\\
\frac{\mathrm{D}\boldsymbol{n}}{\mathrm{D}t}-(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{n}+\mathsfbi{R}:\mathsfbi{E}&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathsfbi{D}+D_{T}\Delta\boldsymbol{n}+(1-d)D_{R}\boldsymbol{n},\\
\frac{\mathrm{D}\mathsfbi{D}}{\mathrm{D}t}-(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\mathsfbi{D}-\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})^{T}+2\mathsfbi{S}:\mathsfbi{E}&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathsfbi{R}+D_{T}\Delta\mathsfbi{D}-2dD_{R}\left(\mathsfbi{D}-\frac{c}{d}\mathsfbi{I}\right).\end{split}

(11)

Above,
\mathrm{D}(\bullet)/\mathrm{D}t\coloneq\partial_{t}(\bullet)+(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla})(\bullet)
is the material derivative. Terms on the left-hand side in (
11
) have purely kinematic origins and result from hydrodynamic advection and rotations. They involve the contractions
\left[\mathsfbi{R}:\mathsfbi{E}\right]_{i}=R_{ijk}E_{jk}
and
\left[\mathsfbi{S}:\mathsfbi{E}\right]_{ij}=S_{ijkl}E_{kl}
, where
\mathsfbi{E}\coloneq(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u}+(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})^{T})/2
is the symmetric strain-rate tensor. On the right-hand side of (
11
), all terms arise from translational and rotational diffusion, except those pre-multiplied by
\beta
, which result from particles’ motility. In each equation, the term in
\beta
acts as a conservative source term involving the next-order moment.

System (
11
) is not closed, for it involves the unknown third and fourth moments
\mathsfbi{R}
and
\mathsfbi{S}
. Proceeding similarly to deriving (
11
), the evolution equations for
\mathsfbi{R}
and
\mathsfbi{S}
can be obtained by integrating (
1
) against
\boldsymbol{p}^{(3)}
and
\boldsymbol{p}^{(4)}
, respectively. However, this will involve the fifth and sixth moments, for which equations must also be derived, and so on: the system will never close. A closure map, providing
\mathsfbi{R}
and
\mathsfbi{S}
as functions of
c
,
\boldsymbol{n}
, and
\mathsfbi{D}
, is thus needed for the final system to have as many equations as unknowns.

For that purpose, we proceed along the lines of
73
, Sec. II. D, and seek solutions for the density function
\psi
under the generalized polar “Bingham” form

\displaystyle\psi_{B}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p},t)=\frac{1}{Z(\boldsymbol{x},t)}\exp\left(\mathsfbi{B}(\boldsymbol{x},t):\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}+\boldsymbol{a}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right).

(12)

Above,
\mathsfbi{B}
is a
d\times d
symmetric and traceless tensor,
\boldsymbol{a}
is a
d
-dimensional vector, and
Z
is a scalar normalization factor that ensures conservation of the total number of particles. Let us also introduce the “relative” entropy

\displaystyle S\left[\psi||\psi_{0}\right](t)=\int_{\Omega}\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\left(\frac{\psi}{\psi_{0}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\psi}{\psi_{0}}\right)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

which, roughly speaking, provides a statistical measure of how different
\psi
is from a given density function
\psi_{0}
(hence the adjective “relative”). In the following,
\psi_{0}
is chosen as the uniform, isotropic density function
\psi_{0}=(\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}1\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p})^{-1}
, an equilibrium solution of (
6
)-(
10
) corresponding in dimensional form to
N
particles indeed. Then, the Bingham density function in (
12
) is special in that it minimizes the relative entropy between
\psi
and
\psi_{0}
, under the constraints that the first three moments of
\psi
, i.e.,
c
,
\boldsymbol{n}
, and
\mathsfbi{D}
, are known and thus must be matched.
The dependence of
\psi_{B}
on
Z
,
\boldsymbol{a}
, and
\mathsfbi{B}
purposely makes this matching possible. Indeed, integrating (
12
) against
1
,
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}
, and equalizing with the known
c
,
\boldsymbol{n}
and
\mathsfbi{D}
, respectively, we obtain

\displaystyle c(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{B}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\ \boldsymbol{n}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{B}\boldsymbol{p}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\ \text{and}\ \mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{B}\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p}.

Inverting the system above yields the proper
Z
,
\boldsymbol{a}
, and
\mathsfbi{B}
. Without matching constraints on any moments,
\psi_{B}
would be equal to
\psi_{0}
.

Seeking solutions in the form of
\psi_{B}
effectively closes the system, for the knowledge of
Z
,
\boldsymbol{a}
, and
\mathsfbi{B}
fully determines
\psi_{B}
, which, in turn, determines all the higher moments according to

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{R}_{B}\left[c,\boldsymbol{n},\mathsfbi{D}\right](\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{B}\boldsymbol{p}^{(3)}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\mathsfbi{S}_{B}\left[c,\boldsymbol{n},\mathsfbi{D}\right](\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{B}\boldsymbol{p}^{(4)}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},

etc. The square bracket denotes that, in effect, the higher moments become functionals of the first three only. The subscript
B
emphasizes that the moments have been obtained through the Bingham closure.

That higher moments can be determined from the lower ones physically amounts to saying that the system remains in a quasi-equilibrium regime
(
43
;
44
;
1
;
37
)
. Indeed,
\psi_{B}
is the density function that, conditioned on
c
,
\boldsymbol{n}
, and
\mathsfbi{D}
, is the closest to
\psi_{0}
under the entropic measure
S
and at each time
t
. Thereby, enforcing the solution
\psi_{B}
inherently assumes that all the moments higher than
\mathsfbi{D}
tend to relax to the equilibrium
\psi_{0}
much more rapidly than the typical time scales of
c
,
\boldsymbol{n}
, and
\mathsfbi{D}
. This separation of time scales justifies slaving the higher moments to the lower ones, since then these former depart from
\psi_{0}
only as a consequence of the matching constraints on these latter, and not because of their own dynamics.

By replacing
\mathsfbi{R}
and
\mathsfbi{S}
in (
11
) by
\mathsfbi{R}_{B}\left[c,\boldsymbol{n},\mathsfbi{D}\right]
and
\mathsfbi{S}_{B}\left[c,\boldsymbol{n},\mathsfbi{D}\right]
, respectively, we arrive at a closed system of five equations

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{D}_{t}c&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+D_{T}\Delta c,\\
\mathrm{D}_{t}\boldsymbol{n}-(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{n}+\mathsfbi{R}_{B}:\mathsfbi{E}&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathsfbi{D}+D_{T}\Delta\boldsymbol{n}+(1-d)D_{R}\boldsymbol{n},\\
\mathrm{D}_{t}\mathsfbi{D}-(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\mathsfbi{D}-\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})^{T}+2\mathsfbi{S}_{B}:\mathsfbi{E}&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathsfbi{R}_{B}+D_{T}\Delta\mathsfbi{D}-2dD_{R}\left(\mathsfbi{D}-\frac{c}{d}\mathsfbi{I}\right)\\
-\Delta\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}\varpi&=\pm\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathsfbi{D},\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0,\end{split}

(13)

for five unknown fields
(c,\boldsymbol{n},\mathsfbi{D},\boldsymbol{u},\varpi)(\boldsymbol{x},t)
. Compared to the DSS model, it was further shown in
73
;
72
that its coarse-grained version (
13
) yields the same temporal evolution equation for the relative entropy
S
. As we shall see, some linear stability properties are also in correct agreement.

3
Linear formulation

In this section, we assess the linear stability properties of the coarse-grained model with Bingham closure (
13
). This will serve as a building block for the weakly nonlinear analysis in the next section. From now onward, the analysis is restricted to pusher particles.

To lighten the notations, we concatenate the variables in the state vector
\boldsymbol{q}
such that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\left(c,\boldsymbol{n},\operatorname{vec}\left(\mathsfbi{D}\right),\boldsymbol{u},\varpi\right)^{T}(\boldsymbol{x},t),

with
\operatorname{vec}\left(\bullet\right)
the vectorization linear operator, sometimes abbreviated in
\underline{\bullet}
and transforming a rank-
n
tensor into a vector of dimension
d^{n}\times 1
. The vector
\boldsymbol{q}
thus has dimension
(1+d+d^{2}+d+1)\times 1
(keeping all redundant entries for
\mathsfbi{D}
).

A linear stability analysis unravels the time-asymptotic response of a system to infinitesimal initial perturbations around some steady, or “base” state. The selected base state, solution to the governing equations (
13
), is

\displaystyle c_{0}=1,\quad\boldsymbol{n}_{0}=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mathsfbi{D}_{0}=\mathsfbi{I}/d,\quad\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\text{and}\quad\varpi_{0}=0,

all concatenated into the vector
\boldsymbol{q}_{0}
. This corresponds to the uniform, isotropic density function
\psi_{0}=(\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}1\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p})^{-1}
(e.g.,
\psi_{0}=1/(2\upi)
for
d=2
) for the particles, as well as zero base velocity and pressure.

We then consider infinitesimally small departures from
\psi_{0}
and, accordingly, from
\boldsymbol{q}_{0}
and higher moments, according to

\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\coloneq\psi_{0}\left(1+\varepsilon\psi_{1}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\right)\equiv\psi_{B}=\exp\left(\mathsfbi{B}:\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}+\boldsymbol{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}+w\right),

(14)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\coloneq\boldsymbol{q}_{0}+\varepsilon\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+O(\varepsilon^{2}),\ \mathsfbi{R}_{B}^{\varepsilon}\coloneq\mathsfbi{R}_{0}+\varepsilon\mathsfbi{R}_{1}+O(\varepsilon^{2}),\ \mathsfbi{S}_{B}^{\varepsilon}=\mathsfbi{S}_{0}+\varepsilon\mathsfbi{S}_{1}+O(\varepsilon^{2}),

(15)

with
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. In the coarse-grained framework, the density function
\psi^{\varepsilon}
in (
14
) must be under Bingham form
\psi_{B}
, where we have defined
w\coloneq\ln(Z^{-1})
between (
12
) and (
14
). Thereby, to be asymptotically consistent, in (
14
) we must also expand

\displaystyle\begin{split}w^{\varepsilon}\coloneq w_{0}+\varepsilon w_{1}+O(\varepsilon^{2}),\ \ \boldsymbol{a}^{\varepsilon}\coloneq\boldsymbol{a}_{0}+\varepsilon\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+O(\varepsilon^{2}),\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathsfbi{B}^{\varepsilon}\coloneq\mathsfbi{B}_{0}+\varepsilon\mathsfbi{B}_{1}+O(\varepsilon^{2}).\end{split}

Injecting these in (
14
) (i.e., replacing each field by its version with the
\varepsilon
superscript), making a Taylor expansion of the exponential in terms of powers of
\varepsilon
, then identifying each power of
\varepsilon
between the right-hand side and the left-hand side of the equation, leads to,

\displaystyle\text{at $O(1)$}:\quad\psi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{0}}\exp\left(\mathsfbi{B}_{0}:\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}+\boldsymbol{a}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}\right),

(16)

\displaystyle\text{at $O(\varepsilon)$}:\quad\psi_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{B}_{1}:\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}+\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}+w_{1},

(17)

as well as higher order terms, irrelevant for the moment since
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Equation (
16
) simply leads to
Z_{0}=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}1\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p}
(e.g.,
Z_{0}=2\upi
for
d=2
),
\boldsymbol{a}_{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
, and
\mathsfbi{B}_{0}=\mathsfbi{0}
, thus confirming that the selected
\psi_{0}
can indeed be written under Bingham form. By construction, (
17
) is linear in
\mathsfbi{B}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}
and
w_{1}
.

At the same time, taking the orientational moments of
\psi^{\varepsilon}
in (
14
), then identifying the powers of
\varepsilon
with the moments expansions in (
15
), brings about

\displaystyle\begin{split}&c_{j}=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{0}\psi_{j}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\boldsymbol{n}_{j}=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{0}\psi_{j}\boldsymbol{p}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\mathsfbi{D}_{j}=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{0}\psi_{j}\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\\
&\mathsfbi{R}_{j}=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{0}\psi_{j}\boldsymbol{p}^{(3)}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\mathsfbi{S}_{j}=\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{0}\psi_{j}\boldsymbol{p}^{(4)}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\end{split}

for each order
j=1,2,...
and where the moments
c_{j}
,
\boldsymbol{n}_{j}
, and
\mathsfbi{D}_{j}
are those in the state vector
\boldsymbol{q}_{j}
. Thereby, multiplying (
17
) by
\psi_{0}
and then taking the orientational moments up to the fourth one yields the series of linear systems

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{D}_{0}:\mathsfbi{B}_{1}+\boldsymbol{n}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+w_{1}c_{0},

(18)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{n}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{R}_{0}:\mathsfbi{B}_{1}+\mathsfbi{D}_{0}:\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+w_{1}\boldsymbol{n}_{0},

(19)

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{D}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{S}_{0}:\mathsfbi{B}_{1}+\mathsfbi{R}_{0}:\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+w_{1}\mathsfbi{D}_{0},

(20)

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{T}_{0}:\mathsfbi{B}_{1}+\mathsfbi{S}_{0}:\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+w_{1}\mathsfbi{R}_{0},

(21)

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{U}_{0}:\mathsfbi{B}_{1}+\mathsfbi{T}_{0}:\boldsymbol{a}_{1}+w_{1}\mathsfbi{S}_{0},\quad\text{etc.}

(22)

where
\mathsfbi{R}_{0}
,
\mathsfbi{S}_{0}
,
\mathsfbi{T}_{0}
and
\mathsfbi{U}_{0}
are the third to sixth moments of the base density
\psi_{0}
. By linearity, (
18
)-(
20
) can be rewritten more synthetically under the form

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}\end{bmatrix}=\mathsfbi{M}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}w_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{B}}_{1}\end{bmatrix},

(23)

where
\mathsfbi{M}_{0}
is the matrix such that (
23
) is equivalent to (
18
)-(
20
). The subscript “
0
” highlights its dependence on base moments (known). Similarly, we can rewrite
\mathsfbi{R}_{1}
and
\mathsfbi{S}_{1}
as

\displaystyle\underline{\mathsfbi{R}}_{1}=\mathsfbi{M}^{R}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}w_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{B}}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{\mathsfbi{S}}_{1}=\mathsfbi{M}^{S}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}w_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{1}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{B}}_{1}\end{bmatrix},

(24)

respectively, where we have defined the matrices
\mathsfbi{M}^{R}_{0}
and
\mathsfbi{M}^{S}_{0}
. The linear system (
23
) can be inverted after embedding the definitions
\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathsfbi{B}_{1}\right)=0
and
\mathsfbi{B}^{T}_{1}=\mathsfbi{B}_{1}
(which must hold at each order
j=1,2,...
), for example by using projector operators or replacing
d(d-1)/2+1
lines of the system by these constraint equations. Injecting the inverse of (
23
) in (
24
) then gives

\displaystyle\underline{\mathsfbi{R}}_{1}=\mathsfbi{M}^{R}_{0}\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{\mathsfbi{S}}_{1}=\mathsfbi{M}^{S}_{0}\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{1}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}\end{bmatrix}.

(25)

Thereby, at
O(\varepsilon)
, the higher moments can be deduced as a linear (because linearized) combination of the first three.

Furthermore, since the base solution does not vary over space or time, and since the spatial domain has periodic boundary conditions and the temporal one extends to positive infinity, perturbations can be sought in the form of Fourier modes

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}\exp\left(\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}+\sigma t\right)+\text{c.c.},

(26)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{1}\exp\left(\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}+\sigma t\right)+\text{c.c.}.

(27)

The components of the wavenumber vector
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
must be integers because the spatial domain is finite and
2\pi
-periodic, which quantizes the admissible wavenumbers. On the other hand,
\sigma\in\mathbb{C}
is a complex-valued scalar. The real part of
\sigma
, i.e.,
\sigma_{r}
, represents the growth rate of the mode, while the imaginary part,
\sigma_{i}
, represents its frequency. The quantities
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}
and
\hat{\psi}_{1}
do not depend on space or time.

Injecting the expansion (
15
) of the state vector into the governing equations (
13
), then using (
25
) and (
26
), and eventually collecting then terms at
O(\varepsilon)
, results in the linear system

\displaystyle\sigma\mathsfbi{H}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}=\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1},\ \text{under the constraints: $\operatorname{tr}\left(\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}\right)=\hat{c}_{1}$ and $\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}^{T}=\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}$}.

(28)

We have defined the singular mass matrix

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{H}\coloneq\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathsfbi{0}&\mathsfbi{0}&\mathsfbi{0}&0\\
0&\mathsfbi{I}_{d\times d}&\mathsfbi{0}&\mathsfbi{0}&0\\
0&\mathsfbi{0}&\mathsfbi{I}_{d^{2}\times d^{2}}&\mathsfbi{0}&0\\
0&\mathsfbi{0}&\mathsfbi{0}&\mathsfbi{0}&0\\
0&\mathsfbi{0}&\mathsfbi{0}&\mathsfbi{0}&0\\
\end{bmatrix},

with
\mathsfbi{I}_{d\times d}
the identity matrix of dimension
d\times d
. We have also defined the linear operator
\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, acting over
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}
according to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}\coloneq\\
&\begin{bmatrix}-\text{i}\beta\boldsymbol{k}^{T}\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}_{1}-D_{T}||\boldsymbol{k}||^{2}\hat{c}_{1}\\
-\text{i}\beta\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}\boldsymbol{k}-\left(D_{T}||\boldsymbol{k}||^{2}+(d-1)D_{R}\right)\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}_{1}\\
\operatorname{vec}\left(-\text{i}\beta\hat{\mathsfbi{R}}_{1}:\boldsymbol{k}+\text{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}\boldsymbol{k}^{T})\mathsfbi{D}_{0}+\text{i}\mathsfbi{D}_{0}(\boldsymbol{k}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}^{T})-2\mathsfbi{S}_{0}:\hat{\mathsfbi{E}}_{1}-\left(D_{T}||\boldsymbol{k}||^{2}+2dD_{R}\right)\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}+2D_{R}\mathsfbi{I}\hat{c}_{1}\right)\\
-\text{i}\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}\boldsymbol{k}-||\boldsymbol{k}||^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}-\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\hat{\varpi}_{1}\\
\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}^{T}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}\end{bmatrix},\end{split}

(29)

with
\hat{\mathsfbi{E}}_{1}=\text{i}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}\boldsymbol{k}^{T}+\boldsymbol{k}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}^{T})/2
and where it is implicit that
\hat{\mathsfbi{R}}_{1}
depends linearly on
\hat{c}_{1}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}_{1}
and
\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}
by application of the matrix
\mathsfbi{M}_{0}^{R}\mathsfbi{M}_{0}^{-1}
in (
25
). In (
29
), the subscript “
\boldsymbol{k}
” emphasizes the dependence of the operator on the wavenumber vector
\boldsymbol{k}
.

Equation (
28
) is a generalized eigenvalue problem for the eigenvalues
\sigma
and corresponding eigenvectors. Since the trace and symmetry constraints correspond to linear algebraic equations (
(d-1)d/2+1
in number), they can be enforced by means of a projection operator. This idea is further developed in Appendix
9
, as it is important for the practical implementation of (
28
). In the remainder, we ignore the eigenvectors contained in the kernel of
\mathsfbi{H}
, i.e., those with nonzero components only in pressure and/or velocity, as they are associated with infinite eigenvalues. Consequently, for a given
\boldsymbol{k}
, we only retain
1+d+d^{2}
eigenvectors. We assume they constitute a complete basis for the concentration, orientation vector, and second moment (but incomplete in velocity and pressure because of the unconsidered kernel).

Although this is not done in what follows, we note that, for such a simple base solution, the analytical developments could be taken further. For instance, taking
d=2
, it is possible to show that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{D_{R}\rightarrow 0}\sigma(k)=\frac{1}{8}\pm\frac{1}{8}\left[1-16(\beta k)^{2}\right]^{1/2}-D_{T}k^{2},\end{split}

(30)

where we have defined
k\coloneq||\boldsymbol{k}||
. By isotropy of the base solution, the wavefront direction does not matter for the stability properties, such that the eigenvalues depend only on
k
. We refer to
73
for further details regarding the derivation of (
30
), although a different nondimensionalization was chosen therein.

From (
26
), it is clear that an eigenmode with wavenumber
k
is (linearly) unstable if the real part of the corresponding eigenvalue
\sigma(k)
is strictly positive. By contrast, an eigenmode is strictly stable if the real part of the associated eigenvalue is strictly negative, and neutral (sometimes also called “marginally stable”) if the real part of the eigenvalue is null. By abuse of language, a wavenumber
k
is said to be unstable if it exhibits at least one unstable eigenmode, and is stable in the opposite scenario where all its eigenmodes are stable. The special case
\boldsymbol{k}=0
requires a special treatment, detailed in Appendix
9
. Indeed, it must be solved in a reduced space free of velocity and pressure. It is then shown in Appendix
9
that, by virtue of the conservation of total particle number,
\boldsymbol{k}=0
yields only strictly stable eigenmodes if
D_{R}>0
.

For all the other
\boldsymbol{k}
, such that
k=||\boldsymbol{k}||>0
, we show in figure
1(a)
the real parts of the eigenvalues
\sigma(k)
solving (
28
). Part of these results were already presented in
73
(figure
1
therein).

(a)

(b)

Figure 1
:
Linear dispersion relation for the DSS model (dashed-dotted line, for
D_{R}=0
) and its coarse-grained version (continuous lines, each for a different
D_{R}\in\left\{10^{-4},10^{-3},0.02\right\}
, darker shades for larger
D_{R}
).
The symbol
k\coloneq||\boldsymbol{k}||
designates the norm of the wavenumber vector. Quantities are shown as a function of
k>0
rescaled by the swimming speed
\beta
.
(a) Growth rate
\sigma_{r}\coloneq\Re(\sigma)
of the most unstable branch; the stabilizing contribution from translational diffusion,
-D_{T}k^{2}
, is subtracted to bring insight to the cut-off mechanisms. The critical
D_{T,c}
below which the
k=1
eigenmodes become unstable is defined as that for which
\sigma_{r}(k=1)=0
. The maximum value of
\sigma_{r}+D_{T}k^{2}
over
\beta k
thus corresponds to
D_{T,c}
over
\beta
. Three dot markers highlight the values of
D_{T,c}
at the parameters selected for further analysis:
D_{R}=0.02
and
\beta\in\left\{0,0.15,0.5\right\}
.
(b) Corresponding frequency
\sigma_{i}\coloneq\Im(\sigma)
of the most unstable branch. It also corresponds to the dependence of
\omega
on
\beta
, with
\omega
the frequency of the
k=1
neutral eigenmodes at
D_{T}=D_{T,c}
; if
|\omega|\neq 0
(resp.
\omega=0
), the
k=1
wavenumbers experience a Hopf (resp. pitchfork) bifurcation by decreasing
D_{T}
below
D_{T,c}

The results are shown as a function of
\beta k
, and the stabilizing contribution from diffusion,
-D_{T}k^{2}
, is subtracted from the growth rates to highlight potential other stabilizing mechanisms. Only the eigenvalue branches for which
\sigma_{r}(k)+D_{T}k^{2}
becomes positive over some
k
range are shown. The corresponding imaginary parts of the eigenvalues, i.e., the oscillation frequency of the corresponding eigenmodes, are shown in figure
1(b)
. Three different values of
D_{R}
are considered. For the sake of comparison, we also reproduce from
51
the result from the DSS model for
D_{R}=0
(figure
3
therein).

For the coarse-grained model, the dispersion relation (
30
) is recovered in the limit
D_{R}\rightarrow 0
, such that
\sigma_{r}+D_{T}k^{2}
becomes constant and equal to
1/8
for
\beta k\geq 1/4
. Still in that limit, for
\beta k<1/4
there are two distinct branches with
\sigma_{r}+D_{T}k^{2}\geq 0
, each of which is associated with a zero frequency. By contrast, for
\beta k\geq 1/4
, the growth rates of these two branches merge, and the frequencies become nonzero and equal and opposite.
For
\beta=0
, i.e., immotile particles, the agreement with the dispersion relation from the DSS model is exact as the third moment
\hat{\mathsfbi{R}}_{1}
vanishes in (
29
), and thus the closure model is unimportant. The agreement between the DSS model and its coarse-grained version then progressively degrades as
\beta k
increases. In particular, the threshold value of
\beta k
at which the two branches merge seems to be slightly overestimated by the coarse-grained version. Moreover, above that threshold, the DSS model presents a decreasing
\sigma_{r}+D_{T}k^{2}
. This suggests that there are stabilizing effects that are not solely due to translational diffusion and that are not captured in the coarse-grained version.

Increasing the rotational diffusion has a stabilizing effect for all
\beta k
. Whether the branches represented in figure
1(a)
correspond to stable or unstable eigenmodes depends on the value of
D_{T}
. However, in all cases, for a fixed value of
\beta
, the growth rate
\sigma_{r}
decreases monotonically over
k>0
, such that among all
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\left\{\boldsymbol{0}\right\}
, the wavenumber vector amplitude
k=1
systematically is the most unstable or the least stable. Recall that
\boldsymbol{k}
must have integer components and that
k=0
is a special case that is always strictly stable.

Let us now define
D_{T,c}
as the critical/threshold value of the translational diffusivity
D_{T}
below which the
k=1
wavenumbers become unstable. In other words, at
D_{T}=D_{T,c}
, the growth rate of the
k=1
wavenumbers is zero by definition. Decreasing (resp. increasing)
D_{T}
below
D_{T,c}
renders them unstable (resp. strictly stable). We also define
\omega
as the frequencies of the neutral eigenmode(s) at
D_{T}=D_{T,c}
.

Precisely because
\sigma_{r}(k=1)=0
for
D_{T}=D_{T,c}
, the dependence of
D_{T,c}
(resp.
\omega
) on
\beta
, for a given
D_{R}
, is the same as that of the maximum value of
\sigma_{r}+D_{T}k^{2}
over
\beta k
in figure
1(a)
(resp. figure
1(b)
). In particular, there is a threshold swimming speed
\beta
above which
D_{T,c}
becomes independent of it. As said, this threshold tends to
\beta=1/4
in the limit
D_{R}\rightarrow 0
, and is found to be
\beta\approx 0.19
for
D_{R}=0.02
. Below this threshold
\beta
, there is only one neutral eigenmode per
\boldsymbol{k}
(with
k=1
), with
\omega=0
. Above the threshold, there are a pair of neutral eigenmodes per
\boldsymbol{k}
with equal and opposite nonzero frequencies. Accordingly, below this threshold
\beta
, the
k=1
wavenumbers undergo a static pitchfork bifurcation as
D_{T}
decreases below
D_{T,c}
, whereas they experience an oscillatory Hopf bifurcation above it. We shall study the weakly nonlinear dynamics past the onset of these bifurcations in
D_{T}
only.

In what follows, for a given
\boldsymbol{k}
, the corresponding family of eigenvectors (or “eigenbasis”) is denoted
\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
, where each
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}
is a solution of (
28
) with eigenvalue
\sigma_{j}
. They are sometimes referred to as the “direct” eigenmodes, in opposition to the “adjoint” ones, which we introduce in a moment. Within the family, the eigenvectors are ordered such that
\sigma_{r,1}\geq\sigma_{r,2}\geq...
.

In the following, it is instructive
to extract the component of the state vector along the bifurcating eigenmode(s) at
k=1
. For that purpose, we shall define another basis, biorthogonal to the eigenbasis under a predefined inner product. Since the fields do not depend on space anymore after isolating the Fourier component along
\boldsymbol{k}
, the inner product is chosen as the Hermitian dot product
\left\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\right\rangle_{u,p}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}^{H}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}
, where
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}
are two arbitrary vectors that do not depend on space (but can depend on time) and where the superscript
H
designates the Hermitian transpose. The subscript “
(u,p)
” emphasizes that the inner product includes velocity and pressure, even though the latter are not differentiated with respect to time due to the presence of
\mathsfbi{H}
. From the choice of the inner product, it is possible to construct an operator
\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\dagger}
, “adjoint” to
\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, and defined as

\left\langle\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{u,p}\coloneq\left\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\middle|\mathsfbi{L}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\right\rangle_{u,p}
, for all
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsfbi{L}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}})

where
\mathcal{D}(\bullet)
is the domain of the operator. By definition of the second moment, the adjoint fields must obey the same trace and symmetry conditions as the direct one, i.e.,
\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathsfbi{D}^{\dagger}\right)=c^{\dagger}
and
\mathsfbi{D}^{\dagger,T}=\mathsfbi{D}^{\dagger}
. The two domains
\mathcal{D}(\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathsfbi{L}^{\dagger}_{k})
each embed these conditions and are the same. For a given
\boldsymbol{k}
, the eigenmodes of the adjoint system, constituting the adjoint eigenbasis
\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
. The adjoint eigenmodes satisfy
\sigma_{j}^{*}\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}_{j}=\mathsfbi{L}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}_{j}
, under the trace and symmetry constraints, and for
j=1,2,...
. We have used the straightforward result
\mathsfbi{H}^{\dagger}=\mathsfbi{H}
, and
\sigma_{j}^{*}
is the complex conjugate of the eigenvalue
\sigma_{j}
of the corresponding direct problem. We discuss the practical construction of the adjoint eigenbasis in Appendix
9
.

Crucially, for a given
\boldsymbol{k}
, the corresponding adjoint and direct families are biorthogonal under the inner product

\displaystyle\left\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\right\rangle\coloneq\left\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\middle|\mathsfbi{H}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\right\rangle_{u,p}=\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}^{H}\mathsfbi{H}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}.

In words,
\left\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{a}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{b}\right\rangle
is the Hermitian dot product that includes the mass matrix,
\mathsfbi{H}
, which removes contributions from velocity and pressure. The biorthogonality property is such that

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}_{j}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{i}\right\rangle=\delta_{ij},

(31)

with
\delta_{ij}
the Kronecker symbol.

We restrict the subsequent analysis to two spatial dimensions, i.e.,
d=2
, with
\boldsymbol{x}=(x,y)^{T}
. Since
\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\backslash\left\{\boldsymbol{0}\right\}
, then the
k=1
wavenumbers of interest only include
\boldsymbol{k}=\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
with
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\coloneq(1,0)^{T}
and
\boldsymbol{k}=\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
with
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\coloneq(0,1)^{T}
.
At
D_{T}=D_{T,c}
, each of these wavenumbers is associated with either one or two neutral eigenmodes, depending on the value of
\beta
. For values of
\beta
corresponding to
\omega=0
, i.e., in the pitchfork bifurcation region, such as for
\beta\leq 0.19
for
D_{R}=0.02
(see figure
1(b)
), there is only one. The neutral eigenmode associated with the wavenumber
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
(and
\omega=0
) is denoted
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A}
, as emphasized by the superscript. Accordingly, the neutral eigenmode associated with
-\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
is the complex conjugate
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A,*}
. We also define the neutral eigenmode
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B}
for
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
and
\omega=0
(and is complex conjugate for
-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
). The adjoint eigenmodes are denoted
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A,\dagger}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B,\dagger}
, respectively.

By contrast, for
\beta>0.19
there is a pair of equal but opposite nonzero frequencies (see figure
1(b)
), and thus there are two neutral eigenmodes per wavenumber. They consist of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{\pm}}
for the eigenmodes oscillating along the wavenumber
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
with frequencies
\pm\omega
(with
\omega>0
), as well as
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{\pm}}
along
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
with frequencies
\pm\omega
. Mathematically,

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\text{i}\omega\mathsfbi{H}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}})\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}=(-\text{i}\omega\mathsfbi{H}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}})\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}=(\text{i}\omega\mathsfbi{H}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}})\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}}=(-\text{i}\omega\mathsfbi{H}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}})\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}}=\boldsymbol{0}.\end{split}

(32)

To facilitate the comparison of our results with those in
51
, all direct eigenmodes for all
\boldsymbol{k}
(including
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B}
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{\pm}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{\pm}}
) are normalized according to

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{32}.

(33)

The normalization of the adjoint eigenmodes follows from (
31
). We have now completely characterized the neutral eigenmodes spanning the slow manifold of (
13
).

4
Stochastic and weakly nonlinear formulation

We now introduce the stochastically forced version of the system (
13
), as this is the central focus of our analysis. We then proceed to reduce the dimensionality of the subsequent system to its slow manifold.

Let us write
\boldsymbol{p}=(\cos(p),\sin(p))^{T}
with
0\leq p\leq 2\pi
. Before coarse-graining, the stochastic forcing is introduced solely in the density conservation equation (
1
), as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi\right)-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{p}\cdot\left(\dot{\boldsymbol{p}}\psi\right)+Ff_{\psi},

(34)

where
f_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{x},p,t)
is an additive Gaussian noise, white in space, orientation, and time, and with zero average and unit intensity, such that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{x},p,t)f_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},p^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]=\delta(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\delta(p-p^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime}),

(35)

The symbol
\mathbb{E}\left[\bullet\right]
denotes the ensemble average over noise realizations. In (
34
) we have also introduced the free scalar parameter
F\in\mathbb{R}
, so that the intensity of
Ff_{\psi}
is directly given by
F^{2}
.

Importantly, we require the stochastic forcing to have zero spatial average for every orientation and time, i.e.,
\int_{\Omega}f_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{x},p,t)\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0
for each
p
and
t
. This guarantees that the total number of particles is conserved for each time
t
, despite the presence of forcing.

The stochastic forcing
Ff_{\psi}
represents the effect of neglected terms in the model, for instance, due to intrinsic particle dynamics, which would modify the conservation equation (see Sec. 2.7.2 in
61
). It could also model the effect of unresolved scales. In the absence of additional experimental information, the noise is chosen to be as non-specific as possible, that is, white. Note that it would perhaps be more physically relevant to write
f_{\psi}
under conservative form, i.e., as the divergence of a tensor. Nonetheless, our paper primarily focuses on methodology, and we expect our developments to be easily adaptable to conservative noise.

The stochastic forcing
Ff_{\psi}
translates into the coarse-grained model as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{D}_{t}c&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+D_{T}\Delta c+Ff_{c},\\
\mathrm{D}_{t}\boldsymbol{n}-(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{n}+\mathsfbi{R}_{B}:\mathsfbi{E}&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathsfbi{D}+D_{T}\Delta\boldsymbol{n}+(1-d)D_{R}\boldsymbol{n}+F\boldsymbol{f}_{n},\\
\mathrm{D}_{t}\mathsfbi{D}-(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\mathsfbi{D}-\mathsfbi{D}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})^{T}+2\mathsfbi{S}_{B}:\mathsfbi{E}&=-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathsfbi{R}_{B}+D_{T}\Delta\mathsfbi{D}-2dD_{R}\left(\mathsfbi{D}-\frac{c}{d}\mathsfbi{I}\right)+F\mathsfbi{F}_{D},\\
-\Delta\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}\varpi&=\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\mathsfbi{D},\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}=0.\end{split}

(36)

Equations for the moments differ from their deterministic counterparts in (
13
) by the inclusion of the stochastic forcing terms in
f_{c}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
,
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
, and
\mathsfbi{F}_{D}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
, defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f_{c}\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}f_{\psi}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\boldsymbol{f}_{n}\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}f_{\psi}\boldsymbol{p}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\mathsfbi{F}_{D}\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}f_{\psi}\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p}.\end{split}

(37)

The equations for the velocity, however, remain unforced and unchanged. The forcing fields
f_{c}
,
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}
, and
\mathsfbi{F}_{D}
inherit from
\boldsymbol{f}
the property of having zero spatial average at each time
t
. Furthermore, their definitions (
37
) imply the trace and symmetry conditions,
\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathsfbi{F}_{D}\right)=f_{c}
, and
\mathsfbi{F}_{D}^{T}=\mathsfbi{F}_{D}
.

The variance and covariance between each component of
f_{c}
,
\boldsymbol{f}_{n}
, and
\mathsfbi{F}_{D}
can be characterized directly from (
37
) and (
35
). For example,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{c}(\boldsymbol{x},t)F_{D,xx}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]&=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos^{2}(p^{\prime})\mathbb{E}\left[f_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{x},p,t)f_{\psi}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},p^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]\mathrm{d}p\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\\
&=\delta(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime})\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos^{2}(p)\mathrm{d}p=\delta(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime})\pi.\end{split}

Proceeding similarly with all the other components, the associated variance and covariance are summarized in table
1
.

f_{c}

f_{n,x}

f_{n,y}

F_{D,xx}

F_{D,xy}

\upi

0

\upi

0

\upi

Table 1
:
Covariance amplitudes, multiplying
\delta(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime})
, between different components of the stochastic forcing at the coarse-grained level. The table is symmetric, so its lower-triangular part is not written explicitly.

All forcing components acting at the coarse-grained level are also white in time and space.

Let us move to the weakly nonlinear reduction of (
36
). We consider values of
D_{T}
smaller but asymptotically close to the threshold
D_{T,c}
, by taking

\begin{split}D_{T}=D_{T,c}-\varepsilon^{2},\end{split}

(38)

with
0\leq\varepsilon\ll 1
.

For
D_{T}
as in (
38
), the neutral eigenmodes at
k=1
computed in the previous section become unstable with
O(\varepsilon^{2})
growth rates. Consequently, in the deterministic regime (i.e., without stochastic forcing), these eigenmodes would slowly grow in amplitude until nonlinear effects become important. All the other eigenmodes remain strictly stable with
O(1)
damping rates and thus, in the absence of sustained excitation, would rapidly vanish over time. In other words, in the deterministic regime, there is a separation of time scales (or “spectral gap”) between the strictly stable, “fast”, eigenmodes and the bifurcating, “slow”, ones. The fast eigenmodes tend to relax to the base state much more rapidly than do the slow ones; therefore, it is these latter that are expected to dominate the deterministic, linear, and weakly nonlinear dynamics near the threshold. In the following, “fast” and “slow” will always refer to the deterministic dynamics

We will maintain this hierarchy between the slow and fast eigenmodes in the stochastically forced case, by assuming a weak stochastic forcing, i.e.,
F\ll 1
. In particular,
F
is scaled in terms of
\varepsilon
as

\begin{split}F=\varepsilon^{2}\phi\ll 1,\end{split}

(39)

where
\phi=O(1)
is a free and real-valued scalar. Given that all eigenmodes are excited indiscriminately and in a sustained manner by the stochastic forcing, it is perhaps not obvious “
a priori
” that the slow eigenmodes still dominate the dynamics. However, under (
39
), the slow eigenmodes will show a much larger response to the forcing than the fast ones, because they are, by definition, closer to resonance. Intuitively, the slow eigenmodes have made less progress in relaxing to equilibrium between successive noise impulses than do the fast ones, and thus can sustain a larger variance.

The solution of the system (
36
) is approximated through an asymptotic expansion with powers of
\varepsilon
, similar to (
14
)-(
15
), though retaining some higher-order terms as

\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{0}\left(1+\varepsilon\psi_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\psi_{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\psi_{3}+O(\varepsilon^{4})\right)\equiv\psi_{B},

(40)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}_{0}+\varepsilon\boldsymbol{q}_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\boldsymbol{q}_{3}+O(\varepsilon^{4}),

(41)

and expanding similarly
\mathsfbi{R}_{B}^{\varepsilon}=\mathsfbi{R}_{0}+\varepsilon\mathsfbi{R}_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathsfbi{R}_{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathsfbi{R}_{3}+O(\varepsilon^{4})
,
\mathsfbi{S}_{B}^{\varepsilon}
,
w^{\varepsilon}
,
\boldsymbol{a}^{\varepsilon}
, and
\mathsfbi{B}^{\varepsilon}
. Again, the density function (
40
) is sought under Bingham form. Consequently, proceeding similarly to the previous section, we obtain

\displaystyle\psi_{j}=\mathsfbi{B}_{j}:\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}+\boldsymbol{a}_{j}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{p}+w_{j}+\chi_{j},\quad j=1,2,...

(42)

where each
\chi_{j}
stems from nonlinear interactions of the
\psi_{j}
s obtained at previous orders. In particular,

\displaystyle\chi_{1}=0,\quad\chi_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\psi_{1}^{2},\quad\chi_{3}=-\frac{1}{3}\psi_{1}^{3}+\psi_{1}\psi_{2},\quad\text{etc.}

(43)

Again, multiplying (
42
) by
\psi_{0}
and taking the zeroith to second moments gives

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}c_{j}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{j}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{j}\end{bmatrix}=\mathsfbi{M}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}w_{j}\\
\boldsymbol{a}_{j}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{B}}_{j}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}c_{\chi,j}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{\chi,j}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{\chi,j}\end{bmatrix},\quad j=1,2,...

(44)

where the subscript “
(\chi,j)
” denotes the corresponding moment of
\psi_{0}\chi_{j}
. That is,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&c_{\chi,j}\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{0}\chi_{j}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\boldsymbol{n}_{\chi,j}\coloneq\int_{||\boldsymbol{p}||=1}\psi_{0}\chi_{j}\boldsymbol{p}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{p},\quad\text{etc.}\end{split}

Thereafter, multiplying (
42
) by
\psi_{0}
, taking the third and fourth moments, and applying
\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}
in (
44
), which again embeds the constraints
\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathsfbi{B}_{j}\right)=0
and
\mathsfbi{B}^{T}_{j}=\mathsfbi{B}_{j}
, leads to

\displaystyle\underline{\mathsfbi{R}}_{j}=\mathsfbi{M}^{R}_{0}\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}c_{j}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{j}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{j}\end{bmatrix}+\underline{\mathsfbi{R}}_{f,j},\ \ \text{where}\ \ \underline{\mathsfbi{R}}_{f,j}\coloneq-\mathsfbi{M}^{R}_{0}\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}c_{\chi,j}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{\chi,j}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{\chi,j}\end{bmatrix}+\underline{\mathsfbi{R}}_{\chi,j},

(45)

for the third moment, as well as

\displaystyle\underline{\mathsfbi{S}}_{j}=\mathsfbi{M}^{S}_{0}\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}c_{j}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{j}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{j}\end{bmatrix}+\underline{\mathsfbi{S}}_{f,j},\ \ \text{where}\ \ \underline{\mathsfbi{S}}_{f,j}\coloneq-\mathsfbi{M}^{S}_{0}\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}c_{\chi,j}\\
\boldsymbol{n}_{\chi,j}\\
\underline{\mathsfbi{D}}_{\chi,j}\end{bmatrix}+\underline{\mathsfbi{S}}_{\chi,j},

(46)

for the fourth, and where
j=1,2,...
. The third and fourth moments (and all the higher ones) are expressed as the sum of two terms. The first term is linear in the first three moments (zeroth to second). In contrast, the second term acts as a forcing term (hence the subscript “
f
)“ and arises purely from the moments of nonlinear interactions among the
\psi_{j}
determined at previous orders. Thus, at the stage of solving for
j
th order, equations (
44
), (
45
), and (
46
) are indeed all linear (because linearized), and the first three moments are the only unknowns. Again, all higher moments can be determined from the first three.
Equation (
44
) is important in what follows, for it is the one that, together with (
42
), links the
j^{th}
correction in the state vector to the
j
th correction in the density.

Keeping this in mind, let us now examine the expansion (
41
) of the state vector. As motivated above, the leading-order solution
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
is chosen to be contained in the slow manifold, such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=&A_{+}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}+\text{i}\omega t}+A_{-}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}-\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+B_{+}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}}e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}+\text{i}\omega t}+B_{-}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}}e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}-\text{i}\omega t}+\text{c.c.},\end{split}

(47)

for the codimension-
4
Hopf bifurcation, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{q}_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=A(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A}e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+B(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B}e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\text{c.c.},\end{split}

(48)

for the codimension-
2
pitchfork bifurcation. In (
47
) and (
48
), the eigenmodes are pre-multiplied by
O(1)
complex-valued scalar amplitudes, depending on time only, and for the moment unknown. The purpose of the expansion procedure is to derive the evolution equations of these amplitudes.

Readers should be aware that the following calculations are intricate. While they may appeal to those interested in the article’s methodological and mathematical aspects, those interested in the physical results are invited to accept the amplitude equations (
89
) and (
88
) and jump directly to Sec.
6.1
for the results.

Without further ado, and no loss of generality, we will first restrict our calculations to the codimension-
4
Hopf bifurcation case. The codimension-
2
pitchfork bifurcation case will then be treated simply by dropping particular terms in the final results.

For (
47
), the evolution equations for the amplitudes are sought in the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{j}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\varepsilon^{2}\left(f_{\Lambda_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})+\varepsilon f^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})+\ldots\right)+\varepsilon\phi\left(\xi_{\Lambda_{j}}(t)+\varepsilon\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)+\ldots\right),\\
&\text{with}\quad\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\doteq(A_{+},A_{-},B_{+},B_{-},A_{+}^{*},A_{-}^{*},B_{+}^{*},B_{-}^{*}),\end{split}

(49)

for
j=1,2,...,8
. Each function
f_{\Lambda_{j}}
and its higher-order corrections,
f^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{j}}
,
f^{(3)}_{\Lambda_{j}}
, …, have a purely deterministic origin and contain both linear (for the leading-order
f_{\Lambda_{j}}
) and nonlinear polynomial terms in the amplitudes. The
\varepsilon^{2}
prefactor follows from the scaling of the distance to criticality in (
38
).

An additive white noise process
\xi_{\Lambda_{j}}
is included in (
49
), as well as multiplicative higher-order corrections
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{j}}
,
\xi^{(3)}_{\Lambda_{j}}
, etc. The
\varepsilon\phi
scaling follows from the fact that, according to (
39
), the forcing is introduced at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
in the original equations, with prefactor
\phi
, whereas the amplitudes appear at
O(\varepsilon)
. Both
f_{\Lambda_{j}}
and
\xi_{\Lambda_{j}}
are unknowns for the moment, but will be determined by the weakly nonlinear procedure, thus validating
a posteriori
the Ansatz in (
49
). Note that
\xi_{\Lambda_{j}}
could have been assumed to also be multiplicative (or not necessarily white), without changing its final expression.

From now on, it would be possible to make the amplitudes depend on a slow timescale
\tau\coloneq\varepsilon^{2}t
, as
\Lambda_{j}=\Lambda_{j}(\tau)
, and proceed with the multiple-scale method. However, while this slow-time dependence is justified in the deterministic regime
(
51
)
, we choose not to enforce it explicitly here. That is because the amplitudes are stochastically forced by white noise, and thus their temporal derivatives are all diverging (at least in the
L^{2}
space of interest here). Therefore, we found it mathematically questionable to stipulate that they vary slowly over time. Nevertheless, we argue in Appendix
12
that the multiple-scale method (or at least a certain interpretation of it) leads to the same final systems at leading-order.
The method proposed below, on the other hand, relies on basic algebraic manipulations and, in that sense, may appear less arbitrary than the multiple-scale method.

A first-order correction to the state vector (
47
) corresponds to a first-order correction to the density function. Using (
42
) and (
44
) with
\chi_{1}=0
, we can derive

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{1}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{p},t)=&A_{+}(t)\hat{\psi}^{A_{+}}(\boldsymbol{p})e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}+\text{i}\omega t}+A_{-}(t)\hat{\psi}^{A_{-}}(\boldsymbol{p})e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}-\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+B_{+}(t)\hat{\psi}^{B_{+}}(\boldsymbol{p})e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}+\text{i}\omega t}+B_{-}(t)\hat{\psi}^{B_{-}}(\boldsymbol{p})e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}-\text{i}\omega t}+\text{c.c.},\end{split}

(50)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\psi}^{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{p})\coloneq\hat{\mathsfbi{B}}^{\Lambda}:\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}+\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\Lambda}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{p}+\hat{w}^{\Lambda},\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{bmatrix}\hat{w}^{\Lambda}\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\Lambda}\\
\underline{\hat{\mathsfbi{B}}}^{\Lambda}\end{bmatrix}=\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{c}^{\Lambda}\\
\tilde{\boldsymbol{n}}^{\Lambda}\\
\underline{\tilde{\mathsfbi{D}}}^{\Lambda}\end{bmatrix},\end{split}

where the symbol
\Lambda
denotes any one of the components of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
.

Injecting the scalings (
38
) and (
39
), as well as the expansion (
41
), into the system (
36
) results in the new expansion

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varepsilon\left[\left(\mathsfbi{H}\partial_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}\right)\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\right]+\varepsilon^{2}\left[\left(\mathsfbi{H}\partial_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}\right)\boldsymbol{q}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\boldsymbol{q}_{1}]-\beta\boldsymbol{r}_{2}-\phi\boldsymbol{f}\right]\\
&+\varepsilon^{3}\left[\left(\mathsfbi{H}\partial_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}\right)\boldsymbol{q}_{3}+\mathsfbi{H}\Delta\boldsymbol{q}_{1}-\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\boldsymbol{q}_{2}]-\beta\boldsymbol{r}_{3}\right]+O(\varepsilon^{4})=\boldsymbol{0}.\end{split}

(51)

In (
51
), we have used that the base state
\boldsymbol{q}_{0}
is an exact solution of (
36
), thus there are no terms at
O(1)
. In addition,
\mathsfbi{L}
results from the linearization of (
36
) around
\boldsymbol{q}_{0}
and at
D_{T,c}
. The fact that we consider
D_{T}
smaller than
D_{T,c}
, with a
\varepsilon^{2}
difference, naturally results in the
\mathsfbi{H}\Delta\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
term at
O(\varepsilon^{3})
in (
51
). By acting over some
\boldsymbol{q}_{j}
, the linear operator
\mathsfbi{L}
encompasses the contribution to
\mathsfbi{R}_{j}
that is linear in the first three moments in (
45
) (it produces a divergence term pre-multiplied by
\beta
in the linear operator). On the other hand, the contributions to
\mathsfbi{R}_{j}
with the subscript
f
result from nonlinear interactions of previous order terms, thus result as forcing terms denoted by
\boldsymbol{r}_{j}
in (
51
), and which is such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{r}_{j}\coloneq\left(0,\boldsymbol{0},-\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\mathbf{\cdot}\mathsfbi{R}_{f,j}\right),\boldsymbol{0},0\right)^{T},\quad\text{with}\quad j=1,2,....\end{split}

(52)

In (
51
), we have also defined the nonlinear, quadratic operator

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{i},\boldsymbol{q}_{j}]\coloneq\mathsfbi{C}[\boldsymbol{q}_{i},\boldsymbol{q}_{j}]+\mathsfbi{C}[\boldsymbol{q}_{j},\boldsymbol{q}_{i}],\end{split}

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathsfbi{C}[\boldsymbol{q}_{i},\boldsymbol{q}_{j}]\coloneq\begin{bmatrix}-(\boldsymbol{u}_{j}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla})c_{i}\\
-(\boldsymbol{u}_{j}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla})\boldsymbol{n}_{i}+(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\boldsymbol{n}_{i}-\mathsfbi{R}_{i}:\mathsfbi{E}_{j}\\
\operatorname{vec}\left(-(\boldsymbol{u}_{j}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla})\mathsfbi{D}_{i}+(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u}_{j})\mathsfbi{D}_{i}+\mathsfbi{D}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u}_{j})^{T}-2\mathsfbi{S}_{i}:\mathsfbi{E}_{j}\right)\\
\boldsymbol{0}\\
0\end{bmatrix},\end{split}

embedding the nonlinear interactions between the velocity field
\boldsymbol{u}_{j}
and the moments
c_{i}
,
\boldsymbol{n}_{i}
, and
\mathsfbi{D}_{i}
. Note that
\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{i},\boldsymbol{q}_{j}]=\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{j},\boldsymbol{q}_{i}]
. Eventually, the symbol
\boldsymbol{f}
denotes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\coloneq\left(f_{c},\boldsymbol{f}_{n},\operatorname{vec}\left(\mathsfbi{F}_{D}\right),\boldsymbol{0},0\right)^{T}(\boldsymbol{x},t).\end{split}

(53)

Applying
\left(\mathsfbi{H}\partial_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}\right)
to the expression (
47
) of
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(\mathsfbi{H}\partial_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}\right)\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\\
