=&\varepsilon\phi\mathsfbi{H}\left[e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\xi_{A_{+}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega t}+\xi_{A_{-}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega t}\right)+e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}\left(\xi_{B_{+}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega t}+\xi_{B_{-}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega t}\right)\right]\\
&+\varepsilon^{2}\mathsfbi{H}\Bigg[e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\left(f_{A_{+}}+\phi\xi^{(2)}_{A_{+}}\right)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega t}+\left(f_{A_{-}}+\phi\xi^{(2)}_{A_{-}}\right)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega t}\right]\\
&+e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}\left[\left(f_{B_{+}}+\phi\xi^{(2)}_{B_{+}}\right)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega t}+\left(f_{B_{-}}+\phi\xi^{(2)}_{B_{-}}\right)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega t}\right]\Bigg]+\text{c.c.}+O(\varepsilon^{3}),\end{split}

(54)

where we have used (
49
) and the definition of the eigenmodes in (
32
). Indeed, we recall that all linear operators in the expansion are constructed at
D_{T,c}
.

On the other hand, assuming the statistics of the desired solution to be uniform in space, each higher-order solution
\boldsymbol{q}_{j}
, with
j=2,3,...
, as well as the stochastic forcing, are represented as a Fourier series

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{q}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\boldsymbol{q}_{j,\boldsymbol{0}}(t)+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\backslash\left\{\boldsymbol{0}\right\}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j,\boldsymbol{k}}(t)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}},\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\backslash\left\{\boldsymbol{0}\right\}}\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}},\end{split}

(55)

where
\boldsymbol{q}_{j,0}(t)
corresponds to the spatial average of
\boldsymbol{q}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
, and where recall that the spatial average of the forcing is null by construction, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{0}}(t)=\boldsymbol{0},\forall t
. From this point forward, the index
\boldsymbol{k}
denotes the Fourier component oscillating in space at wavenumber
\boldsymbol{k}
of the corresponding field.

Introducing (
47
), (
54
) and (
55
) into (
51
) produces a new expansion for each of the spatial Fourier components. The expansion for the component oscillating at
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
, in particular, reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varepsilon^{2}\left[\left(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\right)\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}-\phi\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}+\phi\xi_{A_{+}}\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega t}+\phi\xi_{A_{-}}\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega t}-\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\right]\\
&+\varepsilon^{3}\Big[\left(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\right)\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}+\left(f_{A_{+}}-A_{+}+\phi\xi^{(2)}_{A_{+}}\right)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+\left(f_{A_{-}}-A_{-}+\phi\xi^{(2)}_{A_{-}}\right)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega t}-\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\Big]+O(\varepsilon^{4})=\boldsymbol{0}.\end{split}

(56)

The symbol
\boldsymbol{d}_{j}
denotes the forcing term, appearing at order
j
, and stemming from nonlinear interactions of fields determined at previous orders. Its sole purpose is to lighten the notations. From (
51
), it corresponds to

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}_{2}\coloneq\frac{1}{2}\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\boldsymbol{q}_{1}]+\beta\boldsymbol{r}_{2},\quad\boldsymbol{d}_{3}\coloneq\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\boldsymbol{q}_{2}]+\beta\boldsymbol{r}_{3},\quad\text{etc}.

(57)

In deriving the expansion (
56
), we have also used that the Laplacian
\Delta
becomes
-||\boldsymbol{k}||^{2}
when applied to a Fourier
\boldsymbol{k}
-component, but
||\boldsymbol{k}_{A}||=||\boldsymbol{k}_{B}||=1
.

The expansion for the component oscillating at
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
is similar to (
56
), but
A_{+}
,
A_{-}
, and
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
, are replaced by
B_{+}
,
B_{-}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, respectively (including within the superscripts and subscripts)

Let us now characterize the forcing term
\boldsymbol{d}_{2}
, induced by nonlinear interactions of the first-order with itself. For this, we first inject the expression (
50
) for
\psi_{1}
, into that for
\chi_{2}
given in (
43
). We then use (
45
) to compute the corresponding forcing in the fourth moment,
\mathsfbi{R}_{f,2}
, from which we can deduce
\boldsymbol{r}_{2}
in line with (
52
). In addition, the expression (
47
) for
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
is introduced in
\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\boldsymbol{q}_{1}]
. Since the first-order solution only includes the wavenumbers
\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
and
\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, and since both
\chi_{2}
and
\mathsfbi{N}[\boldsymbol{q}_{1},\boldsymbol{q}_{1}]
are quadratic nonlinearities,
\boldsymbol{d}_{2}
only includes the wavenumbers contained in the set

\displaystyle\begin{split}K\coloneq\left\{\boldsymbol{0},\pm 2\boldsymbol{k}_{A},\pm 2\boldsymbol{k}_{B},\pm(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B}),\pm(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\right\}.\end{split}

This implies in particular that
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, which should accounted for in the expansion (
56
) for
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
and its equivalent for
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
. After tedious but elementary algebra, it is possible to disentangle the following expression

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{d}_{2}(t)=&\boldsymbol{d}_{2,\boldsymbol{0}}(t)+\Big(\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)e^{2\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)e^{2\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}\\
&+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)e^{\text{i}(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)e^{\text{i}(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\text{c.c.}\Big).\end{split}

(58)

The contribution from the wavenumber
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{0}
itself can be further detailed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{d}_{2,\boldsymbol{0}}(t)=&|A_{+}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{+}^{*}}+|A_{-}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}A_{-}^{*}}+|B_{+}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+|B_{-}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-}B_{-}^{*}}\\
&+\left(A_{+}A_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}^{*}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+\text{c.c.}\right)+\left(B_{+}B_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}B_{-}^{*}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+\text{c.c.}\right),\end{split}

where the dependence of the amplitudes on time is implied. The pair of amplitudes in the superscript of each
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}
emphasizes that the latter is pre-multiplied by
\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}
. The order of appearance of the amplitudes in the superscript does not matter, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\Lambda_{j}\Lambda_{i}}
. Each
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}
is time-independent and reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}\coloneq\gamma\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda_{i}},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda_{j}}]+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}},\quad\text{with}\quad\gamma=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\;i\neq j\\
\frac{1}{2}&\text{if}\;i=j\end{cases}\end{split}

Because
\boldsymbol{q}_{1}
is real-valued,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda_{i}}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda_{i-4},*}
for
i=5,...,8
, e.g.,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}^{*}}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},*}
, etc. The operator
\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\bullet,\bullet]
is similar to
\mathsfbi{N}[\bullet,\bullet]
, except that the gradient
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
is replaced by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}\doteq\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}
. If it applies to
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
, then
\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}=\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
, etc. The term
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}=(0,\boldsymbol{0},-\operatorname{vec}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\nabla}}\mathbf{\cdot}\hat{\mathsfbi{R}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}_{f}\right),\boldsymbol{0},0)^{T}
requires the knowledge of
\hat{\mathsfbi{R}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}_{f}
, which is computed from (
45
) by replacing
\chi_{2}
by its contribution
\hat{\chi}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}
, where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\chi}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}(\boldsymbol{p})=\gamma\hat{\psi}^{\Lambda_{i}}(\boldsymbol{p})\hat{\psi}^{\Lambda_{j}}(\boldsymbol{p}).\end{split}

Again, because
\psi_{1}
is real-valued,
\hat{\psi}^{\Lambda_{i}}=\hat{\psi}^{\Lambda_{i-4},*}
for
i\geq 5
. For example, we evaluate
\hat{\chi}^{A_{+}A_{+}^{*}}=|\hat{\psi}^{A_{+}}|^{2}
,
\hat{\chi}^{A_{+}A_{+}}=\hat{\psi}^{A_{+}A_{+}}/2
, etc.

The contributions of the other wavenumbers to
\boldsymbol{d}_{2}
in (
58
) are detailed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)=&A_{+}A_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}+A_{+}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{+}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+A_{-}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}A_{-}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t},\end{split}

(59)

followed by,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)=&B_{+}B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}B_{-}}+B_{+}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}B_{+}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+B_{-}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-}B_{-}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t},\end{split}

then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)=&A_{+}B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}B_{-}}+A_{-}B_{+}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}B_{+}}+A_{+}B_{+}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}B_{+}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+A_{-}B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}B_{-}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t},\end{split}

and, eventually,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)=&A_{+}B_{+}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+A_{-}B_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}B_{-}^{*}}+A_{+}B_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}B_{-}^{*}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+A_{-}B_{+}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}B_{+}^{*}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t}.\end{split}

Meanwhile, the expansion associated with each wavenumber in the set
K
(the only ones contributing to
\boldsymbol{d}_{2}
), reads

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\left[\left(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}-\phi\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}\right]+O(\varepsilon^{3})=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\text{for}\quad\boldsymbol{k}\in K,

(60)

and remembering in particular that
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
. By contrast with the expansions for
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
in (
56
) and
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, we do not show the
O(\varepsilon^{3})
term in (
60
) as they do not play a role in what follows.

We now rewrite each expansion in (
60
) in integral form by applying
\left(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)^{-1}
, which amounts to inverting for each
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j,\boldsymbol{k}}(t)
, with
j=2,3.,..
. For reasons that will become clear, under the Ansatz (
49
), inverting for
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j,\boldsymbol{k}}(t)
and then collecting terms at each power of
\varepsilon
is asymptotically consistent, while doing the opposite, i.e., collecting terms first and then inverting, is not.

In the system (
36
), the Stokes equations are never differentiated with respect to time. Therefore, they act as algebraic constraints while the entire system evolves. Accordingly, we introduce the projector
\mathsfbi{P}_{S}
such that
\mathsfbi{P}_{S}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}(t)
automatically satisfies the Stokes equations in (
36
), even if the state vector
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}(t)
does not. In the remaining, we restrict the state vectors to evolve in the range of
\mathsfbi{P}_{S}
, meaning that the velocity and pressure are slaved to the moments for all time
t\geq 0
. Furthermore, the Stokes equations are such that, for given moments, there must correspond a unique velocity and pressure field.

Note that requiring
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}(t)\in\ran(\mathsfbi{P}_{S})
for
t\geq 0
constitutes a loss of generality, because (
36
) need not be true for
t=0
, and thus the initial condition need not be in the range of
\mathsfbi{P}_{S}
(i.e., it has no reason to be “compatible” with the equations). This loss of generality, however, is unimportant.

It is then useful to introduce the propagator
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, such that the operator
(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}})
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}(t)=\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,0)\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(0,t)\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}(t)\right),\quad\text{for all}\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}(t)\in\ran(\mathsfbi{P}_{S}),

(61)

where
\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,0)=\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,0)
and
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
acting in
\ran(\mathsfbi{P}_{S})
. In the absence of the singular mass matrix
\mathsfbi{H}
, the propagator
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,s)
would simply be the matrix exponential
e^{\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t-s)}
. The propagator obeys the semi-group composition properties
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,s)=\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,r)\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(r,s)
, and
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,t)=\mathsfbi{I}_{\ran(\mathsfbi{P}_{S})}
. The system
(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}})=\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}(t)
can be easily inverted for an arbitrary
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}(t)
using (
61
) by successively: (i) multiplying by
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(0,t)
, (ii) integrating between
0
and
t
, and (iii) multiplying by
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,0)
. This gives
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}(t)=\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,0)\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}(0)+\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,0)\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(0,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}(s)\mathrm{d}s
.

Crucially, the propagator yields the dyadic decomposition

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,0)(\bullet)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}_{j}\middle|(\bullet)\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}e^{\sigma_{j}t},

where we recall
\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
to be the set of
1+d+d^{2}
eigenmodes solving (
28
) with finite eigenvalues and for a given
\boldsymbol{k}
, and
\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
to be the set of their respective adjoints.
Since the inner product in the dyadic representation contains the mass matrix, a zero initial condition in
c
,
\boldsymbol{n}
, and
\mathsfbi{D}
, implies that the linear response is zero in all components for all
t\geq 0
regardless of the initial velocity or pressure. That is precisely because we have restricted the state vector to be within
\ran(\mathsfbi{P}_{S})
also at
t=0
. This implies that the initial condition must be compatible with the equation, and that the velocity and pressure adapt to the first three moments: if the latter are zero, then the former must be zero as well by inverting the Stokes equations, and the linear solution remains zero for all times. This is consistent with our numerical time-stepping procedure in Sec.
5.1
.

Rewriting the expansions in (
60
) in inverted/integral form results in

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varepsilon^{2}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}(t)-\phi\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,s)\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\right)+O(\varepsilon^{3})=\boldsymbol{0},\end{split}

(62)

for each
\boldsymbol{k}\in K
. Without loss of generality, we set
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}(0)=\boldsymbol{0}
when deriving (
62
), and the initial conditions of each higher-order field are similarly set to zero in the following. Equivalently, all transients will be ignored because they vanish over time, and only the statistically steady regime will be considered. The integral term
\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\mathrm{d}s
involves monomials in the amplitudes. For example, if
\boldsymbol{k}=2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\in K
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,s)\left(A_{+}A_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}+A_{+}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{+}}e^{2\text{i}\omega s}+A_{-}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}A_{-}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega s}\right)\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

(63)

where (
59
) was used. As a consequence of the amplitudes having an
O(\varepsilon^{2})
deterministic dynamics and being forced with a noise of amplitude
O(\varepsilon)
in (
49
), the integrals involving functions of the amplitudes can (and thus must) be expanded asymptotically by performing successive integration by parts. This procedure extracts the amplitudes out of the temporal integrals up to any order in
\varepsilon
. For example, isolating the first of the three integral terms in (
63
) and defining
\boldsymbol{h}(s)\coloneq\int_{0}^{s}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,x)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}\mathrm{d}x
, we develop

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}A_{+}A_{-}\underbrace{\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}}_{=\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{h}(s)/\mathrm{d}s}\mathrm{d}s\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny i.b.p.}}}}{{=}}}\left[A_{+}A_{-}\boldsymbol{h}\right]_{s=0}^{s=t}-\int_{0}^{t}(A_{+}\mathrm{d}_{s}A_{-}+A_{-}\mathrm{d}_{s}A_{+})\boldsymbol{h}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{+}(t)A_{-}(t)\boldsymbol{h}(t)-\varepsilon\phi\int_{0}^{t}(A_{+}\xi_{A_{-}}+A_{-}\xi_{A_{+}})\boldsymbol{h}\mathrm{d}s+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

\displaystyle\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny i.b.p.}}}}{{=}}}

\displaystyle A_{+}(t)A_{-}(t)\boldsymbol{h}(t)-\varepsilon\phi\left(A_{+}(t)\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{A_{-}}\boldsymbol{h}\mathrm{d}s+A_{-}(t)\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{A_{+}}\boldsymbol{h}\mathrm{d}s\right)+O(\varepsilon^{2}),

(64)

An integration by parts was performed between the first and second line, and (
49
) was used to express
\mathrm{d}_{t}A_{-}
and
\mathrm{d}_{t}A_{+}
. This resulted in the appearance of integral terms in
\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon^{2}
,
\varepsilon^{3}
, etc. In turn, the amplitudes and functions
f_{\Lambda_{j}}
can be extracted from these new integral terms by performing additional integration by parts, as was done between the second and third line.

Importantly, the term
\boldsymbol{h}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,x)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}\mathrm{d}x
is identified as the solution to
(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}})\boldsymbol{h}(t)=\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}
at time
t
. As we have seen in Sec.
3
, the linear system
2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
possesses only strictly stable eigenmodes. Therefore, we expect
\boldsymbol{h}(t)
to remain bounded for all times and, in particular, to converge to the time-independent vector
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}
, solving
-\mathsfbi{L}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}
. We further show in Appendix
10
that the integral terms at
O(\varepsilon)
in (
64
) also yield a bounded variance despite the presence of white noise in the integrand, and for this reason can rigorously be scaled asymptotically. Overall, under (
49
), the integral (
64
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}A_{+}A_{-}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}\mathrm{d}s=A_{+}A_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}+O(\varepsilon).\end{split}

Proceeding along the same lines of calculations for each of the other terms in (
63
) permits us to transform the expansion (
62
) into

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varepsilon^{2}\bigg(\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)-\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)-A_{+}A_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}-A_{+}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{+}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}-A_{-}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}A_{-}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t}\bigg)\\
&+O(\varepsilon^{3})=\boldsymbol{0},\end{split}

(65)

for
\boldsymbol{k}=2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
, and where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t,s)\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\quad\text{for}\quad\boldsymbol{k}\in K,\end{split}

is the linear response to the stochastic forcing
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)
(hence the superscript “
\phi
”). Although
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)
has a bounded root mean square because each
\boldsymbol{k}\in K
is strictly stable, it does not converge to any specific value in the long-time limit, and thus has to be kept time-dependent.

In (
65
), the higher-order terms produced by the integration by parts have been absorbed at
O(\varepsilon^{3})
. This is possible because the terms have not yet been collected. We now understand perhaps more clearly why it is asymptotically more consistent to first rewrite the expansions in (
60
) in inverted form and then collect the terms, rather than doing the opposite. Because rewriting an expansion in inverted form reveals that contributions to certain integral terms must be prioritized asymptotically. Therefore, they must appear in their respective orders. For instance, the integral term pre-multiplied by
\varepsilon
in (
64
) must contribute to
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{3,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)
, the one at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
must contribute to
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{4,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)
, etc… Inverting the system only after having collected the terms would force the contributions at each order to all be absorbed within
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)
.

Another reason to collect the terms only after rewriting the expansion in inverted/integral form is that the white noise forcings in expansions (
60
) have an infinite variance. However, as shown in Appendix
10
, their linear responses have finite variances. Thus, it is these latter responses that can be rigorously measured and collected accordingly at each power of
\varepsilon
.

Now collecting terms at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
in (
65
) gives the
2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
-component of the second-order solution

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)=\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)+A_{+}A_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}+A_{+}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{+}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+A_{-}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}A_{-}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t}.\end{split}

(66)

By applying for each of the others
\boldsymbol{k}\in K
the established calculation pattern, that is: (i) writing the expansion under inverted/integral form, then (ii) expanding the integral involving the amplitudes asymptotically by integration by parts, and (iii) prioritizing the terms thus produced at their respective orders and only then collecting the terms at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, we gather,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{q}_{2,\boldsymbol{0}}(t)=&|A_{+}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{+}^{*}}+|A_{-}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}A_{-}^{*}}+|B_{+}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+|B_{-}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}B_{-}^{*}}\\
&+\left(A_{+}A_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}A_{-}^{*}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+\text{c.c.}\right)+\left(B_{+}B_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}B_{-}^{*}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+\text{c.c.}\right),\end{split}

then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)=&\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)+B_{+}B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}B_{-}}+B_{+}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}B_{+}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}+B_{-}^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}B_{-}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t},\end{split}

(67)

followed by,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)=&\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)+A_{+}B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}B_{-}}+A_{-}B_{+}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}B_{+}}+A_{+}B_{+}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}B_{+}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+A_{-}B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}B_{-}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t},\end{split}

(68)

and, eventually,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)=&\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)+A_{+}B_{+}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+A_{-}B_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}B_{-}^{*}}+A_{+}B_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}B_{-}^{*}}e^{2\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+A_{-}B_{+}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}B_{+}^{*}}e^{-2\text{i}\omega t}.\end{split}

(69)

In the expressions above, each
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}
is time-independent and solves

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left[\text{i}(\omega_{\Lambda_{i}}+\omega_{\Lambda_{j}})\mathsfbi{H}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{i}}+\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{j}}}\right]\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}},\quad\text{where}\quad\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{i}}+\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{j}}\in K\end{split}

(70)

where
\omega_{\Lambda_{j}}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{j}}
are the frequency and wavenumber, respectively, of the eigenmode
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda_{j}}
pre-multiplied by the amplitude
\Lambda_{j}
, with
j=1,...,8
. For instance, if in (
70
) we choose
i=1
(recall that
\Lambda_{1}=A_{+}
) and
j=8
(recall that
\Lambda_{8}=B_{-}^{*}
), then
\omega_{\Lambda_{1}}=\omega
,
\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{1}}=\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
,
\omega_{\Lambda_{8}}=\omega
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{8}}=-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, such that
\left(2\text{i}\omega\mathsfbi{H}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}\right)\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}B_{-}^{*}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}B_{-}^{*}}
.

The linear systems in (
70
) are invertible only because
\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{i}}+\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{j}}\in K
, such that they are associated with strictly stable eigenvalues only. For the reasons mentioned in Sec.
3
(see (
117
) and the discussion below), the system for
\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{i}}+\boldsymbol{k}_{\Lambda_{j}}=\boldsymbol{0}
requires a special treatment before being invertible. In practice, it is solved in a projected space free of the velocity and pressure degrees of freedom, although it is also possible to solve for the pseudo-inverse in the full space using the command
pinv
of
Matlab
.

All the wavenumbers not contained in the set
K\cup\left\{\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{A},\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\right\}
are only excited by the stochastic forcing at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, such that

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}\left[\left(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right)\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}-\phi\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right]+O(\varepsilon^{3})=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\text{for}\quad\boldsymbol{k}\notin K\cup\left\{\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{A},\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\right\},

(71)

which gives
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}(t)=\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}}^{\phi}(t)
.

Now that we have determined the second-order corrections for all wavenumbers except
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, let us focus on the expansions for these two latter wavenumbers. In the expansion for
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
, given in (
54
), again applying
(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}})^{-1}
and using the propagator formalism brings about

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varepsilon^{2}\bigg[\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)-\phi\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\mathrm{d}s+\phi\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\left(\xi_{A_{+}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}+\xi_{A_{-}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\right)\mathrm{d}s\bigg]\\
&+\varepsilon^{3}\bigg[\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\left(f_{A_{+}}-A_{+}+\phi\xi^{(2)}_{A_{+}}\right)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\left(f_{A_{-}}-A_{-}+\phi\xi^{(2)}_{A_{-}}\right)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\mathrm{d}s\bigg]+O(\varepsilon^{4})=\boldsymbol{0}.\end{split}

(72)

where
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
is the shortened symbol for
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
, and we have used that
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}=\boldsymbol{0}
. The temporal integrals at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
cannot be further expanded asymptotically by integrating by parts, because they do not involve the amplitudes. Therefore, it is legitimate to directly collect the term at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, leading to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)=\phi\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\mathrm{d}s-\phi\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\left(\xi_{A_{+}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}+\xi_{A_{-}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\right)\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(73)

By contrast with the cases treated above, the temporal integrals in (
73
) are problematic, for they produce diverging (or “secular”) terms. That is because the linear system at
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
possesses neutral eigenmodes. More precisely, the dyadic representation of the propagator
\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)(=\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,s))
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)(\bullet)=&\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|(\bullet)\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega(t-s)}+\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|(\bullet)\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega(t-s)}\\
&+\underbrace{\sum_{j\geq 3}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}_{j}\middle|(\bullet)\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}e^{\sigma_{j}(t-s)}}_{=\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{P}_{o}(\bullet)},\end{split}

(74)

where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
, such that all the eigenmodes involved in the sum from
j\geq 3
are strictly stable. Accordingly, let us define the projector operator

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{P}_{o}(\bullet)\coloneq\sum_{j\geq 1}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\dagger}_{j}\middle|(\bullet)\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}-\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|(\bullet)\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}-\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|(\bullet)\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}},

(75)

which removes the component of an arbitrary state vector along the neutral eigenmodes
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
, and with respect to the adjoint basis. This implies that
\mathsfbi{P}_{o}
is an oblique projector (hence the subscript “
o
”). While
\mathsfbi{P}_{o}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}=\mathsfbi{P}_{o}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, all the other, strictly stable eigenmodes remain untouched under the application of
\mathsfbi{P}_{o}
, i.e.,
\mathsfbi{P}_{o}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}
, for
j\geq 3
, since by bi-orthogonality
\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}\right\rangle=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}\right\rangle=0
for
j\geq 3
.

In other words, (
74
) says the action of the propagator can be decomposed as a non-decaying part contained in the neutral (slow) eigen-subspace, spanned by
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
, and a part
\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{P}_{o}(\bullet)(=\mathsfbi{P}_{o}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)(\bullet))
contained in the strictly stable (fast) eigen-subspace, and whose contribution thus decays exponentially with time
t
.

Expression (
74
) can then be used in (
73
) to show that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)=&\phi e^{\text{i}\omega t}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(s)\right\rangle e^{-\text{i}\omega s}-\xi_{A_{+}}(s)\mathrm{d}s}_{\text{root mean square diverging $\propto\sqrt{t}$}}\\
&+\phi e^{-\text{i}\omega t}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(s)\right\rangle e^{\text{i}\omega s}-\xi_{A_{-}}(s)\mathrm{d}s}_{\text{root mean square diverging $\propto\sqrt{t}$}}\\
&+\phi\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{P}_{o}\left(\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(s)-\xi_{A_{+}}(s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}-\xi_{A_{-}}(s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\right)\mathrm{d}s}_{\text{remains bounded as evolving in the strictly stable (fast) eigen-subspace}}.\end{split}

(76)

where the bi-orthogonality property
\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}\right\rangle=0
was again used. The integral term containing
\mathsfbi{P}_{o}
evolves into the strictly stable eigen-subspace. For that reason, it remains bounded and poses no threat to the asymptotic hierarchy. However, the two other terms, proportional to
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
or
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
, respectively, may be problematic. Indeed, say that
\xi_{A_{+}}=\xi_{A_{-}}=0
. Then, the two terms proportional to
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
or
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
integrate white noise processes without any stabilizing effect. Therefore, they are Wiener processes, whose root mean square
\propto\sqrt{t}
diverges with time. This can easily be shown using the Itô isometry. This ruins the asymptotic hierarchy (at least in the root mean square sense), as it implies in particular that
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)
should in fact be scaled at
O(\varepsilon)
after a time
t\sim 1/\varepsilon^{2}
. Consequently, to preserve the asymptotics, the processes
\xi_{A_{+}}
and
\xi_{A_{-}}
are chosen in such a way as to cancel the associated integrands in (
76
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi_{A_{+}}(t)&\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)\right\rangle e^{-\text{i}\omega t},\quad\xi_{A_{-}}(t)\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)\right\rangle e^{\text{i}\omega t}.\end{split}

(77)

This way, the two integral terms proportional to
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
or
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
vanish for all times, and
\xi_{A_{+}}
and
\xi_{A_{-}}
are white noise indeed, consistently with our Ansatz in (
49
).

Note that, to preserve the asymptotic for all times larger than
t\sim 1/\varepsilon^{2}
, it would be sufficient to choose
\xi_{A_{+}}
and
\xi_{A_{-}}
to cancel out only the low frequencies
|\omega|\leq O(\varepsilon^{2})
of the integrand. In the latter case,
\xi_{A_{+}}
and
\xi_{A_{-}}
would be low-pass filtered versions of their expressions in (
77
). The choice made in (
77
) is thereby conservative, because it cancels the components along
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
for all frequencies. It has the advantage of avoiding introducing a non-specific low-pass filter.

By proceeding similarly for the expansion associated with the wavenumber
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi_{B_{+}}(t)&\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+},\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)\right\rangle e^{-\text{i}\omega t},\quad\xi_{B_{-}}(t)\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-},\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)\right\rangle e^{\text{i}\omega t}.\end{split}

(78)

By combining the results for all wavenumbers, we eventually obtain that the second-order correction in the state vector is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{q}_{2}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=&\boldsymbol{q}_{2,0}(t)+\Big(\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)e^{2\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)e^{2\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)e^{\text{i}(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}\\
&+\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)e^{\text{i}(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}\notin K}(\boldsymbol{x},t)+\text{c.c.}\Big),\end{split}

(79)

where the expression of each Fourier component was given above, and where we have regrouped all components
\boldsymbol{k}\notin K
in the terms
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}\notin K}
.
It follows from the relations (
42
) and (
44
) that, to the second-order correction
\boldsymbol{q}_{2}
of the state vector, must correspond a second-order correction
\psi_{2}
of the density of the same form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{2}=&\psi_{2,0}+\Big(\hat{\psi}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}e^{2\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\hat{\psi}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}e^{2\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\hat{\psi}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}e^{\text{i}(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}\\
&+\hat{\psi}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}e^{\text{i}(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}+\hat{\psi}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}\notin K}+\text{c.c.}\Big).\end{split}

(80)

Each Fourier component of the density also has the same detailed shape as its counterpart in the state vector. For example, it follows from (
66
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\psi}_{2,2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(\boldsymbol{p},t)=&\phi\hat{\psi}^{\phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(\boldsymbol{p},t)+A_{+}A_{-}\hat{\psi}^{A_{+}A_{-}}(\boldsymbol{p})+A_{+}^{2}\hat{\psi}^{A_{+}A_{+}}(\boldsymbol{p})e^{2\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+A_{-}^{2}\hat{\psi}^{A_{-}A_{-}}(\boldsymbol{p})e^{-2\text{i}\omega t},\end{split}

(81)

where each
\hat{\psi}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}
is such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\psi}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}(\boldsymbol{p})\coloneq\hat{\mathsfbi{B}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}:\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T}+\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{p}+\hat{w}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}+\hat{\chi}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}},\quad\text{and}\\
&\begin{bmatrix}\hat{w}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}\\
\underline{\hat{\mathsfbi{B}}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}\end{bmatrix}=\mathsfbi{M}^{-1}_{0}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\hat{c}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}\\
\hat{n}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}\\
\underline{\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}\hat{c}_{\hat{\chi}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}}\\
\hat{n}_{\hat{\chi}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}}\\
\underline{\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{\hat{\chi}^{\Lambda_{i}\Lambda_{j}}}}\end{bmatrix}\right).\end{split}

Returning to the expansion for
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
in (
72
), the nonlinearly-induced forcing term arising at third order,
\boldsymbol{d}_{3}
, given in (
57
), involves a quadratic interaction between the first- and second-order solutions
via
the operator
\mathsfbi{N}[\bullet,\bullet]
. Through the term
\boldsymbol{r}_{3}
, which arises from the moments of
\chi_{3}
in (
43
),
\boldsymbol{d}_{3}
also involves a cubic nonlinearity in the first-order density correction. Overall, the components of
\boldsymbol{d}_{3}
oscillating at
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
can only be produced via the interactions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}=\left\{\begin{matrix}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{0}\\
-\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\\
-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\\
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\\
\end{matrix}\right.,\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{k}_{B}=\left\{\begin{matrix}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{0}\\
-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+2\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\\
-\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}+\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\\
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-(\boldsymbol{k}_{A}-\boldsymbol{k}_{B})\\
\end{matrix}\right.,\end{split}

respectively. Therefore, in the second-order corrections (
79
) and (
80
), the terms with
\boldsymbol{k}\in K
are the only ones needed to compute
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}
. By contrast, the terms
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}\notin K}
and
\hat{\psi}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}\notin K}
have no importance. After long calculations, the forcing terms
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}
are found to be

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)=&\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)+\left(A_{-}B_{+}B_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{(1)}+A_{+}\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\Lambda_{j}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,j}^{(2)}\right)e^{\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+\left(A_{+}B_{+}^{*}B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}^{(1)}+A_{-}\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\Lambda_{j}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,j}^{(2)}\right)e^{-\text{i}\omega t}+N.R.T.,\end{split}

(82)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)=&\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)+\left(A_{+}A_{-}^{*}B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3}^{(1)}+B_{+}\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\Lambda_{j}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3,j}^{(2)}\right)e^{\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+\left(A_{+}^{*}A_{-}B_{+}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4}^{(1)}+B_{-}\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\Lambda_{j}|^{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4,j}^{(2)}\right)e^{-\text{i}\omega t}+N.R.T.,\end{split}

(83)

respectively. To lighten the notation, we have introduced the time-independent vectors
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{(1)}_{j}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{(2)}_{j,k}
, whose detailed expressions can be found in Appendix
14
. We have also defined the multiplicative stochastic processes
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}
, which result from the quadratic interactions between the first-order terms and the second-order ones of the form
\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}\in K}
, appearing in (
66
) and (
67
)-(
69
). The latter second-order terms are responses of the linearized system to the external noise, and thus
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}
involve a convolution integral over the history of the external noise, i.e., they are memory-dependent. Their detailed expressions are given in Appendix
13
. The acronym “N. R. T.” stands for “non-resonant” terms, meaning harmonic terms that do not oscillate at the neutral frequencies
\omega
or
-\omega
. We shall see in a moment that these “N. R. T” terms are unimportant, and thus their full expressions are not shown.

We insert (
82
) into the expansion (
72
), and, as previously developed, move the amplitudes (and their functions) outside of the temporal integrals by integrating by parts. For instance,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)f_{A_{+}}\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny i.b.p.}}}}{{=}}}f_{A_{+}}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{8}\frac{\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{j}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{\partial f_{A_{+}}}{\partial\Lambda_{j}}\right)\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&=f_{A_{+}}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+O(\varepsilon),\end{split}

by virtue of (
49
). All higher-order terms produced by integrating by parts are absorbed at
O(\varepsilon^{4})
in (
72
), and, by then collecting terms at
O(\varepsilon^{3})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)=&\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)+A_{+}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+A_{-}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&+A_{-}B_{+}B_{-}^{*}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{(1)}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+A_{+}\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\Lambda_{j}|^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,j}^{(2)}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&+A_{+}B_{+}^{*}B_{-}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}^{(1)}e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+A_{-}\sum_{j=1}^{4}|\Lambda_{j}|^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,j}^{(2)}e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&-f_{A_{+}}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s-f_{A_{-}}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+N.R.T.\end{split}

(84)

The term
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
stems from the multiplicative stochastic forcing
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
, as well as the the higher-order noise corrections
\xi^{(2)}_{A_{+}}
and
\xi^{(2)}_{A_{-}}
acting on the amplitudes. We show in Appendix
13
that the latter two processes are determined by canceling the components of
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
along
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
, respectively. This choice conservatively preserves the asymptotic hierarchy at all times, since these components typically yield a diverging root mean square
\propto\sqrt{t}
, resulting from the integration of stochastic processes with low-frequency content. This conservative argument to avoid a secular growth in the root mean square is similar to that already advanced below (
76
). The processes
\xi^{(2)}_{A_{+}}
and
\xi^{(2)}_{A_{-}}
are multiplicative, history-dependent, higher-order noise terms acting over the amplitudes, comparable to those derived in
75
and
5
.

Using the dyadic decomposition of
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
, given in (
74
), reveals that
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{3,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)
also contain terms that diverge algebraically
\propto te^{\pm\text{i}\omega t}
along the neutral eigenmodes. Unlike the previous order, however, the divergence this time is purely deterministic and holds not only in the root-mean-square sense. This ensues from the fact that, in the integrands of (
84
), the
e^{\pm\text{i}\omega s}
cancel out with the
e^{\mp\text{i}\omega s}
coming from the dyadic decomposition of the propagator. The resulting integrand is constant over time, and its integral is
\propto t
. For instance,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{(1)}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s=&\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{(1)}\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}te^{\text{i}\omega t}+\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{(1)}\right\rangle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}e^{-\text{i}\omega t}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{P}_{o}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{(1)}e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(85)

While the last two terms correspond to bounded integrals, the first is proportional to
te^{\text{i}\omega t}
and diverges with time. In particular, it should not be scaled at
O(\varepsilon^{3})
but at
O(\varepsilon)
as early as
t\sim 1/\varepsilon^{2}
; this contradicts the asymptotic hierarchy. Such a diverging response of a system forced at its neutral (or “natural”) frequencies is a well-known “resonance” effect. By decomposing each temporal integral in (
84
) in a similar fashion as above, we then exploit the freedom afforded by the deterministic functions
f_{A_{+}}
and
f_{A_{-}}
to cancel the terms diverging
\propto t
along
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
, respectively. In doing so, the asymptotic hierarchy is preserved. This non-resonance condition results in

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{A_{+}}&=A_{+}+\mu A_{+}|A_{+}|^{2}+\eta A_{+}|A_{-}|^{2}+\nu A_{+}\left(|B_{+}|^{2}+|B_{-}|^{2}\right)+\kappa A_{-}B_{+}B_{-}^{*},\\
f_{A_{-}}&=A_{-}+\mu^{*}A_{-}|A_{-}|^{2}+\eta^{*}A_{-}|A_{+}|^{2}+\nu^{*}A_{-}\left(|B_{+}|^{2}+|B_{-}|^{2}\right)+\kappa^{*}A_{+}B_{+}^{*}B_{-},\end{split}

(86)

where we have defined the complex-valued, scalar coefficients

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mu&\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,1}^{(2)}\right\rangle=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,2}^{(2)}\right\rangle^{*},\quad\eta\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,2}^{(2)}\right\rangle=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,1}^{(2)}\right\rangle^{*},\\
\nu&\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,3}^{(2)}\right\rangle=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,4}^{(2)}\right\rangle=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,3}^{(2)}\right\rangle^{*}=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,4}^{(2)}\right\rangle^{*},\\
\kappa&\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{(1)}\right\rangle=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}^{(1)}\right\rangle^{*}.\end{split}

From the expansion at the wavenumber
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, we obtain accordingly

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{B_{+}}&=B_{+}+\mu B_{+}|B_{+}|^{2}+\eta B_{+}|B_{-}|^{2}+\nu B_{+}\left(|A_{+}|^{2}+|A_{-}|^{2}\right)-\kappa A_{+}A_{-}^{*}B_{-},\ \text{and}\\
f_{B_{-}}&=B_{-}+\mu^{*}B_{-}|B_{-}|^{2}+\eta^{*}B_{-}|B_{+}|^{2}+\nu^{*}B_{-}\left(|A_{+}|^{2}+|A_{-}|^{2}\right)-\kappa^{*}A_{+}^{*}A_{-}B_{+}.\end{split}

(87)

The reduced-order system (
49
) for
A_{+}
,
A_{-}
,
B_{+}
, and
B_{-}
is now fully characterized. It is further possible to make it
\varepsilon
-free by using the rescaled time
\tau=\varepsilon^{2}t
, physically interpreted as a slow time scale, and using the scaling invariance of white noise, i.e.,
\xi_{\Lambda_{j}}(t)=\varepsilon\xi_{\Lambda_{j}}(\varepsilon^{2}t)
(and similarly for the
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{j}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)
terms given in Appendix
15
). By then only keeping the leading-order deterministic and stochastic terms, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\{\begin{matrix}\mathrm{d}_{\tau}A_{+}&=f_{A_{+}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})+\phi\xi_{A_{+}}(\tau),\\
\mathrm{d}_{\tau}A_{-}&=f_{A_{-}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})+\phi\xi_{A_{-}}(\tau),\\
\mathrm{d}_{\tau}B_{+}&=f_{B_{+}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})+\phi\xi_{B_{+}}(\tau),\\
\mathrm{d}_{\tau}B_{-}&=f_{B_{-}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})+\phi\xi_{B_{-}}(\tau),\end{matrix}\right.\end{split}

(88)

where
f_{A_{+}}
and
f_{A_{-}}
are given in (
86
), and
f_{B_{+}}
and
f_{B_{-}}
in (
87
). The white noises processes
\xi_{A_{+}}
and
\xi_{A_{-}}
derive from the spatio-temporal forcing
\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
applied to the full system, according to (
77
), and
\xi_{B_{+}}
and
\xi_{B_{-}}
according to (
78
). Note that
\xi_{A_{+}}
and
\xi_{A_{-}}
are generally correlated, and similarly for
\xi_{B_{+}}
and
\xi_{B_{-}}
; however, the former pair is uncorrelated with the latter pair because the noise is white in space.

The higher-order noise terms
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda}
only appear at
O(\varepsilon)
in (
88
), and thus are neglected. This may appear inconsistent since they are multiplied by
\varepsilon^{2}
in (
49
) in the same way that the terms
f_{\Lambda}
are. In addition, the terms
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda}
and
f_{\Lambda}
are all determined at the same
O(\varepsilon^{3})
. One way to understand this is to notice that the deterministic functions
f_{\Lambda}
cancel secular terms growing
\propto t
, whereas the stochastic processes
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda}
cancel terms growing
\propto\sqrt{t}
. Therefore, the former terms prevent secular terms from emerging at
O(\varepsilon)
after a time
\tau\sim 1
(and thus must appear in (
88
)), while the latter terms prevent secular terms from emerging at
O(\varepsilon)
after a much longer time,
\tau\sim 1/\varepsilon^{2}
. That is why
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda}
do not appear at the leading-order in (
88
), as it is written over
\tau
.

In the codimension-2 pitchfork bifurcation case, the frequency
\omega=0
, such that only one eigenmode per wavenumber bifurcates. Therefore, the system (
88
) reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\{\begin{matrix}\mathrm{d}_{\tau}A&=A+\mu A|A|^{2}+\nu A|B|^{2}+\phi\xi_{A}(\tau),\\
\mathrm{d}_{\tau}B&=B+\mu B|B|^{2}+\nu B|A|^{2}+\phi\xi_{B}(\tau),\end{matrix}\right.\end{split}

(89)

where we have renamed
A_{+}
as
A
and set
A_{-}
to zero. The reverse could as easily have been done (rename
A_{-}
as
A
and set
A_{+}
to zero) since the coefficients become real-valued as soon as
\omega=0
. We have also renamed
B_{+}
in
B
and set
B_{-}
to zero. The definitions of the coefficients and white noise processes remain the same as for the codimension-4 Hopf bifurcation, and
\omega
should simply be set to
0
in (
77
) and (
78
). This gives, in particular

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi_{A}(t)&\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A,\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)\right\rangle,\quad\xi_{B}(t)\coloneq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B,\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)\right\rangle.\end{split}

(90)

Note that the deterministic version of (
89
) was already derived in
51
.

Overall, the stochastic terms
\xi_{\Lambda_{j}}
,
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{j}}
,… in (
49
) are chosen so that each stochastic higher-order correction in the state vector is fully contained within the strictly stable eigen-subspace. This is a conservative way to avoid secular stochastic terms from emerging in the expansion. On the other hand, the deterministic terms
f_{\Lambda_{j}}
,
f^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{j}}
,… prevent deterministic secular growth while still allowing for the deterministic higher-order corrections to have (bounded) component in the neutral eigen-subspace (e.g., the second term in the right-hand-side in (
85
)). While the former approach to deriving the stochastic terms adopts the center-manifold perspective, the latter approach to deriving the deterministic terms adopts the normal-form or multiple-scale perspectives.

5
Fully nonlinear numerical methods

The predictions from the low-dimensional systems of amplitude equations, derived above, are compared with the results obtained by directly simulating the full system (
36
). We say a word on the direct numerical simulations (DNS) of (
36
) in Sec.
5.1
and briefly recall the working principle of the rare event Adaptive Multilevel Splitting (AMS) algorithm in Sec.
5.2
.

5.1
Direct numerical simulations

We denote
\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}\coloneq(c,\boldsymbol{n},\operatorname{vec}\left(\mathsfbi{D}\right))^{T}
the reduced state vector, free of the velocity and pressure components. System (
36
) can be written in compact form as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}}{\partial t}=D_{T}\Delta\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}+\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}},\boldsymbol{u})+F\boldsymbol{f},\end{split}

(91)

where
\boldsymbol{g}
includes the nonlinear terms in (
36
). We evolve (
91
) by proceeding as follows

1.

From known
\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}^{(n)}
at the
n
th time step, the corresponding velocity
\boldsymbol{u}^{(n)}
and pressure
\varpi^{(n)}
can be found by inverting the linear Stokes equations, which is done in the Fourier space.

2.

From
\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}^{(n)}
,
\boldsymbol{u}^{(n)}
, and
\varpi^{(n)}
(and the same fields at
n-1
),
\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}^{(n+1)}
is computed by using the stochastic generalization of the SBDF2 time-stepper (Itô convention). It is implicit in the Laplacian term in (
91
), and explicit in
\boldsymbol{g}
and the stochastic forcing terms (although the latter two are discretized differently), and is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left(\frac{3}{2\mathrm{d}t}\mathsfbi{I}-D_{T}\Delta\right)\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}^{(n+1)}=&\frac{4\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}^{(n)}-\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}^{(n-1)}}{2\mathrm{d}t}+2\boldsymbol{g}^{(n)}-\boldsymbol{g}^{(n-1)}+F\frac{\boldsymbol{W}^{(n+1)}}{\sqrt{\mathrm{d}t}\sqrt{(\mathrm{d}x)^{2}}},\end{split}

(92)

where
\boldsymbol{g}^{(n)}\coloneq\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{q}_{\text{m}}^{(n)},\boldsymbol{u}^{(n)})
and
\mathrm{d}t
is the time increment. We use
N
spatial discretisation points, uniformly spaced by
\mathrm{d}x
along each spatial dimension. The exponent of two in
\sqrt{(\mathrm{d}x)^{2}}
arises because the noise is white in two spatial dimensions. Each component of the vector
\boldsymbol{W}=\left(W_{c},\boldsymbol{W}_{n},\operatorname{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{W}_{D}\right)\right)^{T}
is a Gaussian-distributed random variable with zero mean and finite variance, which can be deduced from Table
1
. More precisely, at each time step, the components of
\boldsymbol{W}
are drawn randomly according to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&W_{c}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,2\upi),\quad W_{n,x}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\upi),\quad W_{n,y}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\upi)\\
&W_{D,{xx}}=\frac{W_{c}}{2}+Y,\ \text{with}\ Y\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\upi}{4}\right),\quad W_{D,{xy}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\upi}{4}\right),\end{split}

(93)

as well as
W_{D,{yx}}=W_{D,{xy}}
and
W_{D,{yy}}=W_{c}-W_{D,xx}
. The random variables in (
93
) are drawn independently, but, importantly,
W_{D,{xx}}
depends on
W_{c}
. Any spatial average is removed from each field at each time step.

System (
92
) is also solved in Fourier space. Since it requires the two previous time steps, it is replaced by the semi-implicit Euler scheme for
n=1
. We refer to
18
for different examples of integrators for stochastic PDEs.

3.

Iterate to
1.

After computing a fully nonlinear trajectory,
\boldsymbol{q}^{\text{DNS}}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
, the amplitudes
A_{+}
,
A_{-}
,
B_{+}
, and
B_{-}
, or
A
and
B
, are extracted for comparison with the weakly nonlinear predictions. The first step is to extract the Fourier component oscillating at wavenumber
\boldsymbol{k}
, i.e.,
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\text{DNS}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)
, with
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
and
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
.
Each
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\text{DNS}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is further decomposed into the eigenbasis at the corresponding wavenumber,
\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}\right\}_{j\geq 1}
, obtained by solving (
28
). This gives
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\text{DNS}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)=\sum_{j}a_{j}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}
, with
a_{j}(t)
the amplitude along the eigenmode
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}_{j}
. Eventually, by using the bi-orthogonality property (
31
) between the direct and adjoint eigenbasis, it follows that the amplitudes along the bifurcating eigenmodes are

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Lambda^{\text{DNS}}(t)=\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda,\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\text{DNS}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)\right\rangle\quad\text{where}\quad\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{A}&\text{if}\;\Lambda\in\left\{A,A_{+},A_{-}\right\}\\
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{B}&\text{if}\;\Lambda\in\left\{B,B_{+},B_{-}\right\}.\\
\end{cases}\end{split}

(94)

5.2
The Adaptive Multilevel Splitting (AMS) algorithm

We now briefly recall the working principle of the Adaptive Multilevel Splitting algorithm, which we employ to determine rare events statistics. We closely follow the procedure originally developed in
11
. We also refer to
32
, Section 2(b), for a complete description of the algorithm.

5.2.1
Mean transition time

Two slightly different versions of the algorithm are implemented. The first, described in this subsection, aims to estimate the “mean transition time”. It is the average time for a trajectory, initiated within a given basin of attraction of the system, to reach a distinct basin. This version of the AMS algorithm was applied to the Ginzburg-Landau partial differential equation in
56
, and shown to accurately compute, as compared to analytical results, the mean transition time between two attractors. It was also successful in computing the transition path in the dynamics of multistable turbulent jets
(
7
)
or turbulent plane shear flows
(
32
;
58
)
.

To start, let us define
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
as two non-overlapping regions in the phase space of the system. Each region is contained within a distinct (and deterministic) basin of attraction and corresponds to a particular stable state of interest. The goal of the AMS algorithm is to produce a large number of “reactive” trajectories, initiated within
\mathcal{A}
and ending within
\mathcal{B}
. Thereby, each trajectory is associated with a noise-induced transition.

For this, the first and perhaps the most sensitive step is to construct a scalar real-valued “cost function”
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}](t)
that, for each time
t
, quantifies how close
\boldsymbol{q}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
is to
\mathcal{B}
. Specifically,
\varphi
is taken so that there exist two real scalars
h_{\mathcal{A}}
and
h_{\mathcal{B}}
, with
h_{\mathcal{A}}<h_{\mathcal{B}}
, such that
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}]<h_{\mathcal{A}}
implies
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}]>h_{\mathcal{B}}
implies
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{B}
.
Note that the isosurface
\varphi=h_{\mathcal{A}}
need not coincide with the boundary of
\mathcal{A}
, nor does
\varphi=h_{\mathcal{B}}
with the boundary of
\mathcal{B}
. The regions
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are characterized prior to the cost function. The latter is chosen in consequence so that its sublevel set
\left\{\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}]<h_{\mathcal{A}}\right\}
lies within
\mathcal{A}
, and its superlevel set
\left\{\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}]>h_{\mathcal{B}}\right\}
lies within
\mathcal{B}
. Larger values of
\varphi
are intended to indicate a greater progress of a trajectory toward
\mathcal{B}
.
We also require
\varphi
to have a non-zero gradient, at least in the region of interest. The algorithm also requires a scalar
h_{\mathcal{S}}
, associated with an isosurface
\mathcal{S}
, such that
h_{\mathcal{A}}<h_{\mathcal{S}}<h_{\mathcal{B}}
but
h_{\mathcal{S}}
is chosen close to
h_{\mathcal{A}}
(i.e.,
\mathcal{S}
is closely enclosing
\mathcal{A}
). We refer to
32
, figure
4
therein for an illustration (we purposefully use the same notation). For high-dimensional systems, the cost function
\varphi
is usually constructed based on heuristic considerations, since the theoretical method for constructing involves computing the committor functions between the attractors, which quickly becomes impractical.

The AMS algorithm then proceeds as follows:

1.

The initialization step requires computing
N_{t}
stochastic trajectories, i.e.,
\left\{\boldsymbol{q}_{j}(\boldsymbol{x},t)\right\}_{1\leq j\leq N_{t}}
. Each trajectory is initiated randomly within
\mathcal{A}
and is computed until
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}_{j}](t)>h_{\mathcal{B}}
, or, much more likely, until
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}_{j}](t)<h_{\mathcal{A}}
after it has accomplished
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}_{j}](t)>h_{\mathcal{S}}
(i.e., until it re-enters
\mathcal{A}
after going as far as
h_{\mathcal{S}}
). Therefore, the initial
N_{t}
trajectories all have generally different final times. The latter are generally not too long since
h_{\mathcal{S}}
is close to
h_{\mathcal{A}}
. Note that each trajectory is computed using a different noise realization.

The algorithm requires storing all trajectories, which can lead to excessive CPU and memory usage. To mitigate this effect, we perform a two-dimensional spatial Fourier transform of each trajectory and store only the first
12
Fourier modes in each direction. That is, we only save a low-pass spatially filtered version of the trajectories. We have checked that the final results rapidly converge with the number of saved Fourier modes, since, as seen in Sec.
3
, modes with increasing
||\boldsymbol{k}||>1
are increasingly damped by the system.

2.

At the
n
th iteration, the maximum value reached by the cost function along the
j
th trajectory, say
\varphi^{(n)}_{j}
, is determined. These maximum values are then used to sort the trajectories, from the lowest ones (i.e., the trajectory that has made the smallest excursion towards
h_{\mathcal{B}}
) to the largest (i.e., the trajectory that has made the largest excursion). Namely, the trajectories at iteration
n
are re-indexed such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi^{(n)}_{1}\leq\varphi^{(n)}_{2}\leq...\leq\varphi^{(n)}_{K}\leq...\leq\varphi^{(n)}_{N_{t}}.\end{split}

The idea is then to discard all trajectories with indices from
j=1
to
j=K
(included), corresponding to the
K
trajectories whose maximal value of the cost function is the lowest. We note that these maxima are possibly equal, and thus in practice
K=K^{(n)}
depends on the iteration index. For instance, if we choose
a priori

K=1
but
\varphi^{(n)}_{1}=\varphi^{(n)}_{2}<\varphi^{(n)}_{3}\leq...
, then these are the first two trajectories that must be discarded, and
K^{(n)}=2
. All remaining trajectories, with index
j\in\left\{K^{(n)}+1,...,N_{t}\right\}
are retained.

A new trajectory replaces each of the discarded ones. A new trajectory is constructed to be equal to one of the retained trajectories, with index
l
chosen at random in the retained set
\left\{K^{(n)}+1,...,N_{t}\right\}
, from the initial time
t=0
and until the time
t^{\text{clone}}
where
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}_{l}](t^{\text{clone}})=\varphi^{(n)}_{K}
. The remainder of the new trajectory from
\boldsymbol{q}_{l}(\boldsymbol{x},t^{\text{clone}})
, which serves as an “initial” condition for the time stepper, is then computed by using a new noise realization, until it has reached either
\mathcal{A}
or
\mathcal{B}
.

Replacing the discarded
K^{(n)}
trajectories accordingly, yields a new set of trajectories such that the maximum cost function value for each index
j
is greater than or equal to
\varphi^{(n)}_{K}
, and thus greater than or equal to that of the previous set. In that sense, the new set of trajectories has made more progress towards
\mathcal{B}
, at least as measured by
\varphi
, than the previous set. The value of
n
is incremented to
n+1
, and this step is repeated as many times as necessary until the stopping condition of the algorithm is met.

3.

The iterations stop as soon as all trajectories reach
\mathcal{B}
, i.e., as soon as
\varphi^{(n)}_{1}>h_{\mathcal{B}}
. We have thus computed a large number
N_{t}
of reactive trajectories from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, which can be used to generate converged statistics on the
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
noise-induced transition. In particular, the probability that a trajectory initially on the surface
\mathcal{S}
ends up in
\mathcal{B}
is estimated by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{p}\coloneq\prod_{n=1}^{M}\left(1-\frac{K^{(n)}}{N_{t}}\right),\end{split}

(95)

where
M
is the total number of iterations needed for the algorithm to reach its stopping condition. Accordingly, the probability that a trajectory goes from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathcal{A}
is
1-\hat{p}
, and, by construction of the initial set, the probability that it goes from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{S}
is
1
. From this initialization step, it is possible to estimate
\overline{T}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{S}}
, the average duration of a trajectory conditioned to start within
\mathcal{A}
and to end on the surface
\mathcal{S}
. Similarly, an estimate for
\overline{T}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{A}}
, the average duration of a trajectory conditioned to start on
\mathcal{S}
and to end by re-entering
\mathcal{A}
can also be computed from the initial set of trajectories. Because
\mathcal{S}
is chosen close to
\mathcal{A}
, neither
\overline{T}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{S}}
nor
\overline{T}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{A}}
are expected to be large. From the converged set of
N_{t}
reactive trajectories, we obtain an estimate for
\overline{T}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}}
, the average duration of a trajectory conditioned to start on
\mathcal{S}
and to end by entering
\mathcal{B}
. Although such a trajectory has a very low probability
\hat{p}\ll 1
of occurring, the conditioned time
\overline{T}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}}
need not be large either. From all these quantities, and by describing the
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
transition dynamics as a Markov chain (see figure
5
in
32
), follows an estimate for the mean transition time
T
from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, as

\displaystyle\begin{split}T\coloneq\left(\overline{T}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{S}}+\overline{T}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{A}}\right)\frac{1-\hat{p}}{\hat{p}}+\left(\overline{T}_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{S}}+\overline{T}_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}}\right)\end{split}

This time is not conditioned on the trajectory starting in
\mathcal{A}
and ending in
\mathcal{B}
. It really is an estimate of the average time required for a trajectory to reach
\mathcal{B}
while started within
\mathcal{A}
, and as a consequence of rare external fluctuations. Because
\hat{p}
is very small in the weak forcing limit considered in this article,
T
is expected to be extremely large.

5.2.2
Mean return time

The second version of the AMS algorithm we employ does not require multi-stability. It is used to estimate the average waiting time until a scalar observable of the trajectory exceeds a given threshold in a statistically steady regime. Threshold values of interest are typically much larger than the standard deviation. This average waiting time is called the “mean return time” in the rest of the article. This version of the algorithm was presented in
42
and used in
41
to compute the mean return time for the drag force acting on a square, under the action of an impinging turbulent flow, to become extremely large.

The working principle of this version of the AMS algorithm is similar to that used to estimate the mean transition time. However, because it does not seek to characterize the transition between different basins of attraction, it is here unnecessary to define
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{B}
, and related thresholds
h_{\mathcal{A}}
,
h_{\mathcal{S}}
and
h_{\mathcal{B}}
.

For a stochastic trajectory
\boldsymbol{q}(\boldsymbol{x},t)
in a statistically steady regime, the algorithm estimates the mean return time of the event
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}](t)\geq 1
. The cost function
\varphi
here represents the observable of interest, and we scale it so that the exceedance threshold is always
1
. The algorithm inherently assumes that the rare event of interest follows a Poisson process. This holds if
1
is much larger than the standard deviation of the observable, making the exceedance events independent. Consequently, the mean return times can be obtained from the probability
\tilde{p}
of observing the event over a period of time
t_{\text{max}}
. The time
t_{\text{max}}
must be much larger than the typical correlation time (
\tau\sim 1
, i.e.,
t\sim 1/\varepsilon^{2}
) of the system, but, crucially, can be shorter than the mean return time.

The probability
\tilde{p}
is obtained by using the same killing-and-cloning iterative procedure over the set of
N_{t}
trajectories, as presented above. One difference is that, at each iteration, the
N_{t}
trajectories all have the same maximum duration
t_{\text{max}}
. The trajectories are then prioritized based on the maximum value reached by
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}](t)
between
t=0
and
t=t_{\text{max}}
. Within this interval, the computation of a trajectory is stopped as soon as
\varphi[\boldsymbol{q}](t)\geq 1
, and the exceedance threshold
1
thus plays the role of
h_{\mathcal{B}}
in the previous version of the algorithm. Iterations stop as soon as all
N_{t}
trajectories have reached the exceedance threshold. If
M
again denotes the final number of iterations, the probability
\tilde{p}
is the same expression as in (
95
). Because of the Poisson process approximation, the mean return time, say
r
, ensues from
\tilde{p}
as
r=-t_{\text{max}}/\ln(1-\tilde{p})
.

For the two implemented versions of the algorithm, the final estimate of the mean transition time
T
, or return time
r
, is itself a random variable. Thereby,
N_{\text{re}}>1
realizations of the algorithm are performed, and the resulting estimates are ensemble-averaged. In simple cases, the associated standard deviation can be shown to scale as
1/\sqrt{KN_{t}}
. Thus, a larger
N_{t}
and/or a smaller
K
result in a more reliable average estimate, although coming at a higher computational cost. The rate of convergence of the average estimate was also shown in
9
;
8
to depend on the choice of the function
\varphi
(see figure
4
in
8
).

6
Results in the pitchfork bifurcation region

In this section, we present our results for parameters corresponding to the codimension-2 pitchfork bifurcation. Both the weakly (low-dimensional) and fully (high-dimensional) nonlinear approaches are systematically compared. First, in Sec.
6.1
, we report our results in the purely deterministic regime, followed by comparison of some steady statistics in Sec.
6.2
, and eventually dynamical statistics in Sec.
6.3
.

While we also study rare events in this section, readers specifically interested in noise-induced transitions between two distinct collective states are invited to jump directly to Sec.
7
.

6.1
Deterministic results (
\phi=0
)

The deterministic regime is recovered by setting
\phi=0
. The system (
89
) of amplitude equations then predicts the trajectories to converge to the unique attractor in the magnitude phase space, given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}(|A|,|B|)=(H_{e},H_{e}),\quad\text{where}\quad H_{e}\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{-(\mu+\nu)}},\end{split}

(96)

and arbitrary phases for both
A
and
B
(independently). Indeed, the unforced version of (
89
) remains invariant under a phase-shift of
A
and
B
. For the stable equilibria in (
96
) to exist, implying that the bifurcation is “supercritical”, the sum
\mu+\nu
must be negative. In the opposite case where the sum
\mu+\nu
is strictly positive,
H_{e}
is not defined, and the weakly nonlinear expansion needs to be pursued at higher orders. The bifurcation is then said to be “subcritical”.

Throughout the article, the coefficients are evaluated numerically from the above calculations and are not fitted to DNS data. Recall that, in the pitchfork bifurcation scenario, the coefficients
\mu
and
\nu
are real-valued. Their values depend on the chosen normalization of the eigenmodes, and the values reported below are relative to the choice made in (
33
).

The weakly nonlinear coefficients are shown individually in figure
2(a)
, for three different
D_{R}
and rescaled by the corresponding value of
D_{R}
. Their (non-rescaled) sums are shown in figure
2(b)
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 2
:
Weakly nonlinear coefficients
\mu
(dash-dotted lines) and
\nu
(dashed lines) as a function of
\beta
, the swimming speed of the particles. Three different values of
D_{R}\in\left\{10^{-4},10^{-3},0.02\right\}
are considered, each corresponding to a different color (darker shades larger
D_{R}
).
Only the range of
\beta
for which the bifurcation is of pitchfork type (i.e., for which
\omega=0
in figure
1(b)
) is considered, and delimited by a thin vertical line (increasing
\beta
above which makes the bifurcation of Hopf-type).
In (a), the coefficients are plotted individually and rescaled by the corresponding
D_{R}
, whereas their sum (without rescaling) is shown in (b).

The curves are plotted as a function of the swimming speed, from
\beta=0
up to the threshold value at which the bifurcation becomes of the Hopf type.
For the three
D_{R}
considered, the coefficient
\mu
is always negative and dominates over
\nu
, which is always positive. Consequently,
H_{e}
always exists, and thus the pitchfork bifurcation is always supercritical. Decreasing
D_{R}
even more does not change this fact, as the coefficients appear to have already converged between
D_{R}=10^{-3}
and
10^{-4}
.
Overall, changing
D_{R}
has little effect on the sum
\nu+\mu
, and thus on
H_{e}
, but mostly modifies the threshold at which the bifurcation changes from pitchfork to Hopf (i.e., the vertical lines in figure
2
). Interestingly, as
\beta
closely approaches this threshold, the coefficients undergo abrupt variations. This manifests as
H_{e}
becoming suddenly smaller as
\beta
is increased.

D_{R}=0.02
, and consider only the two values of the swimming speed
\beta=0
and
\beta=0.15
. The corresponding coefficients are given in Table

) (with

.

) (with

).

|A|=|B|=H_{e}
. Since
H_{e}
is a constant,
\varepsilon|A|=\sqrt{D_{T,c}-D_{T}}H_{e}
evolves as a square root when plotted against
D_{T}
. In the fully nonlinear method, DNS of the system (
36
) are run from different initial conditions and until reaching a steady state, and the corresponding amplitudes
A
and
B
are then extracted following (
94
).

In figure

, we found a unique stable fixed point in the DNS for all the
D_{T}
considered (as predicted by the weakly nonlinear approach). The comparison between the two approaches, for both the immotile case at
\beta=0
in figure
3(a)
, and the motile case at
\beta=0.15
in figure
3(b)
, yields convincing results. This validates our calculations of the weakly nonlinear coefficients. As expected, the agreement progressively degrades as
D_{T}
decreases, since it corresponds to larger
\varepsilon
and thus the neglected terms in the weakly nonlinear expansion become important.

|A|
and
|B|
, the leading-order weakly nonlinear equilibrium solution can be reconstructed from (
48
). The results are shown in figure

.

). Left frame: vorticity (colormap) with arrows for the velocity. Middle frame (only for

): polarization vector field with the colormap for the norm. Right frame: nematic order parameter, given by the leading eigenvalue of

(colormap), and representing the strength of particle local alignment; the associated eigenvector is the principal direction of particle alignment (short straight lines).
