.

For the immotile case (top row), only the velocity and the nematic order parameter (i.e., the strength of particle alignment) are nonzero. The superposition of the two bifurcating eigenmodes, one oscillating purely along
x
and the other purely along
y
, results in a macroscopic velocity vortex in the left frame. On the frame on the right, the nematic order parameter assumes its maximum values, i.e., the particles exhibit a clear preferred orientation, wherever the vorticity is zero. For the motile case at
\beta=0.15
, the velocity and nematic order parameter are qualitatively similar to those for the immotile case. The motility makes the polarization vector nonzero, and it coincides with the velocity field.

6.2
Stochastic results (
\phi\neq 0
): steady statistics

We now enable stochastic forcing, i.e.,
\phi\neq 0
, and compare the trajectories and probability distributions in a statistically steady regime.

Since the stochastic forcing applied to the original system is white in space, the processes
\xi_{A}
and
\xi_{B}
, expressed in (
90
) and acting on the amplitudes, are complex-valued and uncorrelated with each other (since they refer to distinct wavenumbers). For each process, the real and imaginary parts are also uncorrelated. Furthermore, they are white in time and Gaussian-distributed with zero average. This follows from the fact that all the components in
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
are white in time. In addition, because components of
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
are uncorrelated at nonzero time lag (see Table
1
), the projection of
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
onto a constant vector must also be white noise. More precisely, if
\boldsymbol{\xi}\doteq(\xi_{A},\xi_{B})^{T}
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\right]=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\boldsymbol{\xi}^{T}(t^{\prime})\right]=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\xi}(t)\boldsymbol{\xi}^{T,*}(t^{\prime})\right]=2\alpha^{2}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\mathsfbi{I},\end{split}

(97)

where we have defined the scalar
\alpha
such that
\alpha^{2}
is the noise intensity of both the real and imaginary parts of both
\xi_{A}
and
\xi_{B}
. We show in Appendix
15
that the value of
\alpha
follows directly from (
90
). Numerical values of
\alpha
are given in Table.
2
.

Rewriting
A=|A|e^{\text{i}\psi_{A}}
and
B=|B|e^{\text{i}\psi_{B}}
, it is easy to show that the equations for the magnitudes,
|A|
and
|B|
, are decoupled from those for the phases,
\psi_{A}
and
\psi_{B}
, such that (
89
) can be reduced to a
2
-dof system. Specifically, using Itô’s Lemma, the magnitudes obey the equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{H}(\tau)}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}V(\boldsymbol{H}(\tau))+\phi\alpha\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\tau),\quad\text{with}\\
&\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\tau)\right]=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\zeta}(\tau)\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{T}(\tau^{\prime})\right]=\delta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\mathsfbi{I},\end{split}

(98)

and where we have defined
\boldsymbol{H}(\tau)\coloneq(|A|,|B|)^{T}(\tau)
. The deterministic part of the right-hand side derives from the potential
V
, defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}V(\boldsymbol{H})\coloneq-\frac{1}{2}\left(|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}\right)-\frac{\mu}{4}\left(|A|^{4}+|B|^{4}\right)-\frac{\nu}{2}|A|^{2}|B|^{2}-\frac{(\alpha\phi)^{2}}{2}\left(\ln{|A|}+\ln{|B|}\right).\end{split}

(99)

We note the presence of logarithmic, infinitely high barriers of potential along the axes
|A|=0
and
|B|=0
, which prevent
|A|
and
|B|
from becoming negative. In (
98
), we imply
\boldsymbol{\nabla}=(\partial_{|A|},\partial_{|B|})^{T}
.

Each magnitude is independent of its associated phase, but the converse is not true. Indeed, the equations for the phases read

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}\psi_{A}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=\frac{\phi\alpha}{|A|}\zeta_{\psi_{A}}(\tau),\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}\psi_{B}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=\frac{\phi\alpha}{|B|}\zeta_{\psi_{B}}(\tau),\end{split}

(100)

where, again,
\zeta_{\psi_{\Lambda}}
is a Gaussian white noise with zero average and unit intensity (the symbol
\Lambda
denoting either
A
or
B
indistinguishably). Importantly, the amplitude of the noise acting on the phase is inversely proportional to the magnitude. This is a simple geometric effect resulting from the fact that a given increment in the trajectory, due to the noise
\zeta_{\psi_{\Lambda}}
, corresponds to a larger increment in the phase if the trajectory is closer to the origin at
|\Lambda|=0
. Thus, the effective noise on the phase is proportional to
\zeta_{\psi_{\Lambda}}/|\Lambda|
. When the trajectory is exactly at the origin (which occurs with probability zero because the potential diverges there), the phase increment diverges.

Figure
5
shows some fully nonlinear trajectories in the
(\varepsilon|A|,\varepsilon|B|)
plane for a fixed
\varepsilon^{2}=0.005
.

(a)

\beta=0

(b)

\beta=0.15

Figure 5
:
For
\varepsilon^{2}=0.005
,
\phi=1.41
, and each frame corresponding to different values of
\beta
.
A DNS trajectory of length
t=10^{4}
(
\varepsilon A
and
\varepsilon B
extracted according to (
94
)) is shown in the
\varepsilon(|A|,|B|)
phase space with transparent red dots (each corresponds to a different time along the trajectory).
Forty isolines of the weakly nonlinear potential (
99
) are also included, starting at the potential minimum (circle marker).
The plus marker highlights the deterministic attractor (
96
). All frames have the same
x
and
y
axis range.

As explained in Sec.
5.1
, they have been produced by running long DNS of (
36
), and then extracting the component along the eigenmodes
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B}
according to (
94
). For comparison, we also display some isolines of the WNL potential (
99
). The latter has a unique global minimum, from which it increases monotonically in all directions. In particular, it increases as both
|A|
and
|B|
approach zero, because the uniform base state is unstable. The thin boundary layer where the potential diverges logarithmically at
|A|=0
and
|B|=0
is also visible.

DNS trajectories consistently spend most of their time around the minimum of the WNL potential (where they are initiated). The farther the trajectories depart from the minimum, the rarer the excursions become (i.e., the points are plotted with increasing transparency), especially in the direction where the potential growth is the sharpest.

Increasing the swimming speed from
\beta=0
to
\beta=0.15
between the left and right panels, while keeping
\phi
fixed, causes the DNS trajectories to explore a larger region of the phase space. In other words, although the amplitude of the externally applied forcing is the same
(=\phi)
in both cases, the motile configuration appears to experience a greater “effective” forcing, or, equivalently, to be more “receptive” to the forcing. This is well captured by the weakly nonlinear system (
98
). Here, the amplitude of the stochastic forcing acting directly on the magnitudes is found to be
\alpha\phi
, which is
\alpha
times larger than that of the applied forcing. In Table.
2
, the pre-factor
\alpha
is found to be
\approx 1.78
times larger for
\beta=0.15
than for
\beta=0
, and thus captures the reported behavior.

We explain the largest receptivity of the system with motile particles, as compared to that for immotile ones, as follows. The polarization field becomes nonzero as soon as
\beta
does, and direct and adjoint eigenmodes inherently have polarization fields advected in opposite directions by the velocity field. For fixed norms of the direct and adjoint eigenmodes, the projection of the latter onto the former, e.g.,
\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A,\dagger}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A}\right\rangle
, is thus much smaller in the motile case than in the immotile one. That is because of this negative contribution from multiplying direct and adjoint polarization fields. Thereby, enforcing
\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A,\dagger}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A}\right\rangle=1
in accordance with (
31
), produces an adjoint field of much larger amplitude in the motile case than in the immotile one.
But, according to the expressions (
90
) for the noises acting at the amplitude equation level, larger amplitudes of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A,\dagger}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B,\dagger}
must correspond to larger intensities, thus a larger
\alpha
from its definition (
97
). We insist that this result is not specific to the normalization of the adjoint eigenmodes chosen in (
31
). Choosing any another normalization would make appear the prefactors
\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A,\dagger}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A}\right\rangle^{-1}
and
\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B,\dagger}\middle|\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B}\right\rangle^{-1}
in the right-hand sides in (
90
), resulting in the same intensities.

Overall, the explanation proposed above is equivalent to noticing that the operators
\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
and
\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}
have a greater degree of non-normality, in the motile case, as compared to the immotile one. Indeed, the degree of non-normality is increased by the off-diagonal terms in
\beta
becoming nonzero (i.e., the particles becoming motile) in the operator (
29
). It is well known that a larger degree of non-normality implies poorer spatial support between direct and adjoint eigenmodes, in turn implying a greater receptivity to external forcing
(
15
;
31
)
.

For the same parameters as in figure
5
, and including two more values of
\phi
, we compare in figure
6
the WNL and DNS trajectories corresponding to
\beta=0.15
.

Figure 6
:
For
\beta=0.15
(and
D_{R}=0.02
, implying
D_{T,c}\approx 0.1333
), corresponding to motile particles, and
\varepsilon^{2}=0.005
(implying
D_{T}=D_{T,c}-\varepsilon^{2}=0.1283
).
Left column: temporal evolution of
\varepsilon|A|
over a trajectory for a realization of the stochastic forcing. The dotted line corresponds to the equilibrium value.
Right column:
\varepsilon A
shown in the complex plane, with shades of gray corresponding to values of
-\ln(P)/(2\sigma^{2})
ranging from the minimum value (lightest shade, highlighted by the dotted circle) to its opposite (darkest shade).
The fully nonlinear approach (DNS, continuous red line) and the weakly nonlinear one (dash-dotted blue line) are compared. The latter consists of marching (
89
) in time, with the same noise realization as in the DNS translated into the reduced system according to (
90
).
Three different values of the forcing intensity
\phi\in\left\{0.71,1.41,2.12\right\}
are considered (increasing from top to bottom)

In the figure, both approaches use the same noise realization and initial condition for the trajectories. From the spatio-temporal noise generated for the DNS, the components
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)
and
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)
are extracted at each time step and translated into the processes
\xi_{A}(t)
and
\xi_{B}(t)
, acting on the amplitudes, by applying (
90
). The system (
89
), rewritten over
t=\tau/\varepsilon^{2}
, e.g.
\mathrm{d}_{t}A=\varepsilon^{2}(A+\mu A|A|^{2}+\nu A|B|^{2})+\varepsilon\phi\xi_{A}(t)
, is then marched in time from the initial condition
(A(0),B(0))=H_{e}(1,1)
, which are also imposed in the DNS, and this yields the curves shown for the WNL. The DNS trajectories required several days of CPU time, whereas the WNL ones required a couple of seconds.

Overall, the two approaches agree well on the magnitude
\varepsilon|A|
, though this agreement deteriorates as the forcing amplitude
\phi
increases. That is because neglected nonlinearities in the WNL expansion are becoming increasingly important. The instantaneous error between the DNS and WNL trajectories, stemming from neglected terms in the expansion, is itself a stochastic process. Although its variance appears low, there are short episodes of time, much shorter than the autocorrelation time
\sim 1/\varepsilon^{2}
, during which the trajectories strongly depart from each other. This occurs more frequently as
\phi
increases, and one of these episodes is visible just after
t=8000
and for
\phi=1.41
. That is because, as mentioned, performing integrations by parts in the above development produces terms that can be scaled at higher orders only in the root mean square sense (Itô isometry). This, indeed, does not prevent large instantaneous errors from occurring randomly. In other words, we only expect convergence in the mean of the WNL results to DNS ones as
\phi\rightarrow 0
.

As expected, increasing
\phi
from the top to the bottom panels results in a greater variance of
\varepsilon|A|
(around its mean) and thus increasingly frequent excursions toward zero. The trajectories seem more inclined to reach values close to zero than to make an excursion of the same magnitude above the mean. This is consistent with the fact that, in figure
5
, starting from its minimum, the potential increases less sharply in the direction of decreasing values of
|A|
and
|B|
, than in the direction of increasing ones (except within a thin logarithmic boundary layer at
|A|=0
and
|B|=0
where the potential diverges).

The trajectories of
\varepsilon A
plotted in the complex plane in the right panels of figure
6
, also reveal the evolution of the phase over time. All trajectories are initiated on the real axis, since
(A(0),B(0))=H_{e}(1,1)
. For the lowest forcing considered,
\phi=0.71
, the magnitude fluctuates only slightly around its mean. Consequently, the phase solving (
100
) is close to simply the integral of white noise, i.e., a Wiener process. Indeed, the trajectory in the right panel resembles a slow random walk around the circle, with the magnitude staying close to equilibrium. For a trajectory of final time
t=10^{4}
, only about a
3/4
-circle is explored.

As the forcing is increased, however, the magnitude fluctuates significantly, and the excursions toward zero are associated with an extremely large forcing on the phase in (
100
). Accordingly, within the same time interval, the trajectories explore a much larger region of the complex plane in terms of both phase and magnitude.

We have performed the same calculation for the immotile case
\beta=0
, and also for
\varepsilon^{2}=0.005
(not shown). For a given
\phi
, the agreement between the two approaches appears significantly improved with respect to the motile case. This is presumably due to the larger degree of non-normality of the operators for the motile case. This results in (i) a larger effective forcing on the amplitudes for fixed
\phi
and (ii) a larger magnitude of the neglected higher-order terms, even though they result from the inversion of stable systems, thus making the asymptotic hierarchy less well-posed and reducing the radius of convergence of the series.

Figure
6
compares trajectories (everything else being fixed), but not the associated statistics. The system (
98
) is associated with the Fokker-Planck equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial p}{\partial\tau}=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{J},\ \ \text{with}\ \ \boldsymbol{J}(\boldsymbol{H},\tau)\coloneq-p(\boldsymbol{H},\tau)\boldsymbol{\nabla}V(\boldsymbol{H})-\frac{(\alpha\phi)^{2}}{2}\boldsymbol{\nabla}p(\boldsymbol{H},\tau),\end{split}

(101)

giving the probability density function (pdf)
p(\boldsymbol{H},\tau)
at a time
\tau
. The probability must be zero at infinity and is naturally also zero at
|A|=0
and
|B|=0
because the potential diverges there. In a statistically steady regime, the density function
p_{s}(\boldsymbol{H})\coloneq\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\infty}p(\boldsymbol{H},\tau)=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\exp\left(-2V(\boldsymbol{H})/(\alpha\phi)^{2}\right)
. The scalar
\mathcal{Z}
is a normalization factor, and the subscript
s
in
p_{s}
emphasizes that the pdf relates to a “steady” regime. Integrating
p_{s}
over one coordinate from zero to infinity gives the steady pdf for the other, such that

\begin{split}P(|A|)&=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{s}(\boldsymbol{H})\mathrm{d}|B|\\
&=\mathcal{Z}^{-1}|A|\exp\left[\frac{|A|^{4}(\nu^{2}-\mu^{2})+2|A|^{2}(-\mu+\nu)}{-2(\alpha\phi)^{2}\mu}\right]\left[1+\erf\left(\frac{\nu|A|^{2}+1}{\sqrt{-2(\alpha\phi)^{2}\mu}}\right)\right].\end{split}

(102)

Furthermore, since the potential has the symmetry
V(|A|,|B|)=V(|B|,|A|)
and the two components of the noise
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
in (
98
) follow the same distribution, it is true that
P(|B|)=P(|A|)
.

Expression (
102
) for the steady pdf of each weakly nonlinear magnitude is plotted in figure
7
. Figure
7(a)
corresponds to the immotile case, whereas figure
7(b)
to
\beta=0.15
.

(a)

\beta=0

(b)

\beta=0.15

Figure 7
:
Steady probability density functions (pdf) of
\varepsilon|A|
(also that of
\varepsilon|B|
by symmetry) for six different values of
\phi\in\left\{0.35,0.71,1.06,1.41,1.77,2.12\right\}
and
\varepsilon^{2}=0.005
.
The fully nonlinear pdf (DNS, red continuous line) is obtained by postprocessing the data shown in figure
6
for
\beta=0.15
(and for intermediate values of
\phi
), and their equivalent (not shown) for
\beta=0
; for a given
\phi
, a circle marker denotes the barycenter of the pdf, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\left[\varepsilon|A|\right]
.
The expression of the weakly nonlinear pdf (blue dashed-dotted line) is given in (
102
), and a dot marker denotes its barycenter.

In both cases, six different values of the forcing are considered. The pdfs corresponding to the DNS trajectories are also reported, obtained by postprocessing the data already presented in figures
6
and its equivalent for the immotile case (not shown).

The weakly and fully nonlinear stationary pdfs are in satisfactory overall agreement. Expression (
102
) captures well the flattening and widening of the probability distribution as the forcing amplitude increases. It also systematically predicts a correct value for (at least) the first moment of the pdf, i.e.
\mathbb{E}\left[\varepsilon|A|\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\varepsilon|B|\right]
.

At least for the parameters considered, the results presented in this section suggest that the WNL expansion accurately predicts the stationary statistics of the leading-order dynamics of the system (
36
) beyond the onset of a bifurcation point. This holds at a considerably lower numerical cost than running DNS.

In the next section, we will assess whether the amplitude equation system can also predict the statistics and paths of rare, noise-induced structural rearrangements of the system.

6.3
Stochastic results: dynamical statistics of rare events

For the immotile case and the second-largest forcing considered in figure
7
, an example of the temporal evolution of the phase
\psi_{A}
(rescaled by
2\upi
) of the amplitude
A
is shown in figure
8
a.

Figure 8
:
For
\beta=0
and
\varepsilon^{2}=0.005
(same as in figure
6
). (a) Evolution over time of the phase of
\psi_{A}
, the phase of
A
, normalized by
2\pi
and extracted from a generic DNS realization for
\phi=1.77
(black line).
A “phase-slip” event, where
\psi_{A}
suddenly varies over a time scale much shorter than that of its otherwise slow Brownian drift, is highlighted by a red dotted line and enlarged in the inset.
Colored dot markers are located at eight, uniformly spaced times
t
. See figure
9
for the corresponding vorticity fields at these times.
(b) Within the time frame of this phase-slip event, the corresponding evolutions of both
\varepsilon A
(red line) and
\varepsilon B
(blue line) are shown in the complex plane; along the trajectory of
\varepsilon A
, colored dot markers are placed at the same times as those in (a).
Shades of gray for values of
-\ln(P)/(2\sigma^{2})
(depending only on the magnitude, thus appearing axisymmetric on the plot) are also included. The latter function realizes its minimum along the dotted circle, whereas the dash-dotted one has radius
\varepsilon H_{e}
.
(c) On the same plane, the event of interest is defined as
\varepsilon|A|
and/or
\varepsilon|B|
becoming smaller than
\varepsilon H_{e}/m
. This is denoted by the black cross on the plot, where the trajectory of
\varepsilon A
(the same as in (b)) enters the red disk of radius
\varepsilon H_{e}/m
.

The data relates to a DNS conducted over a very long period of time. The phase evolution resembles a slow, random walk over long time intervals, associated with the patterns shown in figure
4
stochastically drifting through space. This phenomenon is interspersed with episodes during which the phase changes substantially over a short period of time, much shorter than the otherwise slower random walk. These abrupt variation episodes occur seemingly at random times and will be referred to as “phase-slip” events below.

One of these phase-slip events is highlighted with a red dashed line in the figure, and magnified in the inset. The corresponding evolution of
\varepsilon A
and
\varepsilon B
is displayed in the complex plane in figure
8
b (the colored dots serve as time markers). The weakly nonlinear axisymmetric log-density of the magnitude is also shown. In figure
8
b, the trajectory for
\varepsilon A
starts at some random phase but close to the equilibrium magnitude (corresponding to the minimum of the log-density in the WNL perspective). The phase-slip event then corresponds to
\varepsilon A
radially approaching the local maximum of the log-density at the origin,
|A|=0
, very closely, making about one turn and a half around it there (corresponding to a
\approx-3\upi
increment in the phase), then relaxing to the equilibrium magnitude. Thereby, during the process, the phase has varied considerably in a very short time. That is a consequence of the geometric effect already evoked. In particular, if the trajectory is close to the origin, a few time steps suffice to make a full turn around it, each turn corresponding to
\pm 2\upi
in the phase.

This is formalized by the WNL equation (
100
) for the phase. Here, the probability distribution of
\mathrm{d}\psi_{A}
, conditioned to
|A|
, follows a Gaussian with standard deviation
\sqrt{\mathrm{d}\tau}/|A|=\varepsilon\sqrt{\mathrm{d}t}/|A|
. Consequently, as
|A|\rightarrow 0
, large phase increments within a time step become increasingly likely (see figure
5
in
55
for an illustration).

We show in figure
9
velocity snapshots associated with the trajectories in figure
8
b, and thus corresponding to a phase-slip event.

Figure 9
:
Eight successive snapshots of the velocity (arrows) and vorticity (colormap) of the flow, during the phase-slip event visible in the inset of figure
8
, and at times corresponding to the eight colored dot markers. The shortest time is renamed
t_{0}
for the plot. Fields are shown over the doubly periodic square domain
(x,y)\in[0,4\upi]\times[0,4\upi]
(the pattern being
2\upi
-periodic) and reconstructed from (
48
) knowing
\varepsilon A
and
\varepsilon B
. The yellow lines are passive tracers introduced into the flow at time
t=t_{0}
for visualization purposes.

In figure
8
b, at the same time that the amplitude
\varepsilon A
is near the origin of the complex plane, the amplitude
\varepsilon B
remains around the equilibrium magnitude. Thereby,
\varepsilon B
, associated with the
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B}\exp(\text{i}y)
component, dominates over
\varepsilon A
, associated with the
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A}\exp(\text{i}x)
one. This manifests as the solution becoming uniform in the
x
-direction in figure
9
. Afterwards,
\varepsilon A
relaxes to its equilibrium magnitude and the pattern of velocity vortices reforms, with a large spatial shift in the
x
-direction (modulo
2\upi
in the snapshots), relative to the pattern at
t=t_{0}
. This is precisely because the phase of
\varepsilon A
drifted substantially in the time interval. This is analogous to a noise-induced transition, in the sense that the solution pattern rearranges itself on a much shorter time scale than it would otherwise drift randomly.

Overall, the phase-slip events in the evolution of
\psi_{A}
, visible in figure
8
a, are enabled by the magnitude
|A|
randomly getting close to zero. From figure
8
b, we also understand that these must remain rare events for the weak forcing considered, as they demand the amplitudes getting near the maximum of the log-density at the origin. A more probable way to achieve a given phase difference is to perform a circular random walk while keeping the magnitude close to its equilibrium value.

Consequently, the rare event of interest in what follows is formalized as
A
and/or
B
entering a disk of small radius
H_{e}/m
around the origin of the complex plane, with
m\gg 1
a free parameter (see figure
8
c for an illustration). As
m
increases,
|A|
and/or
|B|
approach the origin of the complex plane more closely, making large phase increments increasingly likely. The mean return time between two such events, when
|A|
and/or
|B|
decreases below the threshold value
H_{e}/m
, and as measured on the slow time scale
\tau
, is named
r(m)
. In what follows, we determine
r(m)
for increasingly large values of
m
, and compare the weakly nonlinear and fully nonlinear approaches.

For the WNL counterpart, we exploit the low-dimensionality of the system (
98
) to compute the mean return time
r(m)
using standard tools from statistical mechanics. For this purpose, the first step is to define the domains in the two-dimensional magnitude phase space,

\begin{split}\Omega_{m}\coloneq\left\{(|A|,|B|)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}:|A|>H_{e}/m,\ \text{and}\ |B|>H_{e}/m\right\},\end{split}

as well as
\mathcal{B}\doteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\setminus\Omega_{m}
. The two domains
\Omega_{m}
and
\mathcal{B}
(whose union is
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
) are separated by the boundary

\begin{split}\partial\mathcal{B}\coloneq\left\{(H_{e}/m,|B|):|B|\geq H_{e}/m\}\cup\{(|A|,H_{e}/m):|A|\geq H_{e}/m\right\}.\end{split}

(103)

We also define the boundary of
\Omega_{m}
at infinity,

\begin{split}\partial\Omega_{m}^{\infty}\coloneq\left\{(\infty,|B|):|B|\geq H_{e}/m\}\cup\{(|A|,\infty):|A|\geq H_{e}/m\right\},\end{split}

such that
\partial\Omega_{m}=\partial\Omega_{m}^{\infty}\cup\partial\mathcal{B}
. The boundary
\partial\mathcal{B}
is represented in figure
12
for
m=10
. For a trajectory initiated anywhere within
\Omega_{m}
, the rare event of interest corresponds to reaching
\mathcal{B}
, which can only happen by crossing the boundary
\partial\mathcal{B}
. From the Fokker-Planck equation, it is then possible to show that
\tau_{e}(\boldsymbol{H})
, defined as the average time for a trajectory beginning at
\boldsymbol{H}\in\Omega_{m}
to reach the boundary
\partial\mathcal{B}
, obeys

\begin{split}-1=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\tau_{e}(\boldsymbol{H})\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla}V(\boldsymbol{H})+\frac{(\alpha\phi)^{2}}{2}\Delta\tau_{e}(\boldsymbol{H}),\ \ \text{over $\Omega_{m}$},\end{split}

(104)

subject to the boundary conditions

\begin{split}\tau_{e}&=0\quad\text{for $\boldsymbol{H}\in\partial\mathcal{B}$ \ (``absorbing'' b.c.)}\\
(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\tau_{e})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}&=0\quad\text{for $\boldsymbol{H}\in\partial\Omega_{m}^{\infty}$.}\end{split}

(105)

We refer to
29
, Chapter
5.4
for a more detailed derivation. The time
\tau_{e}(\boldsymbol{H})
is a function of the starting point
\boldsymbol{H}
of the trajectories, which is unknown but whose density in a statistically steady regime,
p_{s}(\boldsymbol{H})
, was given above. Thenceforth, the mean return time
r(m)
is given by

\begin{split}r(m)=\int_{\Omega_{m}}\tau_{e}(\boldsymbol{H})p_{s}(\boldsymbol{H})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{H}.\end{split}

(106)

Solving for
\tau_{e}
for many different values of
m
and
\phi
, each time using (
106
) to extract the corresponding mean return time, yields the curves shown in figure
10
. There, the mean return time is rescaled as
r_{t}(m)\coloneq r(m)/\varepsilon^{2}
to correspond to the physical time
t
. This way, it can be compared to fully nonlinear results, all obtained over
t
. The mean return time is plotted as a function of
m
, and corresponds to the same
\varepsilon
and forcing amplitudes
\phi
considered in figure
7
.

For the fully nonlinear counterpart, the mean return times are obtained by using the AMS algorithm presented in Sec.
5.2
, and with the algorithm parameters reported in Table
3
.

. The system is discretized in time with a time step
\mathrm{d}t
, and in space over a uniform grid of
N\times N
points. The mean return time version of the algorithm is employed in Sec.

6.3
with cost function (

), whereas the mean transition time version is employed in Sec.

with cost function (

).

To sort the trajectories, the cost function is chosen as

\begin{split}\varphi(\boldsymbol{H})\coloneq 1-\log\left(|A|\frac{m}{H_{e}}\right)\log\left(|B|\frac{m}{H_{e}}\right),\ \ \text{defined for}\ \ |A|,|B|\geq\frac{H_{e}}{m},\end{split}

(107)

which reaches its maximum
(=1)
along the boundary
\partial\mathcal{B}
, and decreases monotonically as
\boldsymbol{H}
departs from the latter.

(a)

(b)

:
For
\varepsilon^{2}=0.005
and

different for each frame. Mean return time
r_{t}(m)
between two events, where an event is defined as
|A|
and/or
|B|
becoming smaller than
H_{e}/m
.
The mean return time is plotted as a function of
m
.
Different
\phi\in\left\{0.46,0.60,0.71,0.88,1.06,1.41\right\}
are considered (larger
\phi
lighter shades).
Two approaches are compared. The first uses the Adaptive Multilevel Splitting (AMS) algorithm on the fully nonlinear model (
36
), with the parameters given in Table
3
.
The second approach is weakly nonlinear (continuous line), in which the mean return times can be determined according to (
106
).

Perhaps unsurprisingly, for a given
\phi
, the mean return time in figure
10
is monotonically increasing with
m
. This is because the disk of radius
H_{e}/m
centered at the origin reduces to a single point of measure zero. By contrast, for a given
m
, the mean return time decreases monotonically with
\phi
as trajectories explore a larger region of space (visible in figure
5
).

For
\beta=0
in figure
10(a)
, and for all the
m
and
\phi
considered, the weakly and fully nonlinear approaches are in good agreement. This is particularly remarkable given that the two approaches employ distinct techniques to calculate the mean return time from their respective governing systems. Although these techniques should, in principle, yield the same results, many sources of bias could arise in the AMS algorithm
(
9
;
57
)
.

The good agreement between the two approaches suggests that the predictions of the WNL reduced system are valid not only for dynamical statistics of rare events, but also for deterministic and statistically steady quantities. This is particularly useful given that the largest return time obtained with the AMS algorithm, of
O(10^{7})
, required several days of calculations. For this reason, we did not try the algorithm in a regime where we expected an even larger return time. The WNL curves, however, required in total only a couple of minutes of computation time and could predict return times of (at least)
O(10^{13})
.

For the motile case at
\beta=0.15
in figure
10(b)
, for a given
\phi
, the mean return times are substantially smaller than for the immotile case. That is again because the noise intensity
\alpha
, acting on the magnitude, is larger in the motile case. For
m=10
, the agreement between the weakly and fully nonlinear approaches appears to degrade slightly more rapidly as
\phi
decreases than for the immotile case.

Setting
m=10
and focusing on the immotile case, we study in figure
11
the effects of varying the small parameter
\varepsilon
on the mean return times.

(a)

\varepsilon^{2}=0.0025

(b)

\varepsilon^{2}=0.005

(c)

\varepsilon^{2}=0.013

(d)

:
For
\beta=0
and different
\varepsilon
for each frames in (a)-(c) (larger
\varepsilon
lighter shades).
(a)-(c) Mean return time
r_{t}(m)
for
m=10
and as a function of
1/F^{2}
, where
F=\varepsilon^{2}\phi
is the forcing intensity.
The same two approaches as in figure
10
are compared (diamond marker for the DNS with the AMS algorithm, and a continuous line for the weakly nonlinear estimate).
Predictions made by using the AMS algorithm directly on the amplitude equations system are also included (cyan dot markers) to reveal potential biases due to the AMS itself.
The best exponential fit of the form
r_{t}\propto\exp(C/F^{2})
, consistent with the Arrhenius law, is also computed (shown with a dashed line for the DNS).
The corresponding rates
C
are reported in (d) as a function of
\varepsilon
(in blue); the results for
\beta=0.15
are also included (in red) for comparison.

The latter is plotted as a function of
1/F^{2}
, where we recall that
F=\varepsilon^{2}\phi
is the non-rescaled forcing amplitude. When plotted this way, the Arrhenius law for an equilibrium system (i.e., deriving from a potential) predicts that the mean return time grows exponentially. The same two weakly and fully nonlinear approaches as in figure
10
are shown and compared. To assess potential bias coming from the AMS algorithm, we also use the latter directly and the WNL system (
98
) (instead of solving (
104
) then (
106
)). The results are labeled WNL (AMS) in the figure.

For all values of
\varepsilon
considered, and for both approaches, the mean return time is indeed exponential in the inverse of the forcing intensity. The associated exponential rates are interpreted as the potential barrier. For the smallest
\varepsilon
considered in figures
11(a)
, there is a slight mismatch between the rates (i.e., the slopes in the figures in log-lin scale) of the WNL and DNS (with the AMS algorithm) methods. However, this mismatch is corrected by also applying the AMS to the amplitude equation system (the circle markers align well with the diamond ones). Therefore, we conclude that this slight discrepancy is due to algorithm biases and/or discretization errors.

Nonetheless, increasing
\varepsilon
eventually makes the mismatch between weakly and fully nonlinear exponential rates significant, and this is not due to numerical biases. This is perhaps better seen in figures
11(d)
, where the rates are reported as a function of
\varepsilon
. While the rates converge towards each other in the limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, indicating that the weakly nonlinear expansion is well-posed, they depart from each other above
\varepsilon\sim 10^{-1}
. In particular, the fully nonlinear rates and associated mean return times become substantially smaller than the weakly nonlinear predictions.

This overestimation can be attributed to the neglected, higher-order multiplicative noise terms
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{j}}
appearing at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
in (
49
), and detailed in Appendix
13
. For increasingly large
\varepsilon
, these multiplicative noises presumably lower the potential barrier and greatly reduce the return times.

We note that, by contrast, for
\varepsilon\sim 10^{-1}
and
\beta=0
, corresponding to
D_{T}=0.16
in figure
3(a)
, the weakly/fully nonlinear agreement for the deterministic equilibrium magnitude remains excellent. This suggests that the radius of convergence of the weakly nonlinear expansion is much smaller for predicting rare event statistics than for deterministic quantities. This confirms that neglecting the higher-order multiplicative noises is the main source of error in figure
11(d)
. The mean return time, due to its exponential dependence on the potential-barrier-to-noise ratio, amplifies enormously any small mistake made on the latter. Nonetheless,
\varepsilon\sim 10^{-1}
remains a reasonably large value, below which the weakly nonlinear expansion accurately predicts the mean return time between two rare events.

Still in figure
11(d)
, the exponential rates corresponding to
\beta=0.15
yield a lesser radius of convergence, as compared to the immotile case. Again, this is presumably due to the motile dynamics having a greater degree of non-normality and thus being more receptive to forcing.

We now turn our attention to the associated transition paths in the
(|A|,|B|)
phase space. For this purpose, we first define a disk
\mathcal{A}
of rather small radius
r_{\mathcal{A}}
centered around
(H_{e},H_{e})
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}\coloneq\{\boldsymbol{H}:d(\boldsymbol{H},\boldsymbol{H}_{e})<r_{\mathcal{A}}\}
, such that
\mathcal{A}
denotes the region of the phase space near the deterministic attractor, where trajectories spend most of their time. Therefore, we seek to characterize how, on average, trajectories go from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
. This amounts to computing the reactive probability current, denoted
\boldsymbol{J}_{R}
in what follows. For the weakly nonlinear approach, the reactive probability current can be computed directly, as shown in Appendix
11
and again made possible by (
98
) having only two degrees of freedom.

For the immotile case, and the forcing intensity corresponding to the largest return time (
\sim 10^{7})
computed by the AMS algorithm in figure
10
, we show the streamlines of the reactive probability current in figure
12
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 12
:
For
\beta=0
,
\varepsilon^{2}=0.005
,
\phi=0.71
and
m=10
.
(a) Streamlines of the reactive probability current
\boldsymbol{J}_{R}
, which characterize how, on average, trajectories go from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
.
The current
\boldsymbol{J}_{R}
results from the weakly nonlinear approach, and is computed according to (
118
).
Each streamline is colored according to its own weight (the colors are interpolated between the streamlines). This weight is computed as flux of the reactive probability current through the
1/2
-isocontour of the
q^{+}
committor function, at the point where the streamline intersects it. The darker the shade, the greater the flux of reactive trajectories passing through the region.
Thereby, along the boundary
\partial\mathcal{B}
, darker shades are naturally observed at the local minima of the weakly nonlinear potential, whose isocontours are shown as dash-dotted lines.
(b) The same streamlines as in (a) are shown, together with a density plot of the
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
reactive trajectories,
120
in total, as obtained from the fully nonlinear approach with the AMS algorithm.

These streamlines show how, on average, the reactive trajectories transition from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
. Furthermore, trajectories from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
are more likely to follow streamlines with a darker color in figure
12(a)
. For comparison, we have also included in figure
12(b)
a density plot of the reactive trajectories produced by the AMS algorithm applied to the fully nonlinear model. Both approaches seem in excellent agreement. Firstly, because the fully nonlinear reactive trajectories appear to, on average, follow the streamlines of the reactive probability current, obtained from the system of amplitude equations. Secondly, because the reactive trajectories indeed seem to concentrate where the flux carried by the streamlines is the largest.

The reactive trajectories correspond to
|A|
or
|B|
decreasing below
H_{e}/m
, while the other remains close to its equilibrium value (as was already visible in figure
8
b). Because of the
V(|A|,|B|)=V(|B|,|A|)
symmetry of the potential, the two scenarios are equally likely. In going from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, the trajectories choose a path that minimizes the potential elevation along the way. For example, if the rare event is realized by
|A|
decreasing below
H_{e}/m
, then, for each value of
|A|
along the way, the corresponding reactive trajectories concentrate around the minimum of the potential along
|B|
. In the limit
m\rightarrow\infty
, we expect the reactive trajectories to reach
\partial\mathcal{B}
through the minimum of
V(|A|,0)
, or, with the same probability, the minimum of
V(0,|B|)
, these two points indeed correspond to saddle points of (
89
).

That reactive trajectories follow the minimal-energy path on average is a well-known result for systems deriving from a potential. The main result of figure
12
is precisely to reveal that the fully nonlinear trajectories follow such a minimal energy path, even though the deterministic part of (
36
) does not derive from a potential. However, the weakly nonlinear expansion has shown that, in a certain regime, the system (
36
) can be represented in a reduced set of reaction coordinates whose dynamics do derive from a potential. Here, these reaction coordinates naturally emerge as the modulation amplitudes of the bifurcation eigenmodes.

Overall, the results presented in this section build our confidence that weakly nonlinear expansion techniques are also capable of making quantitative predictions about rare-event dynamical statistics.

7
Results in the Hopf bifurcation region

Let us now study the dynamics past the onset of the codimension-4 Hopf bifurcation. The results in the deterministic regime are first given in Sec.
7.1
, in particular, the co-existence of two stable periodic orbits. The consequent statistical results of the noise-induced transitions are presented in Sec.
7.2
. In this section, unlike in the previous one, the system of amplitude equations does not derive from a potential.

7.1
Deterministic results

The amplitude equations system (
88
) for the Hopf bifurcation involves more eigenmodes and thus more nonlinear interactions than for the pitchfork one. This time, all coefficients are generically complex-valued, and
\eta
and
\kappa
did not exist for the pitchfork case. In the deterministic regime, i.e., for
\phi=0
, the system (
88
) can be rewritten for the magnitudes of the amplitudes as

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}|A_{+}|}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=&|A_{+}|+\mu_{r}|A_{+}|^{3}+\eta_{r}|A_{+}||A_{-}|^{2}+\nu_{r}|A_{+}|||\boldsymbol{B}||^{2}+\Re(\kappa e^{\text{i}\delta})|A_{-}||B_{+}||B_{-}|,\\
\frac{\mathrm{d}|A_{-}|}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=&|A_{-}|+\mu_{r}|A_{-}|^{3}+\eta_{r}|A_{-}||A_{+}|^{2}+\nu_{r}|A_{-}|||\boldsymbol{B}||^{2}+\Re(\kappa e^{\text{i}\delta})|A_{+}||B_{+}||B_{-}|,\\
\frac{\mathrm{d}|B_{+}|}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=&|B_{+}|+\mu_{r}|B_{+}|^{3}+\eta_{r}|B_{+}||B_{-}|^{2}+\nu_{r}|B_{+}|||\boldsymbol{A}||^{2}-\Re(\kappa e^{-\text{i}\delta})|A_{+}||A_{-}||B_{-}|,\\
\frac{\mathrm{d}|B_{-}|}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=&|B_{-}|+\mu_{r}|B_{-}|^{3}+\eta_{r}|B_{-}||B_{+}|^{2}+\nu_{r}|B_{-}|||\boldsymbol{A}||^{2}-\Re(\kappa e^{-\text{i}\delta})|A_{+}||A_{-}||B_{+}|,\end{split}

(108)

where
||\boldsymbol{B}||^{2}=|B_{+}|^{2}+|B_{-}|^{2}
,
||\boldsymbol{A}||^{2}=|A_{+}|^{2}+|A_{-}|^{2}
and both
\Re(\bullet)
and the subscript “
r
” stand for the real part. Remarkably, the evolution of the magnitudes now depends upon

\begin{split}\delta\doteq-\psi_{A_{+}}+\psi_{A_{-}}+\psi_{B_{+}}-\psi_{B_{-}},\end{split}

(109)

a linear combination of the phases. The evolution equation for
\delta
is

\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}\delta}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=&(\mu_{i}+\eta_{i}-2\nu_{i})\left(-||\boldsymbol{A}||^{2}+||\boldsymbol{B}||^{2}\right)-\Im(\kappa e^{\text{i}\delta})\left(\frac{|A_{-}||B_{+}||B_{-}|}{|A_{+}|}+\frac{|A_{+}||B_{+}||B_{-}|}{|A_{-}|}\right)\\
&-\Im(\kappa e^{-\text{i}\delta})\left(\frac{|A_{+}||A_{-}||B_{-}|}{|B_{+}|}+\frac{|A_{+}||A_{-}||B_{+}|}{|B_{-}|}\right).\end{split}

(110)

which couples nonlinearly to the magnitudes. Both
\Im(\bullet)
and the subscript “
i
” denote the imaginary part. The dependence of the magnitudes on the phase combination
\delta
is a consequence of the
3
-wave interaction terms, pre-multiplied by
\kappa
or
\kappa^{*}
in (
86
) and (
87
). These terms indicate that energy can be transferred to a specific eigenmode by nonlinear interactions between the other three eigenmodes. The amount and the direction of energy transferred depend on the phase combination
\delta
.

System (
108
)-(
110
) has only two stable fixed points, or, more precisely, two discrete families of stable fixed points
2\upi
-periodic in
\delta
. Each family has
|A_{+}|=|A_{-}|=|B_{+}|=|B_{-}|=H_{e}
, where

\begin{split}H_{e}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-(\mu_{r}+2\nu_{r}+\eta_{r})+\sin(\delta_{e})\kappa_{i}}},\quad\text{and}\quad\sin(\delta_{e})=\pm 1.\end{split}

(111)

The symbol
\delta_{e}
designates the equilibrium values of
\delta
. The first (family of) stable fixed point(s), which we name “
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
”, corresponds to
\sin(\delta_{e})=1
(i.e.,
\delta_{e}=\upi/2+2\upi n
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
). The second (family of) stable fixed point(s) is denoted “
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
” and yields the opposite value,
\sin(\delta_{e})=-1
(i.e.,
\delta_{e}=-\upi/2+2\upi n
). We emphasize that, as soon as
\kappa_{i}\neq 0
, each family also yields a different value of
H_{e}
since the latter depends on
\sin(\delta_{e})
. While
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
are fixed points in the phase space of the amplitude equation system, they are periodic orbits in the phase space of the original system.

Importantly, system (
108
)-(
110
) is invariant under the involution

\begin{split}S^{\star}:(|A_{+}|,|A_{-}|,|B_{+}|,|B_{-}|,\delta)\rightarrow(|B_{+}|,|B_{-}|,|A_{+}|,|A_{-}|,\upi-\delta).\end{split}

In particular,
S^{\star}
maps any representative of the
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
family to another representative of this same family, and similarly for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Once again, the values of the weakly nonlinear coefficients
\mu
,
\nu
,
\eta
, and
\kappa
depend on the normalization choice of the eigenmodes. Furthermore, a normalized eigenmode is defined only up to a scalar prefactor, with a unit magnitude but an arbitrary phase. The coefficient
\kappa
(only) inherits from this arbitrary phase.
In other words, it is possible to make
\kappa_{i}
take any value such that
-|\kappa|\leq\kappa_{i}\leq|\kappa|
.

The condition for both
H_{e}
to exist and be finite in (
111
) regardless of the phase of
\kappa
, is that

\begin{split}\mu_{r}+2\nu_{r}+\eta_{r}<-|\kappa|.\end{split}

(112)

This condition is derived by considering the worst-case scenario in (
111
), where
\sin(\delta_{e})\kappa_{i}=-|\kappa|
. If (
112
) holds, then the bifurcation is supercritical. If it does not, then it is always possible to adjust the phase of
\kappa
such that one or both
H_{e}
cease to exist, and the bifurcation becomes subcritical.

In figure
13
, for
D_{R}=0.02
, we show the weakly nonlinear coefficients as a function of the swimming speed
\beta
(in the range corresponding to the Hopf bifurcation region, and thus the
\beta
-axes begin at the values marked by vertical lines in figure
2
).

Figure 13
:
Weakly nonlinear coefficients in the range of
\beta
for which the bifurcation is of Hopf type (i.e., for which
|\omega|\neq 0
in figure
1(b)
), and for
D_{R}=0.02
. The value of
\beta
at which the horizontal axis begins is that highlighted by a thin vertical line in figure
2
.
Note the appearance of the coefficients
\eta
and
\kappa
, undefined in the pitchfork bifurcation region.
The left frames show the real parts of
\mu
,
\nu
and
\eta
, whereas the right frames show
\mu_{r}+2\nu_{r}+\eta_{r}
together with
-|\kappa|
. According to (
112
), whenever the latter is below the former (highlighted by a grayed zone), the phase of the coefficient
\kappa
can be adjusted to make the bifurcation subcritical. Otherwise, the bifurcation is supercritical regardless of the phase of
\kappa
.

The panel on the right shows the existence of a small interval
\beta\in[0.190,0.192]
where the condition (
112
) is not met. It is highlighted in gray and begins at the threshold value between the pitchfork/Hopf region. Within this interval, the bifurcation can be rendered subcritical by adjusting the phase of
\kappa
. From the left panel, we conclude that this subcriticality is enabled by the positive contribution of the coefficient
\eta_{r}
to the sum
\mu_{r}+2\nu_{r}+\eta_{r}
over this interval. The coefficient
\eta_{r}
embeds the
2
-wave nonlinear interactions between the pair of eigenmodes with the same wavenumber, but propagating in opposite directions. Furthermore,
|\kappa|
takes particularly large values over this same interval, which also makes the condition (
112
) difficult to achieve. Beyond this interval, the bifurcation remains supercritical for all
\beta
considered.
We have found that decreasing
D_{R}
enlarges the interval of
\beta
over which the bifurcation is subcritical (not shown). For
D_{R}=10^{-4}
, this interval is
\beta\in[0.250,0.265]
.

For the rest of this section, we set
D_{R}=0.02
(as in the previous section) and
\beta=0.5
. This corresponds to the supercritical regime in figure
13
(right panel) and thus, past the onset of the bifurcation, both
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
exist, and the system is bistable. The numerical values of the weakly nonlinear coefficients are reported in Table
2
.

By deriving from (
88
) the evolution equations for the phases of the amplitudes, the WNL calculations predict
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
to oscillate in time at the frequency
\omega_{nl}
(chosen positive by convention) and such that

\begin{split}\omega_{nl}\coloneq|\omega+\underbrace{\varepsilon^{2}\left(\mu_{i}+\eta_{i}+2\nu_{i}+\sin(\delta_{e})\kappa_{r}\right)H_{e}^{2}}_{\text{leading-order nonlinear frequency correction}}|,\end{split}

(113)

where, once again,
\sin(\delta_{e})=1
for
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\sin(\delta_{e})=-1
for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
(which also change
H_{e}
), respectively. Consequently, as soon as
\kappa_{r}\neq 0
(e.g., in Table
2
), the two periodic orbits
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
have distinct frequencies, with a difference growing
\propto\varepsilon^{2}
.

Figure
14
shows velocity snapshots of the periodic orbit
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
. Snapshots are spaced uniformly in time, so that the intrinsic dynamics within an oscillation period are visible. They were obtained by simulating the system of amplitude equations (
88
) (with
\phi=0
) in the equilibrium regime corresponding to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
, and then reconstructing the first-order solution according to (
47
).

Figure 14
:
For
\beta=0.5
(motile particles, Hopf bifurcation region) and
\varepsilon^{2}=0.0025
, snapshots of the first stable periodic orbit predicted by the system of amplitude equations (
88
) and reconstructed by then evaluating (
47
). It is named “
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
” to emphasize that the pattern “reconfigures” over space within a temporal period.
The velocity field of the fluid (arrows) and corresponding vorticity (colormap) are shown over the doubly periodic square domain
(x,y)\in[0,2\upi]\times[0,2\upi]
, and the time frame
0\leq t\leq 7T(1/8+1)
, with
T=2\pi/\omega_{nl}
the (nonlinear) period of the limit cycle shown in figure
16
.
The snapshots are taken at uniformly spaced times within the time frame, chosen so that the dynamics shown are also those over a single period. Specifically, eight snapshots taken at
t\in\left\{(0,1,2,3,4,5,6,7)T/8\right\}
would yield the same figure for the Eulerian fields.
Nonetheless, choosing
7T(1/8+1)
as the final time permits larger excursions of the Lagrangian trajectories of passive tracers (yellow lines), thus revealing a possible nonlinear drift.

Passive tracers are also included and reveal some Lagrangian trajectories. In the first snapshot at
t=0
(an arbitrary reference time in the periodic cycle), the orbit
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
corresponds to macroscopic vortices. Within the next eighth period, it transitions to alternating anti-parallel jets in the vertical direction. Then, within another eighth period, it transitions back to the vortical pattern, although the latter is shifted by
\upi
in the vertical direction with respect to that at
t=0
. This process repeats in the horizontal direction within the next quarter period, then a second time in the vertical direction, but with opposite sign (compare the second snapshot to the sixth). It is then repeated a second time in the horizontal direction, but with opposite sign (compare the fourth snapshot to the eighth), which concludes a period.

This explains why we named this periodic orbit “
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
”: to emphasize that the pattern reconfigures over time. The Lagrangian trajectories, evolving over eight periods in the figure, exhibit a clear “Stokes drift” over time; it is symptomatic of both the “traveling” aspect of the pattern and the nonlinearities in the amplitude equation system.

While figure
15
is similar to figure
14
, it displays the second stable periodic orbit,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Figure 15
:
Same as in figure
14
but for the second co-existing stable periodic orbit predicted by the system of amplitude equations, named “
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
” to emphasize the “standing” nature of the pattern in space within a temporal period. The green arrows, plotted beneath the black arrows, represent the polarization vector field.

Rather than transitioning to anti-parallel jets, the macroscopic vortex structure at the reference time
t=0
is now preserved. However, the velocity field decays in amplitude until it vanishes at every point in space (at least at the leading order) after a quarter period. Following this, the velocity field regrows in amplitude until, at half period, it becomes the same as at
t=0
but with vorticity of opposite sign. In other words, within a half period, the flow has experienced a “vortex-reversal” phenomenon, reminiscent of what was observed in the bacterial suspension experiments of
50
and
54
(figure
5.20
therein). Within the next half period, the flow reverts to its original state. Strikingly, we observe in the third and seventh snapshots in figure
15
that the polar alignment of the particles reaches its maximum (significant) strength whenever the fluid velocity is uniformly zero. Conversely, the polar alignment becomes uniformly zero when the velocity field reaches its maximal strength. Overall, the velocity and polarization vectors are phase-shifted in time by a quarter period.

Still in figure
15
, the Lagrangian trajectories repeat at each period without drift. In other words, while the pattern is modulated in amplitude, it has a “standing” nature, hence the name
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
. This contrasts with
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
, and these two periodic orbits show distinct dynamics.

Deterministic predictions from the amplitude equations are now confronted with their fully nonlinear counterpart. We plot the Hopf bifurcation diagram in figure
16
, both for the magnitudes
\varepsilon|A_{+}|
(the equilibrium magnitude is the same for all amplitudes), and the periodic orbit frequencies
\omega_{nl}
.

(a)

Magnitude

(b)

Frequency

Figure 16
:
For
(D_{R},\beta)=(0.02,0.5)
, bifurcation diagram of the system (
36
) with
F=0
(deterministic regime), and by decreasing the translational diffusion parameter
D_{T}
below its critical value
D_{T,c}=0.075
. The bifurcation is of Hopf type.
The fully and weakly nonlinear approaches are compared, both presenting two co-existing stable periodic orbits named “
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
” and “
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
”.
Their dynamics over a period are distinct, as visible in figure
14
and
15
, respectively.
Both periodic orbits correspond to slightly different values of the magnitude
H_{e}
and the frequency
\omega_{nl}
, as given in (
111
) and (
113
), respectively. Yet, they are quantitatively distinguished mostly by their corresponding value of
\delta
.

In the fully nonlinear approach, several simulations are run from different initial conditions until steady states are reached. For all values of
D_{T}
reported in figure
16
, only two stable periodic orbits are found in the DNS, and the system is attracted to one of the two depending on the initial condition; this also reveals that the basins have very different sizes. A Fourier transform in space extracts the
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
components, from which we can further extract the amplitudes
A_{+},A_{-},
etc. according to (
94
). The corresponding phase combination
\delta
allows us to quantitatively distinguish between
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

The DNS and WNL approaches agree well on the magnitudes and frequencies of both periodic orbits. In particular, in the DNS in figure
16(b)
, the frequencies are indeed found to depart from one another nonlinearly. The two slopes are well predicted by the weakly nonlinear correction in (
113
).

We note that the
\varepsilon^{2}
frequency correction in (
113
) appears linear when plotted over
D_{T}
, due to the scaling
D_{T}=D_{T,c}-\varepsilon^{2}
. This implies that, for larger
D_{T}
, the seemingly quadratic behavior of the DNS frequencies can only be captured by correcting (
113
) with
O(\varepsilon^{4})
terms.

The coexistence of two stable periodic orbits, captured by the amplitude equations, makes noise-induced transitions possible by activating stochastic forcing. This is presented in the next section.

7.2
Stochastic results: dynamical statistics of rare events

Henceforth, we fix the translational diffusivity to
D_{T}=0.0725
. Since in the Hopf bifurcation region
D_{T,c}=0.075
(see figure
1(a)
), this value of
D_{T}
corresponds to
\varepsilon=\sqrt{D_{T,c}-D_{T}}=0.05
(small indeed).

To illustrate the occurrence of noise-induced transition phenomena between
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
, we simulate the amplitude equation system (
88
) for
\phi=0.35
up to a very large time
t\approx 5.5\times 10^{6}
. Running a DNS with the same parameters is now prohibitively expensive. We report the corresponding evolution of
\sin(\delta)
in figure
17
.

Figure 17
:
For
\phi=0.35
and
\varepsilon^{2}=0.0025
, a weakly nonlinear trajectory (continuous black line) is obtained by simulating (
88
) for a realization of the stochastic forcing. Shown is the evolution of
\sin(\delta)
over
t=\tau/\varepsilon^{2}
, exhibiting clear noise-induced transitions between the two attracting values
+1
(corresponding to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
) and
-1
(corresponding to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
).

Clear transitions of the trajectory between
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
are visible. These occur at random times, with potentially very long intervals between successive transitions. Strikingly, the trajectory is observed to be around
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
for substantially shorter periods of time than around
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
. In other words, the transition
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
is much less rare than the transition
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
, indicating that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
possesses the smaller basin of attraction.

In figure
18
, we study the corresponding average transition paths in the
(|A_{+}|,|B_{+}|,\delta)
-projected phase space, for the two transitions
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Figure 18
:
For
\phi=0.35
. Weakly nonlinear mean transition paths between the two stable period orbits
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
, and shown in the
(|A_{+}|,|B_{+}|,\delta)
reduced (projected) phase space with
-4.9\leq\delta\leq 4.9
(left frame); projections in the
(|B_{+}|,\delta)
(middle frame) and the
(|A_{+}|,\delta)
(right frame) are also shown.
Dot markers stand for the stable fixed points, whose
z
-coordinates are
\delta=\upi/2+2\upi n
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}
) for
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
(in red) and
\delta=-\upi/2+2\upi n
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}
) for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
(in blue).
The saddle points whose unstable manifold has dimension
1
(i.e., the “index-1” saddles) are shown as diamond markers, yellow for
\mathcal{C}
and cyan
\mathcal{C}^{\star}=S^{\star}(\mathcal{C})
(both also having replicas every
2\upi
in
\delta
).
The mean transition paths are obtained by applying the AMS algorithm to the system of amplitude equations (
88
), then averaging the resulting reactive trajectories after interpolating them onto a common arclength grid.
Small arrows indicate the direction of the transition.

These mean transition paths are obtained by applying the AMS algorithm directly to the system (
88
) for the amplitudes
A_{+}
,
A_{-}
,
B_{+}
, and
B_{-}
. The algorithm produces
N_{t}
reactive trajectories as an output, each initialized at
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
(resp.
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
) and transitioning to the neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
(resp.
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
). More precisely, the initial data for the AMS algorithm are such that the four amplitudes have their common equilibrium magnitude
H_{e}
, and three of them are assigned random phases. The initial phase of the fourth amplitude is adjusted for the phase combination
\delta
, given in (
109
), to be such that
\sin(\delta(t=0))=\pm 1
, one of the two equilibrium values; in particular, we assign
\sin(\delta(0))=1
for the trajectories to start at
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and thus for the algorithm to characterize the
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
transition, and we assign
\sin(\delta(0))=-1
for the algorithm to characterize the
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
transition. In both cases,

\displaystyle\varphi[(A_{+},A_{-},B_{+},B_{-})](t)\coloneq-\sin(\delta(t))\sin(\delta_{e})

(114)

is chosen as the cost function. This way, the trajectories systematically have
\varphi=-1
at the initial time, whether they begin in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
or
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
; further,
\varphi
increases monotonically until the trajectories reach the other orbit, corresponding to
\varphi=1
. The algorithmic parameters are given in Table
3
, though the number of trajectories is increased to
N_{t}=10^{3}
to obtain a smoother mean path in figure
18
.

In this figure, the stable fixed points
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
are found every
2\upi
in
\delta
. Importantly, the deterministic system (
108
)-(
110
) also possesses many unstable equilibria. In particular, it possesses the saddle point
\mathcal{C}
whose unstable manifold has dimension one (“index-1” saddle), and which corresponds to
|A_{+}|=|A_{-}|=H_{e,1}
,
|B_{+}|=|B_{-}|=H_{e,2}\neq H_{e,1}
and
\delta=\delta_{e,1}
(plus multiples of
2\upi
). Because the deterministic system is invariant under the involution
S^{\star}
, then
\mathcal{C}^{\star}\doteq S^{\star}(\mathcal{C})
is also an index-1 saddle point, and by construction has
|A_{+}|=|A_{-}|=H_{e,2}
,
|B_{+}|=|B_{-}|=H_{e,1}
and
\delta=\upi-\delta_{e,1}
(plus multiples of
2\upi
). We include these two (discrete
2\upi
-periodic families of) saddle points in figure
18
.

We note that, in this stochastically forced regime, the system (
108
)-(
110
) for the magnitudes and the phase combination
\delta
should incorporate terms in
\phi^{2}
, such as in (
98
), and resulting from the Itô stochastic integration of multiplicative noise. This will slightly shift the locations of the stable/saddle points. We believe this difference to be unimportant in the comments below, because
\phi
is chosen to be small.

Starting from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
at
\delta=-\upi/2
, the trajectories on average transition to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
either via an upward path to
\delta=\upi/2
, or via a downward path to
\delta=-3\upi/2
. In the first scenario, the mean transition path crosses the saddle point
\mathcal{C}
, and, in the second, it crosses
\mathcal{C}^{\star}
. We observe that these two possible transition paths are equiprobable. This reflects the fact that they are also observed to be images of each other under the involution
S^{\star}
(minus
2\upi
), a symmetry of the system.

By considering the reciprocal transition, starting from
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
at
\delta=\upi/2
, the two transition paths to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
at
\delta=-\upi/2
or
\delta=3\upi/2
also crosses the saddle points
\mathcal{C}
or
\mathcal{C}^{\star}
, respectively. These two mean paths are also found to be equally likely and to be images of each other under the involution
S^{\star}
. However, the saddle-crossing is perhaps less clear than for the
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
transition.

That the mean transition between two attractors apparently intersects the saddle point whose dimension-one unstable manifolds connect them is consistent with the Freidlin-Wentzell large-deviation theory
(
27
)
(or even the Arrhenius law for an equilibrium system). The Freidlin-Wentzell theory is valid in the limit of infinitesimally small forcing, and it is not obvious that it should apply here. Indeed, recent examples in the literature show that for small but non-infinitesimal forcing (as here), the saddle points can be bypassed by the mean path
(
58
;
2
)
. The fact that the saddle points seem slightly closer to the
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
transition path than to its reciprocal could be linked to the greater rarity of the former.

Another striking feature in figure
18
is that the mean transition path of the
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
transition is not the time-reversal of that for the
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
transition. This is particularly evident in the (
|A_{+}|
,
\delta
)-projected space (right frame), where the paths diverge significantly in the vicinity of
\mathcal{C}
. This non-reciprocity of the paths is made possible by the fact that the deterministic system (
108
)-(
110
) does not derive from a potential. The impossibility to construct a potential is due to the three-wave interaction terms in (
108
). These terms represent energy exchanges between the eigenmodes, the sign of which depends on the phases via
\delta
. For instance, when
\delta=0
, then
A_{+}
and
A_{-}
provide energy to
B_{+}
and
B_{-}
, whereas when
\delta=\upi
, the opposite occurs. In the language of statistical mechanics, the absence of a potential now makes the dynamics out of equilibrium, and it is expected that the mean paths of reciprocal transitions do not coincide.

Thus far, the results presented in figures
17
-
18
are predictions obtained from the weakly nonlinear method. We now compare these predictions with results obtained by applying the AMS algorithm to the fully nonlinear system, with the parameters provided in Table
3
. The weakly nonlinear initial conditions on the amplitudes are extended to the full-dimensional space by evaluating (
47
).

The mean transition times associated with the two noise-induced transitions,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
, are reported in figure
19
.

(a)

log-lin

(b)

zoomed, lin-lin

Figure 19
:
For
\varepsilon^{2}=0.0025
. Mean transition times
T
of the noise-induced transitions between the two stable periodic orbits
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
.
The AMS algorithm was used for both the fully and the weakly nonlinear approaches.
The two transition directions
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
(plain square for the WNL, empty square with error-bar for the DNS) and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
(plain circle for the WNL, empty circle with errorbar for the DNS) are considered.
Panel (b) proposes a zoomed view on lin-lin scale of the dashed frame in (a).

Recall that the mean transition time
T
is the average time required for a trajectory that starts in the vicinity of the first orbit to reach the vicinity of the second. For both transitions, the WNL approach predicts that the mean return times are exponential when plotted against
1/F^{2}
and in the limit of
F\rightarrow 0
. This is consistent with the large deviation theory.

The mean transition time for the
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
transition is systematically larger than for
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
. Thereby, if both periodic orbits are possible states in which to find the system, it is always more likely to find it in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
(the “standing”, vortex-reversal periodic orbit) than in
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
; furthermore, as
F\to 0
, it is more likely by an exponential factor.

The mean transition times computed from the fully nonlinear model agree very well with the WNL predictions. Each estimate of these fully nonlinear mean return times required about a week of CPU time, and we could not afford to seek times larger than
\sim 10^{5}
. Plotting the same data in a lin-lin scale in figure
19(b)
highlights slight discrepancies between the two approaches, particularly for the
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
transition and small
F
, corresponding to the largest mean transition times. It is plausible that these mismatches have a numerical/algorithmic origin and disappear as
\mathrm{d}t
decreases and/or
N_{t}
increases, but testing these hypotheses would be numerically too costly. It is also possible that these small mismatches originate from the asymptotic procedure itself, in particular from the neglected higher-order multiplicative noises derived in (
121
), similarly as in figure
11
. In the latter scenario, decreasing
\varepsilon
is the only way to improve the agreement.

We further pursue our comparison by showing in figures
20
and
21
the
80\%
-percentile probability tube of the transition paths for the
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{O}}
transitions, respectively. Some reactive trajectories are also included. The same
(|A_{+}|,|B_{+}|,\delta)
projected phase space as in figure
18
is considered. The corresponding forcing amplitude differs between each figure, as it is the one that yields the largest mean transition time reported in figure
19
for the fully nonlinear system.
