.

The probability tubes obtained from both approaches are in excellent agreement, and for both transition directions. From figure
18
we understand the probability tubes are curved because, on average, reactive trajectories cross the index-
1
saddle points between two attractors, and these saddles have
|A_{+}|\neq|B_{+}|
.

This good agreement, together with figure
19
, suggests that, at least for the
\varepsilon
considered, the system of amplitude equations can accurately reconstruct the entire flow map of the leading order nonlinear dynamics. Indeed, the reduced system not only correctly locates the stable fixed points, but also the saddle/unstable points, and how their manifolds connect to bring about noise-induced transitions.

8
Summary and perspectives

8.1
Summary

We consider the coarse-grained DSS model for a dilute suspension of swimming, rod-like particles, subject to additive white-noise forcing. The model is described by the first three orientational moments of the particle density, i.e., the particle concentration field, the polarity vector field, and the second moment tensor field. The evolution equation for each of these fields is further coupled with the Stokes equations for the fluid velocity. This coarse-grained model, based on the generalized Bingham closure, is thermodynamically consistent with respect to the DSS model
(
73
)
.

The uniform, isotropic solution is shown to become unstable by decreasing the strength of the transitional diffusivity, the bifurcation being of either pitchfork or Hopf type depending on the swimming velocity of the particles. In either way, the weakly nonlinear, post-bifurcation dynamics are spanned by a handful of eigenmodes with
||\boldsymbol{k}||=1
. Therefore, we reduce the system there to its low-dimensional slow manifold. This reduction persists in the presence of stochastic forcing, provided that the latter is sufficiently weak.

This results in a system of simple ODEs for the amplitudes of the bifurcating eigenmodes. The deterministic part of the system is derived classically by canceling secular terms
\propto t
emerging in the expansion. The stochastic part is derived analogously by canceling terms whose standard deviation grows as
\propto\sqrt{t}
. In this way, the additive and multiplicative stochastic forcing acting on the reduced system do not result from
ad hoc
considerations, but rather from a formal treatment.

The deterministic steady states predicted by the amplitude equations are in excellent quantitative agreement with those computed directly from the coarse-grained equations. That holds for both the codimension-
2
pitchfork and codimension-
4
Hopf bifurcations. While the pitchfork bifurcation is always supercritical, the Hopf bifurcation is subcritical over a particular range of particle swimming speeds.

The weakly nonlinear approach also yields the correct steady density for the magnitudes of the amplitudes over stochastically forced trajectories. In the pitchfork case, the leading-order dynamics are found to derive from a potential. This is not apparent from the original full system, but is revealed by the weakly nonlinear expansion. The fact that an original active matter system can still exhibit equilibrium dynamics, when represented in a few judicious reaction coordinates, is in line with
12
. By contrast, in the Hopf bifurcation region, no such potential can be constructed, and the weakly nonlinear dynamics are fundamentally out of equilibrium.

Having established the utility of the amplitude equations in the deterministic regime, we then use them to study rare events. In the pitchfork case, the rare phenomenon of interest is a sudden, large drift of the coherent spatial pattern in a short amount of time (“phase-slip” event). This event is enabled by the trajectory of at least one of the two amplitudes entering a small disk around the origin of the complex plane, where the potential reaches a local maximum (hence the rarity of the event). Because of the low dimensionality of the amplitude equation system, it is possible to compute the mean return time of such an event directly from the associated Fokker-Planck equation. For comparison with the fully nonlinear coarse-grained model, we use the AMS rare event algorithm. These respective approaches, applied to their respective systems, are found in excellent agreement.

Our analysis also makes clear that motile particles experience a larger effect of stochastic forcing than their immotile counterparts. That is because the linearized operator is more non-normal in the motile case, as motility activates off-diagonal terms by coupling each moment to the higher one.
This considerably reduces the mean return time for a given forcing intensity. This is in line with the conclusions of
69
, who also find that a greater degree of non-normality is associated with a much reduced transition time via a renormalization of the noise intensity.

In the case of supercritical Hopf bifurcation, the nonlinear coupling between four co-bifurcating eigenmodes results in two stable coexisting orbits. Each orbit corresponds to a distinct collective state with its own intra-period dynamics. One of the orbits exhibits a time-periodic alternation of macroscopic vortices and anti-parallel jets. The second orbit exhibits a vortex-reversal phenomenon, in which the macroscopic circulation switches periodically between clockwise and counterclockwise directions; between these two states, the system achieves a state of zero (leading-order) velocity. The presence of stochastic forcing enables noise-induced transitions between these two stable collective states, with the predominance of time spent in the second one.

In comparison with the full coarse-grained DSS model, the amplitude equations give accurate predictions regarding the mean transition times between these two states, at a considerably lower numerical cost. Furthermore, the relative simplicity of the amplitude equation description allows us to understand the geometry of the transition and the importance of saddle states.

8.2
Perspectives

We believe that our approach offers perspectives, some of which are proposed below.

In this article, we consider a two-dimensional periodic box, which quantizes the admissible wavenumber vectors. In the analogous three-dimensional setting, the Hopf bifurcation is of codimension-
6
, which requires including more waves in the weakly nonlinear expansion. Moreover, as the spatial domain becomes very large, an increasing number of eigenmodes at
||\boldsymbol{k}||=1
need to be included in the weakly nonlinear developments, all of them interacting nonlinearly, as their damping rates become asymptotically small.

Obtaining a system of amplitude equations of higher dimension could capture even richer dynamics. In particular, it could propose a theoretical framework for wave turbulence in active matter systems, which has been reported in many experimental studies
(
74
)
. A rich, hysteretic dynamics could also be found by expanding the amplitude equations to higher orders in the Hopf subcritical regime.

It is challenging to do by hand the weakly nonlinear calculations that aim to include more eigenmodes and/or higher-order terms. However, the deterministic parts of the amplitude equations can be deduced from symmetry considerations alone. It is the calculation of the coefficients pre-multiplying each monomial that made the developments above complicated. To illustrate this, let us consider the Hopf bifurcation and determine all possible nonlinear combinations of the eighth waves,
A_{+}
,
A_{-}
,
B_{+}
,
B_{-}
and complex conjugates, that feed back on the wave
A_{+}
. These combinations solve the simple system

\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}&\boldsymbol{k}_{A}&\boldsymbol{k}_{B}&\boldsymbol{k}_{B}&-\boldsymbol{k}_{A}&-\boldsymbol{k}_{A}&-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}&-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\\
\omega&-\omega&\omega&-\omega&-\omega&\omega&-\omega&\omega\\
\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{c}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\\
\omega\end{bmatrix}\end{split}

(115)

where each
c_{j}
, with
j=1,...,8
, is a positive or null integer. Each solution of the linear matrix-vector system (
115
), and there are an infinite number of them, is associated with a monomial

\prod_{j=1}^{8}\Lambda_{j}^{c_{j}}

to be included in
f_{A_{+}}
. In particular, solving under the constraint
\sum_{j}c_{j}=n
yields all the monomials to be included at order
n
. By choosing
n=4
, for instance, system (
115
) has no solutions, from which we deduce that
f_{A_{+}}
in (
86
) should not be completed with fourth-order terms. Choosing
n=5
, we found
18
solutions to (
115
), for example,
\boldsymbol{c}=(0,1,2,0,0,0,1,1)
, corresponding to
A_{-}B_{+}|B_{+}|^{2}B_{-}^{*}
.

System (
115
) can easily be generalized to include more eigenmodes, yielding the form of the amplitude equations for an arbitrary number of modes and up to an arbitrary order (although the shape of higher-order noise terms would also need to be deduced in a systematic manner). From here, the coefficients could be fitted to fully nonlinear trajectories or experimental data, for instance, using the maximum likelihood estimation technique.

Having a systematic way to construct amplitude equations from kinetic (e.g., DSS) or coarse-grained theories, and to infer the coefficients from pre-existing data, could also shed light on the collective nonlinear dynamics in confined (non-periodic) experimental geometries
(
45
;
50
;
53
)
. Among them, the dilute bacterial suspension experiments in a cylindrical cavity performed in
53
;
54
exhibit a very rich phenomenology.

Past the onset of the bifurcation threshold, the authors report the coexistence of a quasi-two-dimensional, coherent vortex state whose size is set by cylinder radius (the largest scale available), with a three-dimensional chaotic state whose characteristic size is that of the smaller, vertical confinement (see
54
, figure 5.7.b therein). Furthermore, the bacterial system exhibits sporadic transitions between these two states, after very long and possibly random times (see
54
, figures 5.17 and 5.18 therein).

It would be experimentally very challenging to produce the statistics of these large transition times, which may motivate a theoretical, reduced-order approach such as that adopted here. Indeed, it is plausible that the coexistence of these two states is captured by the coarse-grained DSS model (
36
) in a three-dimensional, confined cylindrical geometry. Encouragingly, the authors of
54
report a time-periodic, time-reversal state in figure 5.20, achieved by increasing viscosity. This experimental state is evocative of the state
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{O}}
predicted by our calculations (in dimensional units, the vortex size of the latter is also proportional to the domain size).

Identifying the handful of dominant post-bifurcation eigenmodes from the dispersion relation of the coarse-grained DSS model in a cylindrical geometry, and then applying the above symmetry-based reasoning, will result in a system of nonlinearly coupled amplitudes for these modes. The related coefficients and stochastic forcing intensity can then be inferred from experimental data. By the means advanced in this article, such a system can be used to compute statistics of rare transitions between the coherent vortex and the chaotic attracting states, and possibly many more metastable ones not observed over the short experimental time scales.

{appen}

9
Practical implementation of the linear stability problem

While solving the generalized eigenvalue problem (
28
), the trace and symmetry constraints must be enforced. This can be done using a projection operator, say
\mathsfbi{P}_{c}
. The latter is such that
\mathsfbi{P}_{c}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}
satisfies the trace and symmetry equations even if an arbitrary
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}
does not. For instance, for
d=2
, the projector
\mathsfbi{P}_{c}
reads

\displaystyle\mathsfbi{P}_{c}=\mathsfbi{I}_{10}-\boldsymbol{e}_{6}\boldsymbol{e}_{6}^{T}-\boldsymbol{e}_{7}\boldsymbol{e}_{7}^{T}+\boldsymbol{e}_{6}\boldsymbol{e}_{5}^{T}+\boldsymbol{e}_{7}(\boldsymbol{e}_{1}-\boldsymbol{e}_{4})^{T}\Rightarrow\mathsfbi{P}_{c}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}=(\hat{c},\hat{n}_{x},\hat{n}_{y},\hat{D}_{xx},\hat{D}_{xy},\hat{D}_{xy},\hat{c}-\hat{D}_{xx},\hat{u}_{x},\hat{u}_{y},\hat{\varpi})^{T},

where
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
is the
i
th basis vector. Thereupon, if
\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}
denotes an orthonormal basis matrix for the range of
\mathsfbi{P}_{c}
, the eigenvalue problem (
28
) is better written in the reduced subspace as

\displaystyle\sigma\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}^{H}\mathsfbi{H}\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}\hat{\overline{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{1}=\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}^{H}\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}\hat{\overline{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{1},

(116)

where
\hat{\overline{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{1}
is a reduced state vector free of the
(d-1)d/2+1
component corresponding to dependent fields (e.g., free of the
Q_{yx}
and
Q_{yy}
component in the example above). The eigenvector solutions of (
116
) can then be transformed back in the full-dimensional space by application of
\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}
, the trace and symmetry constraints being thus automatically satisfied.

Due to its very low dimension, the eigenproblem (
116
) can easily be solved numerically even without an explicit expression for
\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. In practice, we solve (
116
) using the
Matlab
pre-implemented function
eig
, where the operator
\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is constructed explicitly by successive application over basis vectors.

Importantly, the special case
\boldsymbol{k}=0
requires a special treatment. Indeed, the action of the linear operator in (
29
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathsfbi{L}_{\boldsymbol{0}}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{1}=\left(0,-(d-1)D_{R}\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}_{1},\operatorname{vec}\left(-2dD_{R}\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}+2D_{R}\mathsfbi{I}\hat{c}_{1}\right),\boldsymbol{0},0\right)^{T}.\end{split}

(117)

The equations for velocity and pressure are reduced to trivial equalities and can be removed. It is then easy to show that the system possesses
d
real eigenvalues equal to
\sigma=-(d-1)D_{R}
, associated with eigenvectors with nonzero component only in
\hat{\boldsymbol{n}}_{1}
. It also possesses
d^{2}
real eigenvalues equal to
\sigma=-2dD_{R}
, associated with eigenvectors nonzero only in
\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}
. Eventually, there exists a null eigenvalue
\sigma=0
whose associated eigenvector has a nonzero component in both
\hat{c}_{1}
and
\hat{\mathsfbi{D}}_{1}
. However, the latter linearly neutral eigenvector must be ignored by virtue of the conservation of the total number of particles

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}c^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=\int_{\Omega}c_{0}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}c_{1}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}+O(\varepsilon^{2})=(2\upi)^{d},\quad\forall t.

Since
\int_{\Omega}c_{0}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=(2\upi)^{d}
, collecting powers of
\varepsilon
implies that
\int_{\Omega}c_{j}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=0
for each order
j=1,2,...
. Accordingly, at each order, the concentration field associated with
\boldsymbol{k}=0
must be zero. This implies that the wavenumber vector
\boldsymbol{k}=0
is strictly stable as soon as
D_{R}>0
, since then the real parts of all physically admissible eigenvalues are strictly negative.

Let us now comment on the construction of the adjoint eigenbasis. Since the inner product of interest is the Hermitian dot product, then
\mathsfbi{L}^{\dagger}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\mathsfbi{L}^{H}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, and the adjoint eigenmodes are solutions of

\displaystyle\sigma_{j}^{*}\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}^{H}\mathsfbi{H}\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}\tilde{\overline{\boldsymbol{q}}}^{\dagger}_{j}=\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}^{H}\mathsfbi{L}^{H}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}\tilde{\overline{\boldsymbol{q}}}^{\dagger}_{j},

in the reduced (constraints-free) space. Again, multiplying a reduced adjoint eigenmode by
\mathsfbi{Z}_{c}
transforms it back into the full space while automatically enforcing the trace and symmetry constraints.

10
Showing that higher-order terms have a bounded variance

Let
\boldsymbol{h}_{1}
denote any of the temporal integrals at
O(\varepsilon)
in (
64
), then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{h}_{1}(t)&\coloneq\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{\Lambda}(s)\boldsymbol{h}(s)\mathrm{d}s=\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,0)\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{\Lambda}(s)\int_{0}^{s}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(0,x)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}s\\
&\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny i.b.p.}}}}{{=}}}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,0)\int_{0}^{t}\left(W_{\Lambda}(t)-W_{\Lambda}(s)\right)\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(0,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

where
\mathrm{d}W_{\Lambda}/\mathrm{d}t\coloneq\xi_{\Lambda}
. Applying
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(0,t)
to the equation above then differentiating with respect to
t
leads to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(0,t)\boldsymbol{h}_{1}(t)\right)=\xi_{\Lambda}(t)\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(0,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}A_{-}}\mathrm{d}s\Rightarrow\\
&\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t,0)\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(0,t)\boldsymbol{h}_{1}(t)\right)=\xi_{\Lambda}(t)\boldsymbol{h}(t)\Rightarrow(\mathsfbi{H}\mathrm{d}_{t}-\mathsfbi{L}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}})\boldsymbol{h}_{1}(t)=\xi_{\Lambda}(t)\boldsymbol{h}(t),\end{split}

where we have used (
61
). In other words,
\boldsymbol{h}_{1}(t)
is the linear response to
\xi_{\Lambda}(t)\boldsymbol{h}(t)
. Since
\boldsymbol{h}(t)
is bounded, and again by virtue of the strict stability of the linear system
2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
, we expect
\boldsymbol{h}_{1}(t)
to also have a bounded root mean square. The integral terms at
O(\varepsilon)
in (
64
) thus do not threaten the asymptotic hierarchy, at least in the root mean square sense. We postulate that all the integral terms absorbed at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
in (
64
) are also bounded.

11
Computing the reactive probability current

The first step is to solve for the committor functions
q^{+}(\boldsymbol{H})
and
q^{-}(\boldsymbol{H})
. The former,
q^{+}
, is the probability that, starting from
\boldsymbol{H}
, the trajectory reaches
\mathcal{B}
before it reaches
\mathcal{A}
; the latter,
q^{-}
, is the probability that, on the contrary, the trajectory reaches
\mathcal{A}
before it reaches
\mathcal{B}
. As can be found, for example, in
24
(equations
(18)
and
(19)
therein), among others, the committor function
q^{+}
is solution of

-\boldsymbol{\nabla}q^{+}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\nabla}V+\frac{(\alpha\phi)^{2}}{2}\Delta q^{+}=0,\ \ \text{subject to}\ \ \begin{cases}q^{+}=0&\text{on}\;\partial\mathcal{A}\\
q^{+}=1&\text{on}\;\partial\mathcal{B}.\\
\end{cases}

Since the drift term in (
98
) derives from a potential, the other committor function is immediately given by
q^{-}=1-q^{+}
.
From the knowledge of
q^{+}
,
q^{-}
, and the probability current
\boldsymbol{J}
appearing in the Fokker-Planck equation (
101
), the reactive probability current is given by

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{J}_{R}\coloneq q^{+}q^{-}\boldsymbol{J}+\frac{(\alpha\phi)^{2}}{2}p_{s}\left(q^{-}\boldsymbol{\nabla}q^{+}-q^{+}\boldsymbol{\nabla}q^{-}\right),\end{split}

(118)

which can be shown to be divergence-free. This expression can be found in
24
, equation
(26)
therein.

12
Deriving the same amplitude equations with the multiple-scale method

The same systems of amplitude equations as (
88
) and (
89
), at leading order, with the same expressions for the coefficients and noise processes, could be derived by using the multiple-scale method. This requires introducing the slow time scale
\tau=\varepsilon^{2}t
from the beginning, and making the amplitudes depend on
\tau
only, e.g.,
A_{+}=A_{+}(\tau)
. It is not necessary to postulate (
49
) from the beginning, because it arises naturally from the calculations. Then, as proposed in
48
(or in
22
, Chapter 6.2 therein), the external stochastic forcing is decomposed into the sum of two contributions, (i) and (ii), according to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=&\underbrace{\left(\zeta_{A_{+}}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}+\zeta_{A_{-}}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}\right)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}+\left(\zeta_{B_{+}}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}}+\zeta_{B_{-}}(t)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}}\right)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}+\text{c.c.}}_{\text{(i): triggers a response linearly fully contained in the slow manifold.}}\\
&+\underbrace{\left(\mathsfbi{P}_{o,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathsfbi{P}_{o,\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}+\text{c.c.}\right)+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\notin\left\{\boldsymbol{0},\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{A},\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\right\}}\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}}_{\text{(ii): triggers a response linearly fully contained in the fast manifold.}},\end{split}

(119)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\zeta_{\Lambda}(t)\doteq\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda,\dagger}\middle|\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)\right\rangle,\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{A}&\text{if}\;\Lambda\in\left\{A_{+},A_{-}\right\}\\
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{B}&\text{if}\;\Lambda\in\left\{B_{+},B_{-}\right\}\end{cases}.\end{split}

The operators
\mathsfbi{P}_{o,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
and
\mathsfbi{P}_{o,\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}
are oblique projectors onto the eigen-subspaces spanned by the strictly stable eigenmodes for
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, respectively, complement of the neutral subspaces. For a given
\boldsymbol{k}
, the full expression of the projector is given by (
75
).

Part (i) of the decomposition excites only the neutral eigenmodes, i.e., yields a response fully contained within the slow manifold. This holds only at the linear level, but only linear problems are solved in the asymptotic expansion. Crucially, since the response into the slow manifold is described as a function of
\tau
(through the amplitudes), the forcing that triggers it must be as well. This can easily be done by using the scaling invariance of white noise,
\zeta_{\Lambda}(t)=\varepsilon\zeta_{\Lambda}(\varepsilon^{2}t)
.

Part (ii) of the decomposition excites only the strictly stable eigenmodes, whether these are in the strictly stable eigen-subspace at
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
, or in the full eigenspace at all the other
\boldsymbol{k}
. In other words, it yields a response fully contained within the fast manifold. For that reason, its temporal dependency is kept as is, since, in the spirit of the multiple-scale method, “
t
” is meant to capture the fast variations. The forcing becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x},t)=&\varepsilon\left[\left(\zeta_{A_{+}}(\tau)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}+\zeta_{A_{-}}(\tau)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}\right)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}+\left(\zeta_{B_{+}}(\tau)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}}+\zeta_{B_{-}}(\tau)\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}}\right)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}+\text{c.c.}\right]\\
&+\left(\mathsfbi{P}_{o,\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}+\mathsfbi{P}_{o,\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}}(t)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\cdot\boldsymbol{x}}+\text{c.c.}\right)+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\notin\left\{\boldsymbol{0},\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{A},\pm\boldsymbol{k}_{B}\right\}}\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)e^{\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}.\end{split}

In the asymptotic expansion,
\boldsymbol{f}
is pre-multiplied by
\phi\varepsilon^{2}
and thus the slow component of the forcing acts at
O(\varepsilon^{3})
, while the fast component acts at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
. By then following the multiple-scale formalism, the same systems (
88
) and (
89
) are obtained. Since it is unnecessary to write the expansion under integral form (the integrations by parts are implied in the multiple-scale procedure), the calculations are lighter than those proposed in the main text.

13
Deriving higher-order noise corrections acting on the amplitudes

The detailed expression of the multiplicative noise processes
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}
, oscillating at
\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
, and resulting from quadratic interactions of first-order terms with the second-order stochastic responses
\phi\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{2,\boldsymbol{k}\in K}^{\phi}(t)
, is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)=&\left(A_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{1}(t)+B_{+}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{2}(t)+B_{-}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{3}(t)\right)e^{\text{i}\omega t}+\left(A_{+}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{4}(t)+B_{+}^{*}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{5}(t)+B_{-}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{6}(t)\right)e^{-\text{i}\omega t},\end{split}

with the additive stochastic processes

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{1}(t)\doteq\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}^{*}},\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)]+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{-}^{*}\phi_{\left\{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\right\}}}(t),\quad&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{2}(t)\doteq\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}},\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)]+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{+}\phi_{\left\{-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\right\}}}(t),\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{3}(t)\doteq\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}^{*}},\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)]+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{-}^{*}\phi_{\left\{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\right\}}}(t),\quad&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{4}(t)\doteq\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}^{*}},\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)]+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{+}^{*}\phi_{\left\{2\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\right\}}}(t),\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{5}(t)\doteq\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{+}^{*}},\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)]+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{+}^{*}\phi_{\left\{\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\right\}}}(t),\quad&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{6}(t)\doteq\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{B_{-}},\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(t)]+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{-}\phi_{\left\{-\boldsymbol{k}_{B}+\boldsymbol{k}_{A}\right\}}}(t),\end{cases}

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\Lambda,\phi_{\left\{\boldsymbol{k}\right\}}}
results from the moments of
\hat{\chi}^{\Lambda\phi_{\left\{\boldsymbol{k}\right\}}}\doteq\hat{\psi}^{\Lambda}\hat{\psi}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. The stochastic processes
\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\phi}_{j}(t)
are history-dependent, as they involve linear responses to the externally applied white noise via the (strictly stable) operators associated with the wavenumbers
\boldsymbol{k}\in K
; for that same reason, they are not generically white, but rather colored (low-pass filtered). Furthermore, recall that
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\boldsymbol{0}}^{\phi}(t)=\boldsymbol{0}
because the spatial mean is unforced, and thus does not contribute to the term above. From here, the term
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)
appearing in (
84
), resulting from inverting and integrating by parts, reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)=A_{-}^{*}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{\phi}(s)e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+B_{+}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}^{\phi}(s)e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+B_{-}^{*}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3}^{\phi}(s)e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&+A_{+}^{*}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4}^{\phi}(s)e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+B_{+}^{*}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{5}^{\phi}(s)e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s+B_{-}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{6}^{\phi}(s)e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s\\
&-\sum_{j=1}^{8}\Lambda_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}\xi^{(2,j)}_{A_{+}}(s)e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s-\sum_{j=1}^{8}\Lambda_{j}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}\xi^{(2,j)}_{A_{-}}(s)e^{-\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s,\end{split}

(120)

where we have postulated a polynomial expansion for the multiplicative noise corrections, according to
\xi^{(2)}_{\Lambda_{i}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)=\sum_{j=1}^{8}\Lambda_{j}\xi^{(2,j)}_{\Lambda_{i}}(t)
(other terms could have been included and then shown to be zero).

Again, because
\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)
possesses neutral eigenmodes, the temporal integrals in (
120
) diverge in the root-mean-square. For instance,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{\phi}(s)e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s=&e^{\text{i}\omega t}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{\phi}(s)\right\rangle\mathrm{d}s}_{\text{standard deviation is $\propto\sqrt{t}$}.}+e^{-\text{i}\omega t}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{\phi}(s)\right\rangle e^{2\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s}_{\text{standard deviation is $\propto\sqrt{t}$}.}\\
&+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(t,s)\mathsfbi{P}_{o}\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{\phi}(s)e^{\text{i}\omega s}\mathrm{d}s.}_{\text{in the strictly stable manifold thus remains bounded}.}\end{split}

In the equation above, the integral terms multiplying
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+}}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-}}
, respectively, each have a standard deviation going as
\propto\sqrt{t}
(even though the integrand are generally not white noises), and thus they appear at
O(\varepsilon^{2})
after a time
t\sim 1/\varepsilon^{2}
and at
O(\varepsilon)
after a time
t\sim 1/\varepsilon^{4}
. This is asymptotically inconsistent with the fact that these terms were collected at
O(\varepsilon^{3})
. Therefore, we use the freedom afforded by the
\xi^{(2,j)}
to cancel all integrand yields that cause such divergence in (
120
), which yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi^{(2)}_{A_{+}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)=&A_{-}^{*}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle+B_{+}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle+B_{-}^{*}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle\\
&+\left(A_{+}^{*}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle+B_{+}^{*}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{5}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle+B_{-}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{+},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{6}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle\right)e^{-2\text{i}\omega t},\\
\xi^{(2)}_{A_{-}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)=&\left(A_{-}^{*}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle+B_{+}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle+B_{-}^{*}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle\right)e^{2\text{i}\omega t}\\
&+A_{+}^{*}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle+B_{+}^{*}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{5}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle+B_{-}\left\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{A_{-},\dagger}\middle|\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{6}^{\phi}(t)\right\rangle.\end{split}

(121)

Furthermore, in this way, the higher-order term
\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{A}}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda},t)
is entirely contained within the strictly stable eigen-subspace.

The noise corrections
\xi^{(2)}_{B_{+}}
and
\xi^{(2)}_{B_{-}}
can be derived by proceeding similarly.

14
Full expressions of the nonlinearly-induced forcing terms at third-order

We provide below the detailed expressions of the nonlinear forcing terms, collected at
O(\varepsilon^{3})
, and involved in computing the weakly nonlinear deterministic coefficients. In the equation for
A_{+}
, we need

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,1}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},A_{+}A_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}^{*},A_{+}A_{+}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{+}A_{+}A_{+}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,2}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},A_{-}A_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},A_{+}A_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}^{*},A_{-}A_{+}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{+}A_{-}A_{-}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,3}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},B_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},A_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}^{*},B_{+}A_{+}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{+}B_{+}B_{+}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1,4}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},B_{-}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},A_{+}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-}^{*},B_{-}A_{+}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{+}B_{-}B_{-}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{1}^{(1)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},B_{+}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},A_{-}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-}^{*},B_{+}A_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{-}B_{+}B_{-}^{*}},\end{cases}

(122)

where we have defined

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{X,YZ}\coloneq\hat{\mathsfbi{N}}[\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{X},\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}^{YZ}]+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{X,YZ}.\end{split}

Note the comma separating
X
from
YZ
in the superscript of
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{X,YZ}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{X,YZ}
. While the order of
Y
and
Z
doesn’t matter, i.e.,
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{X,YZ}=\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{X,ZY}
, the comma emphasizes that
X
and
YZ
cannot be interchanged. That is,
\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{X,YZ}\neq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{YZ,X}
. We have also defined the contribution
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{X,YZ}
, arising from the moments of the quadratic contributions
\hat{\chi}^{X,YZ}
to the third-order forcing terms in density, given in (
43
), such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\chi}^{X,YZ}(\boldsymbol{p})=\hat{\psi}^{X}(\boldsymbol{p})\hat{\psi}^{YZ}(\boldsymbol{p}).\end{split}

In (
122
), there are also terms in
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{XYZ}
. The absence of a comma in the superscript implies that the order of appearance of
X
,
Y
, and
Z
there does not matter. These terms arise from the moments of the cubic contributions to the third-order forcing terms in density in (
43
), given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\chi}^{XYZ}(\boldsymbol{p})=\gamma\hat{\psi}^{X}(\boldsymbol{p})\hat{\psi}^{Y}(\boldsymbol{p})\hat{\psi}^{Z}(\boldsymbol{p}),\quad\text{with}\quad\gamma=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if}\;\text{$Y=X$ and $Z=X^{*}$, or $Y=X^{*}$ and $Z=X$}.\\
-2&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}\end{split}

Computing the coefficients involved in the equation for
A_{-}
requires

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,1}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},A_{+}A_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},A_{-}A_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}^{*},A_{+}A_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{-}A_{+}A_{+}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,2}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},A_{-}A_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}^{*},A_{-}A_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{-}A_{-}A_{-}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,3}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},B_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},A_{-}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}^{*},B_{+}A_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{-}B_{+}B_{+}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2,4}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},B_{-}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},A_{-}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-}^{*},B_{-}A_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{-}B_{-}B_{-}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{2}^{(1)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},B_{-}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},A_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}^{*},A_{+}B_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{+}B_{-}B_{+}^{*}}.\end{cases}

The coefficients in the equation for
B_{+}
are based on

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3,1}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},A_{+}A_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},B_{+}A_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}^{*},A_{+}B_{+}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{+}A_{+}A_{+}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3,2}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},A_{-}A_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},B_{+}A_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}^{*},B_{+}A_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{+}A_{-}A_{-}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3,3}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},B_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}^{*},B_{+}B_{+}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{+}B_{+}B_{+}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3,4}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},B_{-}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},B_{+}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-}^{*},B_{-}B_{+}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{+}B_{-}B_{-}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{3}^{(1)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},B_{-}A_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}^{*},A_{+}B_{-}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},A_{+}A_{-}^{*}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{+}B_{-}A_{-}^{*}}.\end{cases}

Eventually, coefficients in the equation for
B_{-}
stem from

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4,1}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},A_{+}A_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+},B_{-}A_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}^{*},A_{+}B_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{-}A_{+}A_{+}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4,2}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},A_{-}A_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},B_{-}A_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-}^{*},A_{-}B_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{-}A_{-}A_{-}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4,3}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},B_{+}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},B_{-}B_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+}^{*},B_{+}B_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{-}B_{+}B_{+}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4,4}^{(2)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-},B_{-}B_{-}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{-}^{*},B_{-}B_{-}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{B_{-}B_{-}B_{-}^{*}},\\
&\hat{\boldsymbol{g}}_{4}^{(1)}\coloneq\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{-},B_{+}A_{+}^{*}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{A_{+}^{*},A_{-}B_{+}}+\hat{\boldsymbol{d}}^{B_{+},A_{-}A_{+}^{*}}+\beta\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{A_{-}B_{+}A_{+}^{*}}.\end{cases}

15
Autocorrelation of the noise acting on the amplitudes

In this Appendix, we derive the autocorrelation law for the noise processes appearing in the amplitude equations. The first step is to compute the autocorrelation law of the
\boldsymbol{k}
-Fourier component of forcing, given by
\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)\coloneq(4\upi^{2})^{-1}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x},t)e^{-\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\mathbf{\cdot}\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}
, where we recall that
|\Omega|=4\upi^{2}
and
\boldsymbol{f}
is detailed in (
53
). This gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{k},i}(t)\hat{f}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{k},j}(t^{\prime})\right]=\frac{1}{(4\upi^{2})^{2}}\iint_{\Omega}\mathbb{E}\left[f_{i}(x,t)f_{j}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]e^{-\text{i}\boldsymbol{k}\mathbf{\cdot}(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}.\end{split}

where the scalar subscripts
i
or
j
denote the component of the forcing vector, e.g.,
f_{1}=f_{c}
,
f_{2}=f_{n_{x}}
,
f_{3}=f_{n_{y}}
, etc. It is also true that
\mathbb{E}\left[f_{i}(x,t)f_{j}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\right]=\alpha_{ij}\delta(\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime})
, where the scalar
\alpha_{ij}
correspond to the values reported in Table.
1
. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{f}_{\boldsymbol{k},i}(t)\hat{f}^{*}_{\boldsymbol{k},j}(t^{\prime})\right]=(4\upi^{2})^{-1}\alpha_{ij}\delta(t-t^{\prime})
, from which it follows from the definitions (
77
) and (
78
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{\Lambda}(t)\xi^{*}_{\Lambda}(t^{\prime})\right]=&\mathbb{E}\left[(\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda,\dagger})^{H}\mathsfbi{H}\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t)\boldsymbol{\hat{f}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(t^{\prime})^{H}\mathsfbi{H}\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\Lambda,\dagger}\right]=\delta(t-t^{\prime})\underbrace{\frac{1}{4\upi^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{5}\sum_{j=1}^{5}\alpha_{ij}(\tilde{q}_{i}^{\Lambda,\dagger})^{*}\tilde{q}_{j}^{\Lambda,\dagger}}_{=\text{intensity of $\xi_{\Lambda}(t)$}},\end{split}

where
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{A}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}_{B}
) if
\Lambda\in\left\{A,A_{+},A_{-}\right\}
(resp.
\Lambda\in\left\{B,B_{+},B_{-}\right\}
).
